*Authorised by the user 2026-09-02*, after [[id:f099379b-7f78-4c19-85be-abeea0bf3e71][f099379b]] measured that the seams are not mechanically recoverable and classification opened no escape hatch.

*The instruction, and its three parts.* Accept the absence of a drawer on the short headings — the 29 below =bin/lint-org='s ten-prose-line threshold are not a backlog item and never were. Separate plan from debrief on the remaining 64 by *reading*, using semantics, tense, voice and markup rather than a lexical marker. And where a heading is *even debatably* ambiguous, wrap the whole body and leave a =See the :PLAN: drawer for details= note in the body.

*That last clause is a deliberate, bounded exception and must be recorded as one.* It inverts the convention — it puts a debrief inside a drawer readers are told to skip — and it is accepted here only because the alternative is a fabricated seam, which is worse: a wrong cut leaves prospective text standing as the outcome and buries the debrief's opening, and nothing downstream would ever notice. **It applies to this retroactive pass only.** No new heading may use it; [[id:b75d553a-3c77-4b98-9f58-022bfa295816][b75d553a]] is what makes it unnecessary going forward.

*What "read it" actually means here*, since "do your best" needs an operational form: tense (=will= / =should= / =Approach:= versus =shipped= / =verified= / =measured ... and the result was=), voice (proposing versus reporting), and markup — this corpus marks prospective framing with =*Proposed by ...*=, =*The user's proposal*=, =*Decide whether ...*=, and retrospective with =*Shipped ...*=, =*Verified, not just implemented:*=, =*Implementation notes:*=. None of these is decisive alone, which is exactly what [[id:f099379b-7f78-4c19-85be-abeea0bf3e71][f099379b]] measured; together, on a heading being read rather than pattern-matched, they usually are.

*Scope check before starting:* 64 warned in DONE.org and 9 in TODO.org. **73 in total.** Measured rather than assumed: all nine of the TODO.org ones are =DONE= and merely unarchived, so they are the same case as the 64. An earlier draft of this plan called them *live*, which the lint's own condition rules out — it fires only on a finished keyword.

*Ordering:* this precedes [[id:d5eb32a3-fcee-4a25-b39a-fcde597f6cee][d5eb32a3]], and that is the cheap order rather than an arbitrary one. Once the plan is extracted the remaining body *is* the debrief, so a later =:DEBRIEF:= wrap needs no second seam judgement.
:END:

64 finished headings carry a mixed body that =bin/lint-org= reports and no mechanism can split automatically, since the seam is a fact about when a sentence was written. Proposed: read each one and cut it by tense, voice and markup; accept no drawer on the 29 short ones; and for any *debatably* ambiguous heading wrap the body whole with a =See the :PLAN: drawer for details= note — a bounded exception for this retroactive pass only, never for new headings.

*Complete 2026-09-02. All 73 cut; =bin/lint-org= reports zero =:PLAN:= warnings, against 73 when the pass began.* Committed in batches of three or four so any single cut is revertable on its own.

*Integrity checked rather than assumed*, since the pass touched almost every finished heading in the repo: DONE.org 213 keyworded and 213 ids, TODO.org 134 and 146, no duplicate ids in either. =org_wrap_plan= is lossless by construction — two insertions, nothing deleted or reflowed — and every call reported =Text preserved: yes=.

*Four body shapes appeared, and only one needed the exception.*

1. *The ordinary cut* (64). A clean tense flip. Usually a =*Shipped …*= paragraph, but the rule that actually held is sharper: **the debrief begins where the heading's own job produced its outcome.** [[id:ff1352b3-2732-4422-9f0d-4b3cb6fd8ff3][ff1352b3]] was a *decide* heading, so its seam is =*Decided …*=; [[id:9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75][9651d4c8]] is =CANCELLED=, so its seam is =*Cancelled … as superseded by*=; [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was an evaluation, so its seam is =*Run 2026-08-25.*=. A keyword-matching rule would have missed all three.
2. *Debrief-only* (4). [[id:49557477-e50c-496f-85df-82c65109832b][49557477]], [[id:e10071b9-88f0-4dc3-af8e-692965c65868][e10071b9]], [[id:e1284bdb-0a18-402a-81cb-1ac8d09fb8a2][e1284bdb]] and [[id:594adec6-4855-470d-8ceb-5fe29bf0c935][594adec6]] open with their outcome and never propose — =e10071b9= was filed and closed a minute apart. These take an **empty** =:PLAN:= drawer with the body untouched, which is [[id:f421c5c3-f81f-4ebf-99c9-42c53cc00558][f421c5c3]]'s case, and =org_wrap_plan= says so in its reply rather than silently wrapping nothing.
3. *Purely prospective* (1). [[id:0acc1df2-6916-4180-867a-99bfa551806b][0acc1df2]] closed with **no debrief at all** — 27 lines ending on "Manual repair until this is built". Whole body wrapped, which is the convention's ordinary completion transition rather than the exception. The missing debrief is a pre-existing gap and is now recorded on the heading.
4. *The user's exception* (2). [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] and [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]] interleave planning and execution across multiple phases — 29439196 over 70 paragraphs, 3063c3e5 across two ship events with re-scoping between them. No line is the seam. Wrapped whole with a pointer note.

*Two of the four shapes were not anticipated when this was filed*, and the distinction between 2 and 4 is the one worth keeping: **an absent prospective half is not an unrecoverable seam.** The first keeps the debrief visible; only the second buries it. Conflating them would have hidden four debriefs for no reason.

*A side effect worth recording because it was not the goal.* Total lint warnings fell from 140 to 68. Wrapping pushes many bodies under the ten-prose-line threshold, so rules unrelated to =:PLAN:= stop firing too — which is a fair measure of how much of each body was prospective.

*The classification hypothesis is now fully falsified, and the record should say so.* [[id:f099379b-7f78-4c19-85be-abeea0bf3e71][f099379b]] proposed that a debrief marker on line 1 meant a debrief-only body, offering an escape from per-heading reading. Across the whole corpus it held for 4 of 73. Reading each body was the only honest route, exactly as that heading concluded.


* REVIEW "Backlog = wrap unedited" would bury a debrief in 88 of 93 headings :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f099379b-7f78-4c19-85be-abeea0bf3e71
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 15:05]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-02 Wed 15:04]--[2026-09-02 Wed 15:07] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-02 Wed 15:04]--[2026-09-02 Wed 15:07] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-02 Wed 15:10]--[2026-09-02 Wed 15:13] =>  0:03
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-09-02 Wed 15:10] \\
  classify the debrief-first headings
- [2026-09-02 Wed 15:10]--[2026-09-02 Wed 15:13] classify the 28 debrief-first bodies
- State "REVIEW"     from "DOING"      [2026-09-02 Wed 15:13] \\
  classified; three routes await your decision
:END:

*Found 2026-09-02 by starting the sweep the user asked for and measuring before writing.* The sweep was not run; nothing has been modified.

*CLAUDE.md says* "backlog = wrap unedited; opportunistic = split when you are already reading the heading anyway", and that rule is quoted from [[id:cbe282ec-10c3-4aa0-8d3a-f30e17a12fa8][cbe282ec]], which chose it "to keep 30 headings cheap". **Its premise no longer holds.** Wrapping unedited means =org_wrap_plan= with no =until=, which wraps the *whole* body — correct only where the body is purely prospective. Measured against DONE.org's 93 unwrapped post-convention headings:

| body shape | n |
|---|---|
| debrief marker on the *first* line | 28 |
| debrief marker *later* in the body | 60 |
| no marker at all | 5 |

So a blind sweep would wrap a debrief into a =:PLAN:= drawer in **88 of 93** headings — the exact inversion =until= exists to prevent, landing in a drawer readers are instructed to skip. Invisible by design, which is the hazard class CLAUDE.md already names about condensing.

*And the seam is not lexically findable, which kills the obvious mechanical fix.* The natural idea is =until== the first line matching a debrief word. Sampled four of the 60, and in **all four** the first match is in the *prospective* half:

- [[id:8c662dfb-b4ab-4f4d-a5d4-8a4beda996a8][8c662dfb]], line 1 — "Measured 2026-08-28 across TODO.org: 90 live keyworded headings hold 4,027 body lines" — that is the *case for doing the work*.
- [[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]], line 6 — "Measured across the five documents", a prospective survey.
- [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]], line 3 — "its measured finding should transfer", reasoning about a neighbour heading.

Cutting there is *worse than either extreme*: it buries the debrief's opening and leaves prospective text standing as the body.

*Why no wording can work.* The seam is not a property of the prose. It is a claim about *when a sentence was written relative to the work* — and the same sentence is prospective when it motivates the work and retrospective when it reports the outcome. "Measured 2026-08-28: 39 of 97 headings carry no =CLOSED:=" is both, in different headings.

*Where the information actually lives.* At exactly one moment — the session appending the debrief knows the first line it appended — which is what [[id:79a3d89e-77be-4678-919e-637a65f87204][79a3d89e]] now exploits for *new* headings. For the backlog that moment is past, and the body is a flat sequence of paragraphs carrying no provenance.

*But it is not lost, and there is a precedent in this repo.* Git holds it: the commit that appended the debrief shows exactly which lines arrived after the heading reached DONE, and the seam is the first line of that addition. That is the same shape as [[id:b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a][b7b46a26]], which recovered =CLOSED:= times from =git log --reverse -S= for headings with no in-file evidence. Feasible, and non-trivial: bodies were also *edited* after the fact, so the last addition is not always the debrief.

*Two populations to separate before any of that.* The 28 whose marker is on line 1 may be **debrief-only** — no prospective half at all — in which case they need no drawer and the lint is over-reporting them, which is [[id:f421c5c3-f81f-4ebf-99c9-42c53cc00558][f421c5c3]]'s finding from the other side. Classifying those first would shrink the real problem from 93 to something smaller before any git archaeology begins.

*Not proposing a decision here.* The three routes are: a human reads each body; reconstruct seams from git; or accept the backlog stays unwrapped and narrow the lint so it stops reporting what nobody intends to fix.

*Classified the 28 on 2026-09-02, and the hypothesis they were debrief-only is **wrong** for the part that matters.*

*First, the split that does help.* Of the 28, **16 carry fewer than ten prose lines**, so =bin/lint-org= never reports them — its =--lint-substantial-body-p= threshold. They are not a backlog item and never were; a =:PLAN:= drawer on a four-line body is ceremony rather than structure. That leaves **12** actually warned.

*All twelve were read rather than inferred, and ten have a genuine prospective half.* The line-1 marker turns out to be the *case for the work* almost every time, not a debrief:

- [[id:ff1352b3-2732-4422-9f0d-4b3cb6fd8ff3][ff1352b3]] is the clearest refutation — "the only real question is the surface, and the arguments genuinely cut both ways", "A third option worth weighing", and *"Do not build it before deciding"*. That is nothing but prospective deliberation.
- [[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]] states the defect and then "Approach: anchor the fixture to a fixed date rather than to now" — an explicit plan.
- [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]] and [[id:3964c575-50bc-4cc9-abeb-bdd1c0162c2c][3964c575]] both open "Proposed by the user", which is the prospective half by definition.
- [[id:2e660571-26df-41e4-bb64-b9d74a3d7efe][2e660571]], [[id:95c27fca-136c-46f6-b454-fe94261e0e5d][95c27fca]], [[id:7ae6562d-25c8-453e-b78b-920e036c5665][7ae6562d]], [[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]], [[id:05c71d99-2839-447b-a536-60eb4bf43b73][05c71d99]] and [[id:355fa608-e311-4874-b2d3-bbc62172d09a][355fa608]] all open with a measurement *motivating* the work.

*Two are genuinely ambiguous* and are the only candidates for "no drawer owed": [[id:49557477-e50c-496f-85df-82c65109832b][49557477]] and [[id:e1284bdb-0a18-402a-81cb-1ac8d09fb8a2][e1284bdb]], both of which open with a shipped-line and continue into retrospective *analysis* rather than design. Whether after-the-fact rationale counts as prospective is a judgement nobody has made, and it is the same question [[id:f421c5c3-f81f-4ebf-99c9-42c53cc00558][f421c5c3]] answered for slices.

*So the classification does not open an escape hatch, and it is worth saying that plainly.* It was proposed here on the guess that a line-1 marker meant a debrief-only body. Measured, that guess holds for at most 2 of 12. The lexical marker is no more reliable at the *start* of a body than it was in the middle — which is the same finding as above, arriving from the other direction.

*The one durable number to take away:* the actionable backlog is **64**, not 93 — the lint's own count for DONE.org. The other 29 sit below the threshold and will never be reported, so any route only has to answer for 64.


* TODO A boolean argument passed as false arrives as :false, which elisp reads as true :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a8ccd6ac-3452-4926-b176-ff5951dcd5b2
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:50]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Found 2026-09-02 while fixing [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]], noticed in passing and measured rather than left as a suspicion.*

The MCP server parses a request body with =(json-parse-string body :object-type 'alist)=, whose default =:false-object= is the keyword =:false=. So a JSON =false= arrives in elisp as the symbol =:false=, and *every non-nil value is true in elisp*. Measured:

: (json-parse-string "{\"append\":false}" :object-type 'alist)
: => ((append . :false))       ; and (if :false ...) takes the true branch

**The caller gets the opposite of what they asked for**, and only when they are explicit. Omitting the argument yields =nil= and behaves correctly, so the bug is invisible to every call that leaves the default alone — which is why it has never been seen.

Two live arguments, and the second is destructive:

- =org_set_property='s =append=. Passing =false= unions with the existing =:BLOCKER:= set instead of replacing it. Wrong, recoverable.
- =org_amend='s =replace=. Passing =false= **replaces the heading's entire prose** instead of appending to it. That tool's own description says "Destructive and irreversible except through git", and this reaches it by asking for the *safe* behaviour.

*Not hypothetical about which tools are exposed:* those are the only two boolean =:type=s in the surface today, and both were touched on 2026-09-02.

*The fix is one binding, not a per-tool audit.* Parsing with =:false-object nil= makes JSON =false= arrive as =nil= and read correctly everywhere at once. The alternative — teaching each handler to recognise =:false= — is the shape this repo keeps rejecting: a rule applied per site that a later site will forget. Note the change belongs *upstream* in =claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server.el= beside [[id:ce012ad4-87f7-435c-a2ee-25210c071865][ce012ad4]], since the parse is theirs; a local override would have to shadow their function.

*Same family as [[id:6495c8a0-d971-4c77-8b30-9fde1bbf5f26][6495c8a0]]* — a tool accepting an argument and not honouring it — but with the failure inverted. There the value is silently discarded; here it is silently *negated*, which is worse, because discarding leaves the default in place while negating actively performs the operation the caller declined.


* TODO claude-code-ide-org-capture reports a queued capture it never persists :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8326a46f-e3f9-4ecc-8bc2-05d54907987b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:09]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Split out of [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]] on 2026-09-02*, which called this "the dangerous one" and "the last matters most". Its body holds the original observation; only the residue is restated here.

Called directly over =emacsclient=, outside the MCP wrapper, =claude-code-ide-org-capture= returned ="Queued capture: … (ID: …) pending review"= and wrote *nothing* — not to the session queue file, whose mtime never moved, not elsewhere under =~/.claude/org-updates/=, and not to any org file. The ids it reported existed nowhere.

*The presumed mechanism, still unverified:* the queue path needs a session context the MCP wrapper supplies and a bare =emacsclient= call lacks. If that is right the failure is silent by construction — the caller is told the work is recorded, which is the worst available outcome and strictly worse than an error.

*The fix is a refusal, not a repair.* A capture that cannot determine where to queue must say so loudly rather than report success. Whether the =emacsclient= path should additionally be *made* to queue is a separate and smaller question; a tool that cannot persist must not claim it did, however it was invoked.

*Why this outlived its parent's fix.* [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]]'s hang is now diagnosed and fixed — three tools declared a semantically optional argument required, and the resulting =json-rpc-error= escaped every handler. That fix removes the *reason* to reach for the =emacsclient= fallback, which is exactly why this one must not be quietly dropped with it: the trap is now rarer and no less silent, and rarity is what keeps a false success unnoticed.


* TODO org_capture accepts a note argument it never records :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6495c8a0-d971-4c77-8b30-9fde1bbf5f26
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:09]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Split out of [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]] on 2026-09-02*, which found and fixed the hang but left this untouched. It was the second of that heading's three named fixes.

=(cl-defun claude-code-ide-org-capture (title &optional target tags note initial-state))= names =note= in its argument list and references it nowhere in the body — re-confirmed 2026-09-02, still true. The tool description promises more than the arglist does: it says the value is "recorded on the queued event when the write defers", so a caller who supplies one has been told it is kept.

This is [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]]'s "accepts arguments it does not have, and says nothing" in this project's own implementation, which is what makes it worth fixing rather than documenting away.

*Two ways to close it, and the choice is the work.* Either record =note= on the deferred-capture event as the description already claims, or drop the parameter and the sentence together. What must not survive is the present state, where the schema advertises a parameter that is silently discarded.

Note the near neighbour that is now *not* a defect: =org_capture= also once dropped a *body*, and three headings arrived empty because of it. That is the same shape but a different argument, and it is not what this heading is about.


* TODO claude-code-ide-org-review should return point and windows to where I entered from :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6c64cc9c-33a9-4a20-af19-c188a690c755
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 09:09]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

*Asked by the user 2026-09-02:* =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= should
return them to the point they were at when they entered it.

*Locatable, checked the same day.*
=claude-code-ide-org-review= ends in =(pop-to-buffer buffer)= and saves
no window configuration and no point marker, so nothing exists to
restore from. Two things then move the user:

- =pop-to-buffer= itself rearranges windows, and burying the review
  buffer afterwards does not put back what was displaced.
- =claude-code-ide-org-review-goto= jumps to a heading in the org file,
  so point in TODO.org is left wherever the last =RET= landed rather
  than where the human was reading.

*Note the exit is not only =q=.* Burying the review buffer is what runs
the ceremony — drawer consolidation, heading separation, archiving,
sorting — so the restore has to happen *after* four steps that write to
the very buffer the saved point lives in. A raw marker into TODO.org can
therefore be invalidated by the ceremony's own edits: archiving removes
subtrees and sorting reorders every top-level heading, which is
precisely the case a naive =point-marker= gets wrong.

*So the interesting decision is what "where I was" means* once the
ceremony has rewritten the file underneath it. Candidates, none chosen:

- *Window configuration only* — =current-window-configuration= before
  =pop-to-buffer=, restored on quit. Cheap and correct for the layout
  half; says nothing about point moving inside TODO.org.
- *Restore by =:ID:= rather than by position* — record the heading point
  was inside on entry and navigate back to it by id. Survives sorting
  and archiving by construction, which a marker does not. Degrades
  honestly when the heading was archived: it moved to DONE.org and can
  be followed there, or the restore can decline and say so.
- *Both,* which is probably the answer: layout from the window
  configuration, position from the id.

*Adjacent, and worth not conflating:*
[[id:29734f79-e698-43dd-abf8-14bc86b8b0ae][29734f79]] made the ceremony
report its own completion on bury. This heading is about what the human
sees *after* that report — the same moment, a different property of it.

*Done when* entering and leaving the review command is position-neutral:
same window layout, and point in TODO.org where it started, or an
explicit message saying why it could not be.


* TODO Classify gaps by markers at either end, not by tracking an AFK state :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       409a69ff-d57e-4c1c-ab6c-28f7e85fa391
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 14:33]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Split out of [[id:e75d660f-5c0d-4707-a645-b5dea7d7ebc0][e75d660f]] 2026-09-01 at the user's request, because it is a mechanism rather than the question of whether markers are worth capturing at all.

*The refinement: classify each **gap**, using markers at either end, instead of tracking an AFK **state** forward from departure markers.*

*Why the state model fails, and it failed twice before this was found.* An opener can be ambiguous. "afk since 13:30" reads equally as "I was away and am back" and as "I forgot to say AFK and still am" — the user made exactly that objection, and the data agreed: that prompt was followed by a *77-minute* gap. A state machine keyed on openers has to resolve that ambiguity to proceed, and cannot.

*Classifying gaps sidesteps it entirely, because the closing prompt is also evidence.* That 77-minute gap is labelled by its *closer* — an explicit "BAK" — with no need to decide what its opener meant. A gap is an absence when its opening prompt carries a departure marker *or* its closing prompt carries a return marker. An ambiguous opener becomes harmless rather than blocking.

*A prompt is defined by the queue, not the transcript.* Stop-hook feedback is injected into the transcript in the *user role*, so a naive scan counts it as a prompt: 45 raw =type:"user"= entries against *36* real prompts this session, and an earlier measurement here was computed over the contaminated set. The queue's =resume= events fire only on =UserPromptSubmit=, so they are the definition; the transcript supplies the text at those timestamps. This is [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]'s recorded trap — /"276 =type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results"/ — met again from a different direction.

*Marker detection is sentence-bounded, not substring.* =afk= must stand as its own sentence, optionally preceded by =still=. A substring test matches any conversation *about* the markers, which is the mention-versus-subject trap that also governs the footnote modes ([[id:2a6a1355-615f-4c2c-a8af-c9ae8dcda09b][2a6a1355]]) and the weighted-attribution candidate set ([[id:295cde3a-49a5-4960-a828-a4fc175d73c1][295cde3a]]) — three appearances in one day, which is what makes it worth naming rather than fixing three times.

*Coverage is partial and that is the design, not a shortfall.* Of this session's seven longest inter-prompt gaps, markers label three — one by its opener, one by its closer, one by its opener — and four carry nothing, including one of 776 minutes. So markers *override* the threshold where present and the threshold remains the fallback. Aiming for complete coverage would mean annotating every gap, which is the clerical burden this line of work exists to remove.

*One consequence for whatever reports this:* a labelled gap and an inferred one must not be presented as the same thing. The honest output says which gaps carry human testimony and which rest on the 1200s heuristic, rather than one number covering both.

*Deferred, and named so it is not mistaken for an oversight:* the *retrospective correction* channel — a prompt that labels a gap already past, like "afk since 13:30" does. The gap rule above does not need it, but it is a richer signal than a binary marker and would let a human correct the record in passing.

*The user characterised their own AFK discipline 2026-09-04, and it undercuts the marker more than
anything measured so far:* "interpretation of =AFK= relies on my discipline, which I'm sure has been
inconsistent. Often I will pop in, make a quick judgment or queue a prompt during a long-running turn,
and then pop out again without mentioning 'still AFK'."

**That is fatal to the state-tracking reading and awkward for this heading's own one.** Tracking an
AFK *state* fails because nothing clears it. But classifying by *markers at either end* assumes the
markers bound the absence, and the behaviour described puts a marker in the *middle* of one — an
interjection proving a moment of attention without ending the absence around it. So a gap opened by
=AFK= and closed by a substantive prompt may be absence, attention, or absence-attention-absence, and
nothing distinguishes the third.

*Observed the same day, in the session that recorded this.* The span
=[2026-09-03 Thu 15:05]--[15:17]= opens eleven minutes after an =AFK= interjection and closes with a
prompt arguing against a decision made in the preceding turn — unwritable without having read it.
Both halves are true of one twelve-minute interval.

**The user's conclusion, and it is the right one: "formulating a deterministic heuristic is a fool's
errand."** This heading should not be read as promising one. What survives is weaker and still worth
having: markers *raise or lower confidence* on a span a human is going to judge anyway, which is
=5a6d2c75='s frame rather than an automatic classifier.



* TODO Attribute a turn to several tasks by weight, judged from the transcript with lag :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       295cde3a-49a5-4960-a828-a4fc175d73c1
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 13:27]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:

*Proposed by the user 2026-09-01:* enrich the guideposts with task ids drawn from the prompt and response payloads, multi-valued; let a subagent do it *with lag* so it can see several turns at once; and have it emit a *weight* per id per turn rather than chopping the interval into smaller intervals — "a sort of window function", explicitly not required to be exact or deterministic.

*Possible: yes, and the enabling facts are already measured rather than assumed.*

- The join is exact. Every user message in =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= carries an ISO timestamp, and *28 of 28* of this session's =resume= events match one within two seconds ([[id:e75d660f-5c0d-4707-a645-b5dea7d7ebc0][e75d660f]]). So turn text is addressable from the queue with no hook change and no new field.
- The signal is dense. Of 38 turns in this session's transcript, *32 (84%) name at least one known heading id*, mean *6.2* ids per turn, with a genuinely multi-valued distribution — many turns name three to eight.
- The transport is settled and needs no protocol work: =claude -p= asynchronously, per [[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]]'s research. Not MCP sampling, because there is frequently no live session at review time.

*And the naive version is measurably wrong in a way that argues **for** the subagent rather than against it.* The heaviest turns name 36, 21 and 18 ids — those are footnote-heavy *responses*, where an id is cited rather than worked on. *Mentions are not subjects.* A regex cannot tell them apart; distinguishing them and apportioning between the two or three tasks a turn actually advanced is exactly the judgement the proposal asks a model to make. The failure mode of the naive extractor is the strongest evidence the judged version is the right shape.

*Helpful: yes, and for a sharper reason than "more data".* Attribution today guesses from *temporal adjacency* — =--review-suggest-heading= offers the nearest preceding =DOING=. Measured on real use ([[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]]): 18 of 23 spans, while latching onto a =CANCELLED= heading for eight hours and offering one heading for a 45-minute span that actually covered five. *Content is a far stronger signal than adjacency*, and it is the signal that has never been read.

*Weights are the right output shape, and this is the part worth defending.* [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] records the user "often alternating between tasks and meta-tasks inside one session" — so a turn genuinely *is* split, and cutting it into sub-intervals invents boundaries that never existed. A weight admits what it is; a timestamp range does not.

*Three hard problems, none fatal but none solved:*

1. *Org cannot represent a weighted interval.* A =CLOCK:= line is an interval, and a clocktable sums intervals. Weights have no home in that model, so this cannot feed =org_clock_report= without inventing a parallel store.
2. *Anchoring* — [[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]]'s central objection, and weights may be *worse* than prose for it: =0.35= reads as computed in a way "probably mostly this one" does not.
3. *Non-determinism in a durable record.* Acceptable in a suggestion, corrosive in a record: a figure that changes when recomputed cannot be reasoned about later.

*All three dissolve under one reframe, which is the version worth building.* Do not let this touch the clock. Emit the weighted table as *evidence for the attention pass* — phase 2, where [[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]] says the human constructs intervals manually from evidence, and where [[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] was already re-pointed. Then weights inform a judgement instead of impersonating a measurement, org's model is untouched, and re-running the subagent produces a *new opinion* rather than a mutated record.

*Open, and genuinely undecided:* the window's width and cadence; whether weights are ever aggregated across turns or only shown per turn; and whether the output is stored at all or recomputed on demand. Storing it makes it a record with all of problem 3; recomputing it makes it slow and inconsistent between runs. That trade is the substance of this heading.

*The user proposed a representation 2026-09-01, and it dissolves the objection this body called the biggest one:*

: CLOCK: [12:00]--[12:03] =>  0:03
: - [12:00]--[12:12] * 0.25

The CLOCK line carries the weighted *share* as a literal interval org can sum; the annotation preserves the true window and the weight it was derived from. 12 minutes × 0.25 = 3.

*Retraction: "org cannot represent a weighted interval" was wrong, and wrong in an interesting way.* It assumed a CLOCK line is a record of observation, so that writing a 3-minute line for a 12-minute window would be fabrication. *The CLOCK lines this project writes are already derived, not observed.* Apply writes one line per =resume= -> =pause= run rather than the span it came from; =--apply-idle-floor= merges across gaps, so a line already covers time when nothing ran; and =--recompute-logbook-text='s own docstring says *"endpoints are rewritten, not just the =>= total"*. The proposal therefore changes *which* derivation produces the endpoints, not whether they are derived. That is a much smaller step than it first appeared.

*It also preserves an invariant it could easily have broken, which is worth noting because it was not obvious.* =--consolidate-logbook-text= sorts every drawer entry by its first timestamp, and a CLOCK line and its annotation stay adjacent *only because they share one*. The proposed pair both begin =[12:00]=, so consolidation keeps them together. The format is compatible as written.

*Two real obstacles remain, and both are mechanical rather than conceptual.*

1. *=--recompute-logbook-text= would destroy these lines.* It replaces each closed CLOCK line at or after =SINCE= with lines derived from *guideposts*, and a weighted share is not derivable from guideposts — so the next recompute silently reverts every weighted line to a busy-interval reconstruction. This needs an explicit opt-out the recompute honours, and the annotation is the obvious carrier.
2. *Weighted lines overlap by design.* One window attributed 0.25/0.35/0.40 across three headings writes three lines covering the same wall clock. Under weights that is *correct* — the shares sum to 1 and nothing is double-counted — but it collides with machinery that assumes intervals do not overlap, which is [[id:2d100d0d-2d04-43b9-af1e-f4d6b52bbada][2d100d0d]] exactly. Laying the shares end to end instead (12:00-12:03, 12:03-12:09, 12:09-12:12) avoids the collision at the cost of boundaries that are pure fiction, and reintroduces the chopping the weights existed to avoid — as a rendering rather than a claim.

*So the open question moves.* It is no longer "can org hold this" but "which of overlap or fictional boundaries is the lesser evil", and whether the recompute can be taught to leave a marked line alone. Both are answerable; neither is answered here.

*The non-determinism question, checked against the API reference rather than answered from memory 2026-09-01.* The user proposed that model choice is the main source — same telemetry, same prompt, same response, same weight otherwise.

*It is not, and the actual situation is less controllable than that.* On the current models — Fable 5, Opus 5, Opus 4.8/4.7, Sonnet 5 — the sampling parameters =temperature=, =top_p= and =top_k= are *removed and rejected with a 400*. They remain settable only on Opus 4.6 / Sonnet 4.6 and older. So on anything current there is *no knob at all* for pinning output, and no =seed= parameter appears anywhere in the API surface. Identical input does not imply identical output, and cannot be made to.

*Two further sources the premise does not cover:*

- *Adaptive thinking is on by default*, so the model decides per call how much to reason. That is variable by design, before any sampling enters.
- *=output_config.effort=* changes depth and token spend, so a weight produced at =low= is not the same experiment as one at =high=. It is a knob, but a knob that must be *recorded* rather than assumed.
- Model ids like =claude-opus-5= are *aliases without date suffixes*, so "the same model" is not a fixed artefact over months.

*So determinism is unavailable, and chasing it is the wrong goal — which the user already allowed for ("doesn't have to be exact or deterministic").* What makes a non-deterministic figure defensible in a durable record is not reproducibility but *provenance*: record the model id, the effort, the window definition and the time, so a later reader knows the weight is one opinion from one configuration and can produce a *comparable* one rather than an identical one. That is exactly the move =:CLOSED_SOURCE: git= made for [[id:b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a][b7b46a26]] — do not pretend a derived value is measured, mark how it was derived.

*One design refinement follows, and it usefully shrinks the problem.* The *candidate set* need not be model-judged at all: extracting the ids a turn mentions is a regex plus a corpus lookup, and is exact. Only the *weighting over that fixed set* needs judgement. Splitting the pipeline that way bounds the non-determinism to the numbers rather than the membership — so two runs disagree about proportions, never about which tasks were in play, which is the half a reader is most likely to check.

*Decided by the user 2026-09-01: shares laid end to end, not overlapping.* So a 12-minute window split 0.25/0.35/0.40 writes three consecutive CLOCK lines of 3, 4 and 5 minutes, each annotated with the true window and its weight. Boundaries are fiction; durations are the claim.

*Five specific things "careful handling" means, so they are not rediscovered:*

1. *Rounding must conserve the window.* 0.25/0.35/0.40 of 12 minutes is 3 / 4.2 / 4.8, and org writes whole minutes — so naive rounding gives 3+4+5=12 by luck and 2+4+5=11 or 3+4+6=13 just as easily. Largest-remainder apportionment, asserted to sum to the window. This project has already lost an interval to rounding once: [[id:b74e0f19-5a26-4c83-9d70-8e1c5a2f6b04][b74e0f19]], which =--apply-idle-floor='s docstring is careful to say "is not coming back through here."
2. *There is a resolution floor, and it bites hardest exactly where splitting is most wanted.* A share is only representable if weight × window ≥ 1 minute. On a 12-minute window that excludes weights below ~0.08; on a *3-minute* window nothing below 0.33 survives, so a three-way split is already at the limit and a four-way split cannot be written at all. Short windows — which are the common case, median run 353s — can carry two shares, maybe three. The representation is coarser than the judgement it is recording.
3. *The order of shares inside the window is arbitrary and will be read as meaningful.* Someone will infer that the first share happened first. It needs a stated rule — weight descending is the obvious one — and the annotation has to say the order is not chronological.
4. *A window is either weighted or clocked, never both.* End-to-end shares do not overlap each other, but they do occupy time a conventional span would also claim; attributing the same window twice double-counts. Whatever writes these must consume the window.
5. *A zero or sub-minute share must write nothing rather than a =0:00= line.* =--apply-idle-floor= already drops intervals that render as zero, for the reason its docstring gives — "an interval that was never observed" — and a weighted share of zero is the same thing arriving by a different route.

*Note that 2 is the one that constrains the design rather than the implementation.* The others are care; that one says the mechanism cannot express fine-grained splits on short turns, which is precisely where a turn is most likely to touch several tasks. Worth knowing before building, because it may argue for aggregating a *window of turns* before apportioning — which is what the original proposal's "lag" was for.

*A consequence of putting the weight in the annotation, found 2026-09-01 while checking who consumes each :LOGBOOK: line shape.*

*Org ignores the annotation entirely* — to org, =- [start]--[end] note= is a plain list item inside a drawer. Only =CLOCK:= lines are summed. That is exactly why this representation works: the annotation is a free provenance channel that no org tooling interprets.

*But it is not free of consumers here, and one of them is load-bearing.* =claude-code-ide-org--drawer-contains-line-p= supports *content-based idempotency* — =--queue-file-drained-p= and reprocessing both rely on it — and its docstring states the invariant plainly: /"an annotation line is a pure function of its span and label, so an identical one is a replay rather than a second thing that happened."/

*A model-judged weight breaks that invariant.* It is not a pure function of span and label; it is an opinion that varies between runs by construction — and on current models cannot be pinned, since sampling parameters are rejected outright. So two passes over the same window would emit two annotations differing only in the weight, and the replay check would read them as *two things that happened* rather than one thing recomputed.

*Three ways out, none chosen:* exclude the weight from the comparison, so replay detection keys on span and label as it does now; make weighted annotations a distinct shape the idempotency check skips; or forbid recomputation of a window that already carries one, which makes the first weight permanent and the provenance a record rather than a current opinion. The third is the most conservative and the least useful.

*Worth noting the invariant is cited to [[id:78f485a8-e808-43ce-af5f-277a5c97c471][78f485a8]]*, so this is not an incidental implementation detail but a property something was built to have.

*Scoped by the user 2026-09-01, and it settles most of the above:* "provenance and non-determinism are not worth agonizing over. the goal is plausible, representative estimates in the aggregate, say at the per-day or per-slice level."

*That is a design decision, not a relaxation of standards, and it makes the mechanism simpler in four places.* If the unit of interest is a day or a slice, then *apportion at that level rather than per turn* — one pass over the day's turns, one set of shares. Which dissolves:

- *The resolution floor.* A day holds hundreds of minutes, so a 2% share is representable; a three-minute turn could not express one. The floor was an artefact of apportioning per turn.
- *Rounding.* One largest-remainder apportionment over the day, conserved once, instead of one per turn with per-turn residues.
- *The idempotency collision.* One annotation per task per day rather than per turn, and recomputation replaces a day's set wholesale instead of racing the replay check.
- *Cost.* One subagent call per day, not one per turn — which is also what makes the "lag" in the original proposal pay for itself.

*What survives, and it is small.* A weighted CLOCK line still sums into clocktables beside measured ones, so *something* must distinguish them — not for reproducibility, but so a reader does not take a derived minute for an observed one. One token in the annotation does it, exactly as =:CLOSED_SOURCE: git= did for [[id:b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a][b7b46a26]]. That is a marker, not a provenance system, and it should not grow into one.

*One residual bias worth stating rather than agonising over.* Dropping sub-minute shares systematically under-attributes tasks that only ever receive small slices. At day level that is mostly moot — but a task that got 3% of every turn all day would land near zero, and that reads as "untouched" rather than "marginal". Whether that matters depends on what the aggregate is for; it is a known distortion, not a bug to fix in advance.

*And the aggregation level buys a check the per-turn version could not have:* the weighted minutes attributed across a day should sum to that day's agent time. That is a real invariant, cheap to assert, and a much stronger test of plausibility than eyeballing any single turn's split.

*Corrected by the user 2026-09-01, and the correction sharpens rather than blurs:* not "apportion at the aggregation level", but *"the result of aggregating the apportioned intervals should be plausible and representative."*

*The difference is that the aggregate is the acceptance test, not the unit of production.* Apportioning stays at the turn or window level; the day or slice is where the answer is *judged*. The previous entry conflated the two — it moved the mechanism to match the criterion, which threw away two things:

- *Turn-level attribution is itself the finer record, and it re-aggregates.* Apportion per window and you can still ask per-day, per-slice, per-week. Apportion per day and the question "what was this turn about" has no answer any more. Aggregating early is lossy and irreversible.
- *It discarded the reason for windowing at all.* The user's own point — successive turns concern *intersecting sets of referents* — is an argument for a window larger than a turn and smaller than a day. "Day level" was just the far end of that spectrum with the motivation removed.

*So fewer of the four dissolve than the previous entry claimed, and the honest accounting is:*

- *Resolution floor: mitigated, not removed*, and now visibly a *function of the window parameter*. A window of an hour makes a 2% share about a minute — borderline. Window width trades directly against how fine a share can be expressed. That is input to the parameterisation question, which the user has deferred; it is not an answer to it.
- *Rounding: dissolves as a correctness worry but not as a mechanism.* Largest-remainder is unbiased, so per-window residues average out at the aggregate — which is exactly what an aggregate-level criterion licenses.
- *Idempotency: unchanged.* Still one annotation per window; the fix is still to key replay detection on span and label and treat the weight as payload.
- *Cost: per window rather than per day.* Between the two.

*And the correction promotes one concern rather than dissolving it.* "Representative" is a stronger word than "plausible": it asks that the *distribution across tasks* reflect what happened, not merely that no single number look absurd. Dropping sub-minute shares is exactly a failure of that kind — every individual figure stays plausible while a task that received a small slice of every turn aggregates to near zero. *Plausible per turn can aggregate to unrepresentative*, and the criterion the user has stated is the one that catches it.

*Which also names the test.* Aggregate plausibility is checkable against the reconstructible-stretch yardstick [[id:445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb][445ec1f1]] settled on — a day whose commits, test deltas and debriefs bound what actually happened.


* TODO AFK markers are ground truth for the one judgement we cannot automate, and we discard them :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e75d660f-5c0d-4707-a645-b5dea7d7ebc0
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 13:11]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

*Asked by the user 2026-09-01: "how are we currently using my AFK/BAK markers?" Nowhere mechanically. They exist only in the conversation.*

*Verified rather than recalled.* A =resume= event carries four fields — =ts=, =kind=, =session_id=, =source=. Across *1868* =resume=/=pause= events in every queue file including the archive, no other field is ever non-null. =bin/hooks/session-resume= is one line, =exec queue-append resume=, and =queue-append= nulls =id=, =state=, =from= and =note= for the session-global kinds by design. *The prompt text is never captured.*

*What the markers have actually been used for, in-session only:* deciding whether to keep working unattended; writing an AFK boundary into prose by hand — [[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]]'s body records "the user reports being AFK from 13:30" because a session put it there deliberately; and reasoning about which spans not to trust. None of that reaches the queue, and the review pass has no idea any of it was said.

*Which is the finding, and it is sharper than a missing feature.* The markers are *precisely the ground truth for the one judgement the system cannot make*. [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] measured that gap *length* separates presence from absence while clock hour does not — 11 of 19 long gaps began inside working hours — so the system has no way to tell an AFK gap from a thinking gap. And [[id:20cecd53-2338-4237-ab06-f8129e10a2c8][20cecd53]] found that the only judgement the human-to-agent ratio needs is "were you at the keyboard", on five or six gaps a day.

*The user has been answering that question unprompted, in band, at the moment of highest confidence — and it is thrown away every time.* On 2026-09-01 there were 5 gaps of 1200s or more and the boundaries of most were stated in the conversation as they happened. That is a labelled dataset arriving free.

*Why this is worth more than the retrospective alternative.* Every other path to the same label asks the human to reconstruct, hours later, whether they were present during a gap they no longer remember. A marker typed at the boundary is contemporaneous and costs nothing, because the user is already typing it for their own reasons.

*Options, unranked and undecided:*

1. *Capture the whole prompt on =resume=.* Maximal information, and the wrong default: the queue is a durable, unencrypted log that outlives the session, and putting every prompt in it is a privacy and size decision far larger than this question.
2. *Capture a marker only* — detect an AFK/BAK-shaped token and record a boolean, or a distinct =kind=. Narrow, cheap, and leaves the queue's contents as bounded as they are today.
3. *Ask at review instead*, listing the long gaps and taking an answer per gap. No hook change, but it asks the human to remember rather than to have said.

*A precondition for any of them, unverified:* whether the =UserPromptSubmit= payload reaching =queue-append= actually carries the prompt text. Everything above assumes it does. That is one =jq= away and should be checked before any of this is designed, not after.

*Related but distinct:* [[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]] proposes reading power assertions as an attribution signal — machine evidence for the same question. This is the human's own testimony, which is cheaper and probably more accurate, and the two are not exclusive.

*Measured 2026-09-01, and it replaces all three options above with a fourth — plus one problem that is harder than expected.*

*The precondition is moot, because the data is already joinable.* Claude Code's own transcripts live in =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= — 48 of them for this project — and every user message carries an ISO timestamp. *Every one of this session's 28 =resume= events matches a transcript user message within two seconds; 28 of 28.* So the prompt text is already durably recorded beside a key that joins it to the queue exactly.

*Option 4, which dominates 1-3: join queue to transcript on timestamp.* No hook change, no new field in a durable log, no privacy decision about what the queue should hold, and it works *retroactively* across every session ever recorded rather than only forwards.

*The =UserPromptSubmit= payload question is therefore unanswered and no longer blocking.* It was not checked: verifying it means teeing a live hook that runs on every prompt, and the join removes the reason to. It matters only if a *live in-session* marker is ever wanted, which nothing yet needs.

*The hard part is not access, it is the labelling rule — and two attempts at one both failed.* A gap should be labelled absent when the message opening the preceding turn says the user is leaving, or the message ending the gap says they are back. Both drafts over-labelled: the first treated any earlier AFK message as labelling every later gap, the second still marked gaps of *5 and 14 seconds* as absences. Two causes, and both are structural rather than sloppy:

- *Mid-turn messages break the pairing.* The user can send "still AFK" while the agent is working, which writes a =resume= that does not correspond to the start of a wait. The pause/resume stream stops being an alternation.
- *"Back" is ambiguous in ordinary prose* — "go back to the slice" is not a return announcement — while "BAK" is unambiguous and rarer.

*So the honest state: the signal is real and the access is solved; the extraction is not.* Rates from the flawed rules are not reported here on purpose, since both are known wrong and a wrong number is worse than none ([[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]).

*What would settle it is a handful of human judgements rather than a better regex.* Twenty-odd long gaps in this project's history plausibly carry a marker; the user can confirm or reject each in a few minutes, and that becomes the labelled set a rule is *validated against* rather than derived from. That is also the first ground truth the 1200s threshold has ever had, which is [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]'s September occurrence.

*The imputation rule, proposed by the user 2026-09-01, fixes the part my two attempts got wrong:* declare that *any prompt without "still AFK" or similar means BAK*. The user noted they had probably forgotten a BAK this session, and that a mechanical consumer should not depend on remembering one.

*This makes it a state machine rather than a sticky flag, which is precisely where both earlier drafts failed.* Absence *ends* at the next unmarked prompt instead of persisting until an explicit return. Presence becomes the default and absence the marked state — so a forgotten BAK costs nothing, and the "any earlier AFK message labels every later gap" failure cannot occur by construction.

*Detecting the marker is the remaining problem, and it is the mention-versus-subject trap for the third time today* — after the footnote modes and the weighted-attribution candidate set. A substring test for =afk= matches this very conversation, which is *about* AFK markers.

*Sentence-boundedness resolves it, measured on this session's 45 prompts:* require =afk= (optionally preceded by =still=) to stand as its own sentence — delimiter or string start before, delimiter or end after. Eight prompts mention =afk=; the rule marks *five* and rejects three, and all three rejections are correct:

| prompt | verdict |
|--------+---------|
| "how are we currently using my AFK/BAK markers?" | discussion, correctly ignored |
| "re: AFK, I probably forgot to emit a BAK..." | discussion, correctly ignored |
| "afk since 13:30." | *retrospective report*, not a departure |

*That third rejection is right for a subtler reason and names a separate channel.* "afk since 13:30" was sent *on returning* — it labels a gap that had already happened. Under the imputation rule it correctly reads as presence, but the information it carries about the *preceding* gap is dropped. So there are two kinds of statement here: a *departure marker* that opens an absence, and a *retrospective correction* that labels a past one. Only the first is needed for the state machine; the second is a richer channel and a later question.

*So the mechanism is now viable end to end:* transcript join (28 of 28 exact) supplies the prompts, sentence-boundedness supplies the markers, and the imputation rule supplies the state transitions without depending on the user remembering anything.

*The user pushed back 2026-09-01, correctly:* "afk since 13:30" followed by a long gap could equally mean /"I forgot to say AFK and I'm still AFK for now"/ — so classifying it as a return is not airtight. It is not, and the data agrees: that prompt was followed by a *77-minute* gap.

*Two corrections to the entry above, both from measurement.*

- *A contaminated denominator.* The "5 of 8" was measured over raw =type:"user"= transcript entries — which include *stop-hook feedback*, injected in the user role. Using the queue's =resume= events as the spine instead gives *36 real prompts* against 45 raw entries; nine were not prompts at all. This is [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]'s recorded trap ("276 =type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results") walked into again, and the fix is to let the queue define what a prompt is rather than the transcript.
- *The disambiguation comes from the other end.* The gap after "afk since 13:30" *is* labelled — not by its opener but by its *closer*, an explicit "BAK". So classifying **gaps** using markers at *either* end is strictly better than tracking an AFK *state* from openers alone, and it is what makes an ambiguous opener harmless.

*But coverage is partial, and that is the honest bound.* Of this session's seven longest inter-prompt gaps, markers label *three*: 193m by its opener, 74m by its closer, 68m by its opener. Four are unlabelled, including one of 776m and two around an hour. So markers do not replace the threshold; they *override* it where present.

*Which is the right shape anyway, given the stated bar.* The threshold treats every gap over 1200s identically. A marker adds discrimination exactly where the threshold is guessing — and can contradict it in both directions, marking a short gap as absence or a long one as presence. Aiming for airtight would mean asking the user to annotate every gap, which is the clerical burden this whole line of work exists to avoid.

*So: markers as evidence, threshold as fallback, and the residue stated rather than hidden* — roughly half of long gaps carry no marker today, and the honest report says which are labelled and which are inferred rather than presenting one number for both.


* TODO The human half of the ratio is already recorded, and thrown away :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       20cecd53-2338-4237-ab06-f8129e10a2c8
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 12:17]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-01 Tue 12:15]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:18] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-01 Tue 12:15]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:18] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-01 Tue 12:26]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:27] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-01 Tue 12:26]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:27] assigned at review
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-09-01, considering turning clocking off altogether:* the metric they actually want is *the ratio of their own time — thinking, prompting, reading — to the time the agent is working*, and their expectation is that any automated approximation of their attention "is a dramatic underestimate of what is happening in reality".

*The underestimate is real, and it is on the other side of the ledger.* What undercounts is the *agent* attribution, not the human: a span reading =0:11= writes =0:01=, measured repeatedly on 2026-08-31. The human's time is not undercounted — it is *uncollected by choice*.

*Because the numerator is already recorded, on every turn, and then discarded.* =resume= fires on =UserPromptSubmit=; =pause= fires when the turn ends. So a =pause= -> =resume= gap is *exactly* reading the response, thinking, and typing the next prompt. =--span-work-runs= extracts only the =resume= -> =pause= runs and treats the gaps as idle. [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] already says so in as many words: "the human reading a session report -- real attention, discarded by the extraction rather than recorded as anything."

*Measured on this session's own queue, 2026-09-01:* 44 guideposts, 19 agent runs and 19 human gaps.

| quantity | value |
|----------+-------|
| agent working (=resume= -> =pause=) | 2.75 h across 19 runs, median 353 s |
| human gaps (=pause= -> =resume=) | 19.81 h across 19 gaps, median 393 s, max 760 min |
| human gaps under 10 minutes | 1.08 h across 13 gaps |
| turns (=resume= events) | 22 |

*The raw ratio of 7.19 : 1 is meaningless* — it is dominated by six AFK gaps, one of them 12.7 hours. Capped at ten minutes it is *0.39 : 1*. The true figure is between them and only a human knows where.

*Which is the actual finding: the judgement needed is far smaller than the one currently being asked for.* The review pass asks the human to attribute spans *to headings*. This ratio needs no heading at all — it is session-global. The only judgement is "were you at the keyboard", on *six* gaps today rather than nineteen, and only for the raw total; the capped figure and the turn count need none.

*So collection and attribution can be decoupled, and that is the proposal.* Guideposts are written by hooks, cost nothing, and need no Emacs. Attribution to headings is the expensive half and the one the user says they value least. Turning off *attribution* while continuing to *collect* would preserve this metric entirely and remove the clerical burden that prompted the question.

*What that would give up, stated so the trade is visible:* per-heading clock intervals, and with them any answer to "how long did this task take". [[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]] is the heading that says constructing attention intervals is manual by nature; this does not change that, it only observes that the *ratio* does not require it.

*Open, and genuinely undecided:* whether the gap-versus-AFK threshold should ever be automated. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the standing precedent against an invented number, and a ten-minute cap is exactly that. Reporting both bounds and letting the human place the line is the alternative — and unlike the current review pass, it is six questions a day rather than nineteen.

*The theme, briefly, in place of a plan — the full prompt comes later.* The previous slice was about the gap between what the code *knows* and what it *says*. This one is about a gap one layer down: the record itself going wrong while every check reports success.

Three things happened on 2026-08-31/09-01 that share a shape. =bin/lint-org= reported *0 errors* across several runs while a duplicate =:ID:= sat in the corpus — and an =:ID:= is the primary key, so a duplicate makes every id-taking tool ambiguous. The live Emacs served a =config.el= two hours stale, silently, and was caught only because the change happened to be to *visible wording*; a behavioural change would have been invisible. And the ceremony's archive step failed *after* copying and before deleting, leaving a heading in two files at once — a partially-applied write, not a refused one.

To which add the standing friction: the two MCP tools that time out are worked around by =emacsclient= a dozen times a session, so the tool surface is bypassed rather than fixed; and two =id= arguments reject an 8-character prefix, one of them by *silently doing nothing* and reporting a clean no-op.

*So the members divide roughly three ways:* checks that do not catch, writes that half-succeed, and one archive duplication whose mechanism is still unknown. What they have in common is that none of them is visible from a green suite.
