* TODO Three new ways to owe a footnote and not see it: marker, list, quotation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2a6a1355-615f-4c2c-a8af-c9ae8dcda09b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 08:43]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- [2026-09-01 Tue 08:39]--[2026-09-01 Tue 08:42] assigned at review
:END:

*Asked by the user 2026-09-01 after a third stop-hook refusal in one session:* "check for others this session (same or different?)". *Different — all three.*

| # | id refused | what the id was doing |
|---+------------+-----------------------|
| 1 | =72463b68= | superscript ⁴ was reused for two ids; the marker existed, the footnote line was another heading's |
| 2 | eleven at once | enumerated in prose lists and a table — "phase 4 has =c60a1c53=, then =5f1068f9=/=33864a0f=" |
| 3 | =b36e6369= | inside a *quoted example* — ="add X to b36e6369"=, illustrating what a commit subject looks like |

*None of these is either recorded mode.* [[id:d5490814-d174-480a-a9e7-b809f94d71f2][d5490814]] is *id-as-vocabulary*: the token has become the name of a lesson, so it stops reading as a citation. [[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]] is *decay on re-mention*: cited correctly the first time, abbreviated after. In all three cases here the id was neither a name nor a re-mention.

*The unifying observation, and it is sharper than "be careful".* The hook's test is *does the response contain this token*. The writer's test is *am I referring to a heading here*. Those agree only while an id appears **because** it refers. They diverge whenever it appears for some other reason:

- *bookkeeping* — the id was cited, and the failure is in the numbering rather than the recognition (case 1). This is the one where the intent was entirely correct;
- *enumeration* — the id is an item in a list of work, where the eye reads a row rather than a reference (case 2);
- *quotation* — the id belongs to a string being *shown*, not to a sentence being *written* (case 3).

*So the three recorded modes now cover recognition, decay, and reference-versus-appearance*, and only the last has a mechanical smell to it: the hook already knows every id in the response and every footnote marker, so it could say *which* marker is duplicated or missing rather than only which id is unfootnoted. Case 1 would have been a one-line fix at the moment it happened.

*Worth noting what did not fail.* Every response carrying ids in *ordinary prose* passed. The rule is not hard to follow when the id is doing the job the rule was written for.

*Open: whether any of this is worth mechanising.* Against — the hook already catches every instance, so nothing reaches the user, and three refusals in a session of roughly forty responses is a low rate for a check whose whole purpose is to be the backstop. For — case 1 is a *numbering* error the hook could diagnose precisely, and cases 2 and 3 suggest the writer's attention is on the sentence rather than the token, which no amount of care fixes. Deciding that is this heading's job; it should not be assumed either way.

*A fourth mode, found 2026-09-01 immediately after filing the first three, and it is the only one the hook cannot see.*

A response footnoted [[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]] as "Footnote discipline decays on re-mention, not on first citation". Its actual title is *"[1/1] Footnote discipline decays on re-mention; enforce it at Stop"*. The wrong version was reconstructed from a *paraphrase inside another heading's body* — [[id:d5490814-d174-480a-a9e7-b809f94d71f2][d5490814]] describes it as "footnote discipline decaying on *re-mention*, where every offender was correct the first time" — which reads exactly like a title and is not one.

*The hook passed it.* =footnote-check= tests whether each cited id *has* a footnote, not whether the footnote is *right*. So this mode produces a confidently-wrong citation that no mechanism refuses — the opposite of the other three, where the failure is loud and the response is simply rejected.

*Which makes it the more serious of the four, on this project's own reasoning.* A refused response costs a retry. A footnote with a plausible wrong title is [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s shape — a value that is harder to reject than an absence — and it propagates: the next reader has no reason to doubt it, and the footnote exists precisely for readers who cannot see the heading.

*The instruction already anticipates this and says so in as many words* — the standing rule is that each footnote carries the id's "*exact, full title*, looked up rather than recalled", and that "a remembered title is where paraphrase creeps back in". What this instance adds is that *a paraphrase in a sibling heading's body is indistinguishable from a lookup*, because it arrives as prose about that heading and in this project's own voice. Recalling from memory at least feels like recall; reading a neighbour's summary feels like reading the source.

*So the mechanisable half moves.* The first three modes argue for better marker bookkeeping in the hook. This one argues for something different and cheaper: the hook already resolves every cited id to check it is tracked, so it *has* the real title and could compare it against the footnote line. That would turn the only silent mode of the four into a loud one.

*A fifth mode, 2026-09-01: the id arrives inside borrowed prose.* A response compressed [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]'s own sentence — "Since =226ed53b= the gap threshold governs only grouping and display" — into its own words, and =226ed53b= came along as part of the transcription rather than as a citation anyone chose to make. The hook refused it, twice in one response.

*Distinct from the quotation mode above, and worse.* That one was an id inside a *quoted example string*, visibly not the writer's own sentence. This one reads as the writer's prose, because it is — only a fragment of it was copied. Nothing on the surface marks the borrowed span, so there is no cue to check.

*Which makes the pattern across all five statable, and it is sharper than any of them individually.* Every failure is an id the writer *did not choose to write*: marker bookkeeping, list enumeration, quoted example, a title paraphrased from a neighbour's body, and now a sentence adapted from one. The ids that are deliberately cited get footnoted every time — that half of the discipline has never failed.

*So the rule the writer is actually following is "footnote what I decided to reference", and the hook's rule is "footnote every id present".* The gap between those two is exactly the set of ids that arrive by transcription. That is a more useful statement of the defect than five separate modes, and it suggests the check to want is not better discipline but a diff: the hook already knows every id in the response and every id in the footnote list.

*Asked 2026-09-01: could footnoting be done by a subagent? It should not, and the reason is that most of it is already done by a script.*

*=bin/hooks/footnote-check= already performs every step but the last.* It extracts the =\b[0-9a-f]{8}\b= candidates from the response, filters them by grepping =^:ID: +$id= across TODO.org and DONE.org — so it *has located the heading* — and checks which are missing from the footnote block. What it never does is read the *title* sitting a few lines above the =:ID:= line it just matched.

*Which makes the fix small and the model unnecessary.* Emitting id, current keyword and exact title for each unfootnoted citation would make the fourth mode — a title reconstructed from a neighbour's paraphrase — *structurally impossible*, because the correct string would be on screen at the moment the footnote is written. That is the only one of the five modes the hook cannot currently catch, and it is the only one that produces a confidently wrong answer rather than a rejected response.

*A subagent would be the wrong instrument for three reasons.* The task is a lookup, not a judgement — there is nothing to weigh. It would be non-deterministic where the answer is exact. And it could *paraphrase*, which is precisely the failure being fixed; a =grep= cannot.

*The real constraint is what a hook may do, and it is worth stating because it bounds every version of this.* A =Stop= hook can refuse and inform; it cannot rewrite the response. So no mechanism here can *write* the footnotes — it can only hand over the exact lines. That still removes two of the three error classes: detection (the hook already sees every id) and lookup (it would now carry the title). What remains is *placement* — the superscript markers — which is where the marker-collision mode lives and which nothing but care can fix.

*So the honest scope: two of five modes become impossible, one becomes loud, and two stay.* Enumeration and borrowed prose are already caught by the existing check; the marker collision stays a hand error; the paraphrased title stops being possible. That is a good return on adding one field to a message the hook already prints.

*Refined 2026-09-01: not done by a subagent — assisted by one?* The distinction is real, so the question is what a model could add that a script cannot.

*Almost nothing, on inspection.* Detection, lookup, presence-checking, ordering by first appearance and comparing a written footnote against the true title are all exact operations, and =footnote-check= already performs the first three. A model would be slower, non-deterministic, and able to paraphrase — the failure being fixed.

*One candidate is genuinely model-shaped, and it is the inverse of the rule the hook enforces.* The hook asks "is every id present also footnoted". It cannot ask "was every heading *discussed* also cited" — a response can describe a heading at length, by subject rather than by id, and owe a footnote nobody can detect by scanning for tokens. That is a judgement about aboutness, which is exactly what a regex cannot do.

*Measured before proposing it, and the result is a clean negative.* Across *314 assistant responses* in this session, scanning for a heading whose four-word title phrase appears while its id does not: *zero*. The failure this assist would catch did not occur once.

*But the measurement cannot see the case the model would be best at, and that limit is the finding.* The test requires a *verbatim* title fragment. Discussion that paraphrases — "the ceremony trigger", "the read-only guard" — matches nothing and is invisible to it. So the zero rules out verbatim uncited discussion and says nothing about the paraphrased kind, which is precisely where a model would earn its place. *Measuring that needs a different instrument, and the obvious one is the model itself* — which makes the evidence circular unless a human adjudicates a sample.

*So the ordering is clear even though the question is not settled.* Extend the hook first: it is cheap, exact, and closes the only mode that currently produces a wrong answer rather than a refusal. Revisit assistance only if an uncited-by-paraphrase instance is ever *observed*, rather than building for a failure with no recorded occurrence — which is [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s standing argument, and applies to a check as much as to a guess.


* TODO Report worked headings that carry no clock interval that day :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       37147e9e-468b-43ee-91f9-45d9647c6164
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 08:38]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:

Split out of [[id:c60a1c53-ba7f-40a2-979a-6ce0a16ce3c8][c60a1c53]] 2026-09-01, which shipped the slice-omission half and left this one in prose. That body rates *this* the more valuable of the two, so leaving it as a paragraph inside a closed heading was the wrong place for it.

*The signal already exists.* =claude-code-ide-org--commit-subject-ids= returns the headings a commit subject claims to be *about*. Crossing that against the day's clock intervals answers a second question from the same scan: which worked headings carry no clocked time at all.

*Why it is the more valuable half, in that body's own words.* A forgotten slice member is a bookkeeping error someone notices at review. *Un-clocked work is invisible*: it lands on the meta-work day node as an unassigned span, indistinguishable at review from planning, and the human attributing it has no way to tell that three of those hours were implementation on a specific heading.

*Measured when it was found, 2026-08-25:* 52 state changes queued that day and *zero* clock events, the last having been 2026-08-24 16:42. Every heading went =TODO= -> =DONE= in one step — not a listed transition, so no rule ever fired to open a clock. *The omission is structural rather than forgetful*: nothing asks for a clock on a transition that was never made.

*And it is exactly the residue [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] shipped to eliminate*, which is the sharp part. That heading made explicit clock pairs authoritative so attribution would stop needing a human — and it was the first member of the slice during which the pairs stopped being produced at all. It closed the consumption half; the discipline half regressed behind it.

*Open, and it is a different report with a different audience*, which is why it was not smuggled in beside the forgotten-member line: that one asks "should this be a slice member", this one asks "where did the day go". Worth deciding whether it belongs at the end of a review pass at all, or in the =SessionStart= report where the other attention questions live.

*Related:* [[id:445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb][445ec1f1]] is the A/B on whether the logged record means anything; this is one of the concrete gaps that A/B would be measuring.


* TODO bin/lint-org does not detect a duplicate :ID: across TODO.org and DONE.org :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       39039bb6-c70a-4ba9-8b37-edd8dd6921f6
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 08:19]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

*Found 2026-09-01, by a corruption surviving every commit-time check.* A partially-applied archive left [[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]] as a heading in *both* TODO.org and DONE.org ([[id:13ea6770-88ba-484d-87bd-2d20c44dc610][13ea6770]]), and =bin/lint-org= reported *0 errors* throughout — across several runs and a =.githooks/pre-commit= gate.

*Why this is a gap rather than an omission.* An =:ID:= is the primary key of this entire system: [[id:478d6ec9-1b99-4c6c-88e9-c793ad4b2f22][478d6ec9]] made prefixes resolvable precisely so ids could be referenced everywhere, and =--id-find= resolves a prefix by scanning for a *unique* match. A duplicate id therefore does not merely offend tidiness — it makes resolution *ambiguous*, and every tool that takes an id can silently act on the wrong copy. The prefix expander already has an =ambiguous= answer for the case where two ids share a prefix; it has nothing for two headings sharing an id outright.

*It was caught, but by the wrong thing and too late.* The archive's own post-condition reported =Archived 20, but 1 id(s) now appear twice in DONE.org=. That guard fires *after* writing and does not roll back, so it documents a corruption rather than preventing one. A lint rule runs before every commit and would have refused it.

*Cheap, and the scan already exists.* =claude-code-ide-org--lint-heading-ids= already walks every heading in every file collecting ids — the duplicate check is a =seq-group-by= over what it has in hand rather than new traversal.

*Error rather than warning, on this project's own test:* the correct state is computable and a duplicate is never a judgement call. Worth reporting *both* locations, since "id X is duplicated" without saying where sends the reader grepping — the failure [[id:6cc71c36-0fa6-4773-9b35-c0582f4cee82][6cc71c36]] is about.

*One question to settle while building it:* whether an =:ID:= appearing in an =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:=-style property or in body prose should count. It should not — only heading =:ID:= properties are keys — but the scan must be written to distinguish them, and a naive =grep '^:ID:'= over file text would already be right for the wrong reason.


* TODO The running Emacs can be running a stale config.el and nothing says so :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f12f9da4-2870-45b7-8c50-ea54415c7bc6
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 17:47]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-31 after the live Emacs was found serving a =config.el= roughly two hours out of date.* Their question was whether the SOP needs invoking explicitly from the next-session prompt. It does not, and the reason is the interesting part.

*The SOP exists, is thorough, and is in the right place.* The org-dev skill's own description already says to trigger "proactively whenever =config.el=, =config-test.el=, or the Doom config has just been edited in this session — don't wait to be asked before checking that a code change actually loaded", and §1 gives the =load-file= procedure, §2 the cases needing a full restart, §4 a sanity-check pattern. *Nothing about the guidance failed. It was simply not invoked*, across roughly fifteen edits to =config.el= in one session.

*So the gap is not documentation, and adding it to the next-session prompt would be the wrong shape twice over.* That prompt is a *plan* — consumed and rewritten once acted on — so a standing need placed there evaporates by construction. And the instruction already sits where Claude Code actually matches it. Writing it a second time creates a copy that can disagree with the first, which is the objection this project raises against declaring anything already derivable.

*The measured part, because "this has not been a problem in recent sessions" was the user's own framing and deserved testing rather than agreement.* =config.el= churn per day over the preceding fortnight: 2026-08-24 was 16 commits and +1035 lines, 08-26 was 12 and +853, 08-25 was 11 and +849. This session was 16 commits and +959 — *entirely typical*. So "recent sessions edited it less" is dead as an explanation.

*Which leaves the uncomfortable hypothesis, and it is the one this heading is really about: it has been happening and going unnoticed.* The failure is silent by construction — a stale module produces no error, and the review pass simply behaves as it did yesterday. Today it was caught by *luck of the change's shape*: the edit happened to be to visible *wording* in a tool reply, and =writes 0:05 in 1= was recognisable as a string that should no longer exist. The behavioural changes shipped the same afternoon — auto-marking, the =g= confirmation, the no-op skip — would all have been *invisible*, and an apply pass using the old code would have looked entirely normal. Absence of reports is therefore not evidence of absence.

*Precedent that it is a real failure mode rather than a worry:* the org-dev skill records 2026-08-11, when the =:SESSIONS:= drawer was retired, every writer deleted, all 49 drawers removed, suite green — and minutes later a clock-out recreated one, because =config.el= had been edited on disk and never reloaded.

*Proposed fix: make the failure loud rather than the discipline stronger.* A trigger that depends on model judgement has now failed silently for a whole session, and the answer this project reaches for is a mechanism that reports rather than a rule to remember. Sketch, and it is cheap:

- =config.el= sets a =claude-code-ide-org--loaded-at= timestamp at load time. Confirmed absent today, so this is a new =defvar= plus one =setq= at top level.
- Anything already round-tripping to Emacs compares it against =(file-attribute-modification-time (file-attributes config-path))= and says so when the file is newer.
- The natural home is the =SessionStart= report, which already carries the stale-interval and ceremony prompts and already follows the *ask, never act* contract. A second line there costs nothing.

*Deliberately not proposed: auto-reloading on edit.* It looks tidier and is worse — §2 of the skill lists changes a live reload cannot cover, so an automatic =load-file= would sometimes produce a *confident* wrong answer, which is the failure mode this project consistently rates as worse than an absent one.

*Note the check has a real limit worth writing into it:* file mtime is newer than load time is not the same as "the running Emacs is wrong", since an edit may not have touched anything loaded. The report should say what it knows — the file is newer than the load — and let the reader decide, exactly as the stale-interval report states a timestamp and asks rather than guessing.


* TODO Sixty minutes holding 2 turns is not sixty holding 40 [1/7]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f90d745e-ccf9-49d6-a92e-df25c17b4dfb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 16:39]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Clock duration cannot tell attention apart from absence. Sixty minutes holding 2 turns and sixty holding 40 are identical in a =:LOGBOOK:= and completely different in what the human was doing — and attention, not duration, is this project's second stated goal. Turn density is the cheapest available proxy: =resume= events already exist, one per =UserPromptSubmit=, so every child below is a *view* of data already recorded rather than new collection.

The children divide by *where the signal has to appear* and *what has to be true before it means anything*. Two standing constraints govern all of them: **never present a total that looks complete**, since the tool sees Claude sessions and nothing else and the gaps are exactly where meatspace went; and **prefer the lighter presentation every time**, because the point is to keep a human's judgement calls quick, and a readout that is more accurate but heavier to read is a worse readout.

*Born 2026-08-31 by dividing [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]*, which had grown a shipped half and four open ones inside a single body.


** DONE Show turn count on the span line, where the attention call is made :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-31 Mon 16:43]
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:

- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:04]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:08] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07] \\
  surface undecided, analysis recorded
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:31]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:33] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:31]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:33] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:44]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:45] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:44]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:45] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:53]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:58] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:53]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:58] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 15:01]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:03] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 15:01]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:03] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 15:17]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] =>  0:14
- [2026-08-15 Sat 15:17]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-31 Mon 16:38]--[2026-08-31 Mon 16:43] =>  0:05
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-31 Mon 16:38]
- [2026-08-31 Mon 16:38]--[2026-08-31 Mon 16:43] mitosis and prose redistribution
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-31 Mon 16:43]
:END:
:PLAN:

Put the turn count on the review buffer's span line, so a human judging an interval can see what the stretch *felt* like without doing arithmetic.

*Why the review line and not the durable record.* The first recommendation was the opposite — annotation only, keep it out of the review buffer — on the reasoning that =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks later. That reversed once it was settled that attention intervals are constructed *at review*: if the judgement happens there, that is where the information has to be. The annotation keeps a narrower job and is [[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]].

*Count =resume=, not =pause=.* An interrupted turn emits no =pause= but always had a =resume=, so the resume is the robust anchor.

*A recall cue, not a metric.* The value of turn density is that it tells the human what a stretch felt like — at the keyboard, or set running and left — quickly enough that they recognise it and move on. A number they must weigh is heavier than a word they can recognise, and lightness wins.

---

:END:
*Shipped 2026-08-31.* The span line reads =0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns=. Turn count is the third of three observed quantities, built in one pass with [[id:98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d][98c302e0]] and [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] because all three bodies asked for that rather than three rewordings of one line.

*No new data, exactly as predicted.* =claude-code-ide-org--span-turn-seconds= already returned =(SECONDS . TURNS)=; the count was computed and discarded. The "a view of data already recorded" argument held with nothing to add — no hook, no payload field, no failure mode.

*The foundation was checked before building on it, and the check produced a trap worth keeping.* Measured 2026-08-15: 22 =resume= events against ~25 string-content user messages in that session's transcript. Close but not equal, and the discrepancy is systematic rather than random — harness-injected user-role turns (=ToolSearch= "Tool loaded" replies, =/plan= output) and mid-turn interjections. *And the transcript holds 276 =type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results*: a naive count of user messages is off by an order of magnitude.

*The classifier was deliberately not built.* A dense/sparse label would have satisfied the recall-cue requirement more literally, but it needs a threshold, and a threshold is an invented number laid on a measured one — against which this story's own objection stands. Juxtaposition carries it: =6 turns= beside =0:11 span= reads as "at the keyboard" with nothing invented. The interpretive word is deferred, not rejected, and is a one-line addition if wanted.

*Verified:* the suite went 465 to 468 tests, 0 unexpected, exit 0, with the readout rework. Fixing the turn count to a constant fails three tests and nothing else.

*Left to siblings rather than done here, and each is now a heading rather than a bullet:* the =:LOGBOOK:= annotation ([[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]]), the clocktable column ([[id:97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0][97503194]]), the cross-session allocation problem ([[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]]), where the not-productivity caveat lives ([[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]]), and the zero-turns ambiguity for background and subagent sessions ([[id:9b5eb830-8825-4d6c-b701-c167c2d71fdf][9b5eb830]]).

*One thing this heading shipped without:* nothing on the line says the number must never be read as productivity, which under autonomy it inverts. That is [[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]], and it is a real gap rather than a deferral — the argument that juxtaposition carries the meaning covers a reader building an impression, not one importing a reading from elsewhere.

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31, which shipped the review-buffer surface and named this as still open.

The turn count now appears on the review line, but queue files are drained and archived, and =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks later. Not writing it there means re-deriving it from archives — which is possible and is exactly the friction the count exists to remove.

=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation= is the site. The parent's original judgement was "costs one token on a line that already exists", which still looks right.

*Open question inherited rather than settled:* whether the annotation should carry the *envelope* too, now that the review line reports envelope, runs and turns together. An annotation recording only the runs loses the same distinction [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] was filed about, one layer down and where nobody is watching.

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] on 2026-08-31, boiled down from where it sat among three competing surfaces.*

*This was originally judged the most useful of the three, and then demoted — the reversal is the part worth carrying.* The first recommendation was "annotation only, keep it out of the review buffer", on the reasoning that the durable record is the point: queue files are drained and archived, and =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks later, so not writing it there means re-deriving it from archives.

That was reversed once it was settled that the human constructs attention intervals *at review*. If the judgement happens at review, that is where the information has to be — which is why the review line shipped first. *The annotation's job is therefore narrower than it looked:* not to inform the call, but to record enough of the outcome and its evidence that the call can be revisited later without being redone.

*Cost is one token on a line that already exists* — =claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation=.

*Open question this heading now owns.* The review line reports *three* quantities — envelope, runs, turns — because a span's outer bracket is not what it writes. An annotation recording only the runs loses exactly that distinction, one layer down and where nobody is watching, which is [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] recurring in the durable record instead of the buffer. So: does the annotation carry the envelope and the turn count too, and at what cost to a line that has to stay readable inside a drawer?


** TODO Decide whether org_clock_report grows a turns-per-hour column :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:44]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31.

The one place the scalar wins outright, per the parent's own analysis: turns/hour can be averaged across a week, and the gap lists that serve review-time judgement better cannot be summed at all.

*Deliberately still a question, not a task.* The parent deferred it "until a real report wants it", and no report does yet. Filed so the deferral is visible rather than buried in a long body; closing it as =CANCELLED= is a perfectly good outcome if the aggregate never gets asked for.

*Carries the parent's caveat*, which is sharper here than anywhere: turns/hour *drops* precisely when the agent works hardest, so a column labelled anything productivity-adjacent would invert its own meaning. See [[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]].

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] on 2026-08-31.*

*The one place the scalar wins outright, and the argument is about summability.* At review time a *gap list* serves the judgement better than a number — the parent's standing principle is to report the exceptions rather than the measurements, and =--span-evidence= already lists absences for that reason. But gap lists cannot be summed at all, and turns/hour can be averaged across a week. Aggregate post-hoc analysis is where =org_clock_report= lives, so it is the one surface where the scalar is not merely an alternative to the gaps but the only thing that works.

*Deliberately still a question rather than a task*, and the parent deferred it on a rule worth keeping: *defer the column until a real report wants it*. None does yet. Closing this =CANCELLED= is a perfectly good outcome if none ever asks.

*Two constraints it inherits, and the first is sharper here than anywhere else in the story.*

- *Never label it in a way that invites a productivity reading*, because a column heading is exactly where such a reading attaches, and the number is the *inverse* of productivity under autonomy. See [[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]], which owns that question.
- *Never present a total that looks complete.* The tool sees Claude sessions and nothing else, and an aggregate table is the most convincing possible way to imply a full accounting of a day. The gaps are precisely where meatspace went.


** TODO Allocate attention across concurrent sessions, which is zero-sum :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:44]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:BLOCKER:  ids(9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806 5461c349-fd22-4b75-a508-50efbe4b87f8)
:END:

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31. *The substantial half, and the one that actually answers the question the parent was asked.*

Attention is zero-sum across concurrent sessions. If the human was driving session B while session A's agent ground away, A's forty minutes should not be credited to A's heading at all — the attention belongs to B. Annotating each span with its own turn count is the easy half; apportioning an hour among the sessions live in it is the half that answers "what did I attend to".

*Two preconditions, both recorded in the parent and neither met:*

- *Hooks are project-scoped*, so only sessions in this repo write queue files and the directory sees one project. [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] is therefore a *precondition* rather than a neighbour — portability work motivated for an entirely different reason that turns out to be load-bearing here.
- *Queue events record no =cwd=.* The hook payload carries one (verified 2026-08-14), so this is one line in the =jq= object rather than new instrumentation. Deriving project from =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= is a fragile join where a recorded field is free.

*The shape of the answer is constrained, and the constraint is the parent's:* the tool informs a judgement, it does not compute an allocation. Attention intervals are constructed manually by the human on review; "hourly resolution" is a rule of thumb for how coarse the answer needs to be, *not* a bucketing scheme, and an earlier paragraph proposing to bucket resume events by hour was corrected as describing an automation this project is not trying to build.

*Whatever is built must not present a total that looks complete* — the tool sees Claude sessions and nothing else, and the gaps are exactly where meatspace went.

*Newly relevant:* [[id:445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb][445ec1f1]], where the user concluded much of the logged activity is bogus. Cross-session allocation is one of the few things that could make a span's attribution *verifiable* rather than merely plausible, so this heading may be more load-bearing for that A/B than it looked when the parent filed it.

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] on 2026-08-31, and narrowed: the =cwd= field this heading named as a precondition is now [[id:5461c349-fd22-4b75-a508-50efbe4b87f8][5461c349]] on its own, since it is a concrete one-line change that should not wait for a judgement this size.*

*The reframing that makes this the substantial half.* Attention is the *primary* measure for assigning attention intervals post hoc, not a supplement to clock duration. Long agent-work intervals do reflect agent productivity — but they are precisely the intervals in which the human attends to something else: email, post hoc review, or another Claude Code session in another project. Intense back-and-forth means the human was at the keyboard interpreting responses, often alternating between tasks and meta-tasks inside one session.

*So the metric inverting under autonomy is the signal, not a caveat.* A low-turn hour is evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being measured.

*Hourly resolution is a real simplification, and it is not a bucketing scheme.* An earlier draft proposed bucketing resume events by hour per session and comparing; the user corrected it. "Hourly" is a rule of thumb for how coarse the *answer* needs to be — "what did I attend to today, roughly" — and does not mean splitting anything on the hour for presentation. The attention intervals are constructed *manually*, by the human, on review of the gathered data.

*Which fixes the shape of whatever gets built:* the tool informs a judgement, it does not compute an allocation. Constructing intervals depends on reflection about meatspace events this tool cannot capture and never will. What it *can* capture should be as informative as possible — *moderated by keeping those judgement calls a lightweight exercise*. That moderation is a first-class requirement rather than a nicety: a presentation that is more accurate but heavier to read is a worse presentation, and the tradeoff resolves in favour of lightness every time.

*Explicitly out of scope, so it is not mistaken for an oversight:* predicting or constructing attention intervals automatically, given more history, external signals and practice. That may become possible; it is not being attempted.

*The remaining precondition is [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]].* Hooks are project-scoped, so only sessions in this repo write queue files at all and the directory sees one project. Moving hooks to =~/.claude/settings.json= is what gives cross-project allocation anything to compare — portability work motivated for an entirely different reason that turns out to be load-bearing here.

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31, where it is a demand the parent makes and nothing yet satisfies.

*The claim:* turn count must never be labelled or read as productivity, because under autonomy it is the *inverse* of it. A forty-minute agent turn counts 1, the same as "yes", so turns/hour drops precisely when the agent is working hardest. The parent settled that this is the metric *working* rather than failing — a low-turn hour is evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being measured — but that reading is not self-evident from a bare number.

*Nothing on the review line says any of this.* =0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns= shipped 2026-08-31 with no caveat attached, on the argument that juxtaposition carries the meaning and that a classifier would need an invented threshold. That argument covers the *recall-cue* reading; it does not cover a reader who imports a productivity reading from elsewhere.

*Worth deciding before the count reaches a second surface*, since a =:LOGBOOK:= annotation ([[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]]) or a clocktable column ([[id:97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0][97503194]]) is read further from context than a review line is, and a column heading is exactly where a productivity reading would attach.

*Candidate answers, none chosen:* the review buffer's key legend; the tool descriptions; a line in CLAUDE.md's session-tracking section; or nowhere, on the grounds that the only reader is the person who set the convention. The last is a real option — this project has one user — and it should be rejected on evidence rather than reflex.

*Related:* [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the standing precedent that a plausible-but-wrong number is worse than no number, which is the same worry one step earlier: here the number is right and the *reading* is what goes wrong.

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] on 2026-08-31, where it was recorded as a con, then reframed as the signal, leaving exactly this residue.*

*The full arc, because the conclusion is not obvious from either end.* It was first written down as a *cost*: a 40-minute agent turn counts 1, the same as "yes", so turns/hour drops precisely when the agent is working hardest — correct as a measure of human attention, badly misleading if read as productivity. The user then reframed it: that is the metric *working*, since a low-turn hour is evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being measured.

*So the caveat that survives is narrower than the original con and stronger than it sounds:* the number must never be labelled or read as productivity, because it is the *inverse* of it. Not "roughly unrelated" — inverted. A reader who imports a productivity reading gets an answer that is not merely imprecise but backwards.

*The neighbouring con, which is a different worry and belongs here too.* The count is *measured*; the inference from it is a guess. A turn may be "continue" or three paragraphs of design, and the difference is invisible. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the precedent for retiring a plausible-but-wrong number on exactly this reasoning — it applies here with less force, since the count itself is honest, but not with none. It is also the argument that killed the dense/sparse classifier: a threshold would be an invented number laid on top of a measured one.

*Explicit non-goal, recorded so nothing is shaped to serve it:* measuring how productive the human was using Claude versus not using Claude. Nothing in this story should support that comparison.

*Nothing carries the caveat today.* The review line ships =0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns= with no annotation, on the argument that juxtaposition carries the recall-cue meaning. That argument does not cover an imported reading, and it weakens as the number travels: an annotation ([[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]]) or a column ([[id:97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0][97503194]]) is read further from context, and a column heading is exactly where a productivity reading attaches.

*Candidates, none chosen:* the review buffer's key legend; the tool descriptions; a line in CLAUDE.md's session-tracking section; or nowhere, on the grounds that the only reader is the person who set the convention. The last is a real option — this project has one user — and should be rejected on evidence rather than reflex.


** TODO Turn count reads 0 for a background or subagent session, which is not no attention :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9b5eb830-8825-4d6c-b701-c167c2d71fdf
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 16:39]
:END:

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31, where it was the third of three recorded cons and the only one never answered.

*The defect.* Turn count is derived from =resume= events, one per =UserPromptSubmit=. A background session or a subagent has no human submitting prompts, so it counts *zero* — and zero already means something else on this readout: a span in which the human was present but said nothing. A bare =0 turns= cannot distinguish "not applicable" from "no attention", and the two want opposite conclusions when a human is allocating their day.

*Why it matters more since 2026-08-31.* The count is now on every suggested span line (=0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns=), so the ambiguity is in front of a reader at the moment of judgement rather than latent in a field nobody looks at.

*The data to tell them apart is already recorded.* =bin/hooks/queue-append= writes =agent_id= and =agent_type= on every event, so a span backed by subagent events is identifiable without new instrumentation — the same "a view of what already exists" argument that made the turn count itself cheap.

*Open, and the smaller half of the question:* what to render. Candidates, none chosen — omit the clause entirely for a session that cannot have turns; print =n/a= rather than =0=; or name the agent type, which says why. The parent's standing requirement applies: prefer the lighter presentation, and never present a figure that invites a reading it cannot support.

*A live instance the same day this was filed, and it widens the heading past subagents.* With the new readout loaded, a real pending item rendered:

: 0:05 span, 0:05 in 1 run, 0 turns

*One run of five minutes, zero turns* — which reads as contradictory and is not. The run comes from an explicit =clock_in=/=clock_out= pair, while turns count completed =resume= -> =pause= adjacencies; that stretch was a single long agent turn with no completed turn inside the window, so zero is literally correct.

*But it is exactly the wrong impression.* Zero turns beside five clocked minutes reads as "set running and left" when the truth was continuous agent work on one instruction — which is the metric's advertised inversion under autonomy showing up as an apparent inconsistency on the line rather than as a caveat somewhere.

*So this heading is not only about background and subagent sessions.* Any span whose window holds no completed turn reads =0 turns=, and the three causes — no human present, a subagent, and one long turn still in flight — are indistinguishable on the line while wanting different conclusions. The rendering question is therefore broader than "omit the clause for a session that cannot have turns": it has to separate *cannot have turns* from *had none complete in this window*.

*Related:* [[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]] owns the productivity caveat, and this is where the caveat becomes concrete rather than abstract — the reader does not need to import a wrong reading, the line offers one.


** NEXT Record cwd on queue events, so a span can be attributed to a project :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5461c349-fd22-4b75-a508-50efbe4b87f8
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 16:39]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-31 Mon 16:44]
:END:

Split out of [[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]] 2026-08-31, because it is a concrete one-line change sitting inside a much larger judgement and does not need to wait for it.

*What is missing.* =bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from, note, title, target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type and source — and no =cwd=. So a queue file says which *session* an event came from and not which *project*.

*The payload already carries it.* =cwd= was verified present in a captured =PermissionRequest= payload on 2026-08-14, so this is one more line in the =jq= object rather than new instrumentation — the same shape as every other field there.

*Why it is worth recording rather than deriving.* Project is recoverable indirectly, since =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes it, but that is a fragile join against a path convention this project does not own, where a recorded field is free.

*What it unblocks.* Queue files are per-session and all land in =~/.claude/org-updates/=, which is *user-global* rather than per-project — so the directory is already the right shape for cross-session comparison, and this is the field that makes the comparison meaningful. It is one of the two preconditions [[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]] names; the other is [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]], without which only this repo writes queue files at all and there is nothing to compare across.

*Do it before the allocation work, not during it.* The field is only useful on events written *after* it ships, so every day it is not recorded is a day the eventual analysis cannot see. That argues for landing it early even though nothing consumes it yet — the opposite of the usual rule against building unused machinery, and the reason is that this datum cannot be backfilled.

Sharpened 2026-09-03 by the first concurrent worktree session: a session in .claude/worktrees/pr8-review writes queue events whose cwd (once recorded) will differ from the main checkout's while every id-addressed event still resolves through Emacs against the one live tracker. So cwd is necessary but not sufficient as project identity — the consumer must normalise a worktree path to its repository (git worktree list, or the common .git), or events from a worktree would read as a different project than the tracker they target. Same session also demonstrated the fragmentation argument against moving the queue into the repo: each worktree has its own working copy, so a repo-local queue would scatter events across directories the review pass never reads, while the shared user-global dir keeps every worktree's events visible to the one apply pass — which is correct, since they all target the same tracker.


* TODO org_capture's target refuses an 8-character :ID: prefix that every other tool accepts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       25e7b083-360b-4282-8fd0-bb768e2a0f13
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Found 2026-08-31 by using it.* Filing four children under [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] with =(claude-code-ide-org-capture TITLE "9e627dc0" ...)= returned =Error: Cannot find target ID "9e627dc0"= four times. The same prefix resolves fine everywhere else — =org_body=, =org_amend=, =org_set_todo=, =org_outline='s scope — and the capture succeeded immediately when handed the full UUID.

*This is the exact defect =--id-find='s own docstring is a monument to*, recurring at a call site that predates or bypasses it. That docstring records the first version of the fix expanding prefixes inside =--at-id= only, a commit message claiming that therefore covered every tool taking an =id=, and it not being true — =claude-code-ide-org-amend= did its own =org-id-find= first. *Fourteen such call sites existed.* This is a fifteenth, in the *target* argument rather than the =id= argument, which is presumably why the sweep missed it.

*Why it matters more than the keystrokes.* The whole point of prefix expansion is that a caller never has to produce a full UUID, and this project's memory rule is to never type one from recall because nine were fabricated across two sessions with correct prefixes and wrong tails. A tool that demands the full id pushes the caller straight back into the failure mode the expansion exists to remove — and the natural workaround is to type it from memory.

*Where to look:* =claude-code-ide-org--capture-target-spec=. It appears to consult =--id-find= already, so the failure may be a fallback path or an ordering issue rather than a missing call; the error string it produced is not =--id-find='s own wording, which is the thread to pull. Worth checking =org_refile='s =target_id= and =org_divide= for the same shape while there — the twin question this project asks of any list.

*Cheap verification when fixed:* the four captures above, re-run with the prefix, plus a test asserting a prefix target resolves. Note the fixture trap found the same day: =--with-known-ids= leaves its ids unknown to org, so it is the wrong fixture for anything walking =org-id-locations=.

*A second call site, found 2026-09-01 by using it.* =claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice='s own =ID= argument compares with =equal= against the full =:ID:=:

: (equal (downcase (or (org-entry-get nil "ID") "")) (downcase id))

so =(claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice "b36e6369")= matches nothing and returns =0 slices refreshed, 0 member lines rewritten, 0 blockers updated=. *It looks exactly like a working no-op* — a plausible answer for a slice that was already up to date — which is worse than the capture case, where the reply at least said =Error: Cannot find target ID=.

*So the pattern now has three known shapes*, and the third is the dangerous one: an argument that resolves through =--id-find= (fine), one that resolves some other way and *errors* (=org_capture='s target), and one that resolves by string comparison and *silently selects nothing*. A sweep should look for the third first.

*Worth checking in the same pass:* every other function taking an =id= or =target= argument that does not route through =claude-code-ide-org--id-find=. The docstring on =--id-find= records that fourteen such sites existed the first time this was fixed; two more have now surfaced by use rather than by search, which suggests the sweep was by call *shape* rather than by argument.


* TODO A/B the clock record across the phase-3 fixes, since much of what is logged now is bogus :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:10]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 15:11]
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-31, immediately after applying a review pass:* "this tells that much of our logged activity is bogus data. we should do an A/B comparison with B starting after this slice or whatever to check for a qualitative difference."

*The evidence that prompted it, which is unusually good evidence.* The apply pass that had just been accepted contained a span on [[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]] rendered as =[13:53]--[14:04]  writes 0:01 in 1= — eleven minutes of headline interval crediting one minute — for a stretch that was *continuous* work with no idle gap and no permission block. That heading's work is fully reconstructible from four commits and a test suite that went 448 to 454, so this is not a case of guessing whether the number is wrong. It is wrong, and by roughly an order of magnitude, on a stretch we can independently account for.

The same pass carried =[13:13]--[13:48]  writes 0:14 in 3= on [[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]], which spans an Emacs crash and an AFK stretch and so is wrong in the *other* direction. Both were applied before the discrepancy was noticed, so the file now contains them.

*Why this is not simply [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] or [[id:98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d][98c302e0]].* Those two are about the review buffer *saying* what it means — a headline interval that is not the interval written, and a readout whose two numbers cannot be told apart. Fixing them makes the human able to *see* the discrepancy at review time. Neither claims the underlying attribution is right, and this heading is the question of whether it is. A perfectly legible rendering of a bogus number is still a bogus number.

*The design question, and it is the whole task:* what is the B in an A/B where the thing being measured is accuracy, and there is no ground truth? Some candidates, none settled:

- *Reconstructible stretches as the yardstick.* Work with commits, test-count deltas and a debrief can be bounded from outside the clock system entirely. Both spans above were caught precisely because such a record existed. That suggests sampling stretches with strong external evidence rather than comparing aggregates.
- *Turns per interval*, which [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] proposes recording anyway, is an independent signal: a span crediting one minute across many turns is self-evidently suspect without needing to know the true number.
- *Aggregate shape* — total logged hours per day before and after — is the easiest comparison and the weakest, since a change in total does not say which direction is right.

*A boundary marker exists, so B has a defined start.* A ends at commit =b34a12f= (2026-08-31T14:05:07-05:00), the last apply before this was raised. Recording it here because "after this slice" is not measurable later and a commit is.

*One caution worth stating now, while it is cheap.* If A really is largely bogus, that is a claim about data already written to =TODO.org= and =DONE.org=, and the tempting follow-up — correcting or discarding it — should not be reached for casually. The record's value is partly that it is what actually happened, mistakes included; [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is this project's precedent that a plausible fabricated number is worse than an absent one, and a retroactively "corrected" span is exactly that. Deciding what, if anything, to do about A is part of this heading and should not be assumed.

*Measured 2026-09-01, in answer to whether this and [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] complement each other. They share a corpus and a histogram; they do not share a design, and the measurement argues against the before/after framing.*

*B is n=19.* Splitting every queue file at =b34a12f= gives *849* =pause= -> =resume= gaps before and *19* after — one session. No comparison is available now and none will be for weeks.

*The bigger problem is a confound, and it was found by testing the obvious alternative rather than assuming it.* The August band (no gap between 1061s and 2070s) has closed, and the tempting explanation is undersampling: a 150-gap histogram is sparse, so holes appear by chance. *That is refuted.* Drawing 200 random 150-gap samples from the current corpus, *zero* produced an empty band. So the August distribution was genuinely different, not merely thinner.

*Which means the gap distribution drifts substantially month to month — and all of that drift is inside regime A*, since 849 of the 868 gaps predate the boundary. A before/after comparison on a calendar boundary therefore cannot attribute a difference to the code: *when* and *what changed* are entangled, and the =when= term is demonstrably large.

*And there is a second, independent reason to expect no signal.* Almost everything the slice shipped was *presentational*. [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] says so of itself — the fix "is presentational and deliberately does not touch what is extracted" — and the same is true of the readout rework, the wording audit, the prefix change and the marking behaviour. The extraction that produces recorded minutes is unchanged. The only causal path from the slice to the *numbers* runs through the human deciding differently at review, which is slow, mediated, and not separable from drift.

*So the before/after framing should be dropped, and this body already contains the better instrument.* Its own candidate list opens with /"reconstructible stretches as the yardstick"/ — work bounded from outside the clock system by commits, test-count deltas and a debrief. That needs no regime boundary, is immune to drift, and answers the question actually asked: not "did the numbers change" but "were they ever right". The two spans that prompted this heading were caught exactly that way.

*What [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] still contributes*, and it is real: the same histogram feeds both, and the September occurrence has to re-derive it anyway. The overlap is the *scan*, not the *comparison*.

*Checked rather than asserted, 2026-09-01, after the claim "nothing changed how agent time is recorded" was questioned. It was too strong, and the correction sharpens the conclusion rather than softening it.*

*The computation is byte-identical across the slice.* Every function turning guideposts into a duration is unchanged against =main=: =--span-work-runs=, =--apply-idle-floor=, =--aggregate-guideposts=, =--span-turn-seconds=, =--review-apply-clock=, =--format-clock-line=, =--clock-minutes=, =--subtract-intervals=.

*Two things do change what lands, though:*

- =--review-apply-item= gained the =inhibit-read-only= binding, so writes that previously *failed* now succeed. Not hypothetical — an apply on 2026-08-25 reported =Applied 0 item(s); 1 failed= for exactly this reason, and the ceremony hit it again on 2026-09-01.
- *Auto-marking* ([[id:b6e229c7-3035-4c0e-943b-710a12bfd7d1][b6e229c7]]) means items arrive pre-marked, so one =x= applies more per pass — including items a human would previously have missed or never marked at all.

\(=--review-items-from-queue= also changed, for the stranded-span drop, but those items write nothing by definition.)

*Both push B toward more recorded time, which is precisely the wrong direction for this experiment.* More recorded minutes is not more accurate minutes. A B-over-A increase in totals would be fully explained by fewer failed writes and more items applied, with no bearing on whether any individual figure was ever right — so the one comparison a before/after design makes easy is the one that cannot be interpreted.

*Same conclusion by a second route:* the question is "were these minutes real", and only an instrument outside the clock system answers it.


* TODO An Emacs outage strands every org write, because the tools are Emacs :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3ad389be-2797-46c3-a51d-9f603053955a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 13:50]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 13:52]
:END:

The queue's premise is that a session can record work without a running Emacs: =bin/hooks/queue-append= is plain shell and jq, and CLAUDE.md says so outright — "this script must stay runnable with no Emacs at all, which is the premise the whole queue rests on."

*That premise holds for hooks and fails for tools.* =org_set_todo=, =org_clock_out= and =org_amend= do not write the queue directly; they are served by the MCP server running *inside* Emacs, so when Emacs is gone they time out and record nothing. Meanwhile the guidepost stream keeps flowing, because hooks are shell — which makes a total failure look partial: =org_pending_updates= still reports events accumulating while nothing a session actually *decides* can be written.

*Measured 2026-08-31.* Emacs wedged at ~13:27. The GUI thread took a SIGSEGV inside =NSAutoFillHeuristicController= calling =-[EmacsMainView selectedRange]=, which recursed into itself; the Lisp thread then blocked in =mac_select= waiting on a thread already parked in =deliver_fatal_thread_signal=. Neither could proceed, so =debug-on-event= was no help either — it needs the Lisp thread to reach a safe point, and the Lisp thread was the one waiting. Symptom was a beachball at 99% CPU and =emacsclient= getting *connection refused* from a live process. Two finished headings could not be closed and two debriefs could not be written for roughly twenty minutes.

*What it actually cost, stated honestly:* nothing was lost. The debriefs went to a session scratchpad and were written to org once Emacs came back. But they survived because a human was present to be told — and a scratchpad file is exactly the "path to becoming invisible" CLAUDE.md warns about for residue named only in prose.

*The uncomfortable part, recorded rather than acted on.* Four hours earlier this same session removed =QUEUE_APPEND_ID/STATE/FROM= from =queue-append= ([[id:46d225c7-7541-4867-af72-929a572df2a5][46d225c7]]) — the one mechanism in the repo that could write a todo or clock event for a named heading with no Emacs involved. That removal was correct on its own terms: its consumer had been dead since the =PLANNING= retirement, and its own tests proved the branch was live only in the sense of being covered. But the use case that would have wanted it did not exist when it was written, and does now.

*This heading is a question, not a design.* Restoring the overrides is the obvious move and probably the wrong one: a synthetic event has no =tool_response= to recover =from= from, so the staleness check =recovered_from= feeds would go blind on exactly the events written when the system is least healthy. Worth deciding among at least:

1. Tools fail *loudly and durably* rather than silently — a timed-out MCP write leaves a marker that the next =SessionStart= reports, so the record of what could not be recorded is itself in the system. This is the option that composes with the existing recovery report rather than adding a second path.
2. A narrow, non-hook CLI writer for todo/clock on a known =:ID:=, with =from= honestly null rather than fabricated.
3. Nothing, on the grounds that an Emacs crash is rare and a human was present. This is a real option and may well be the right one — today's cost was two debriefs delayed, not lost.

Whatever is chosen, the shape to avoid is the current one, where the failure is silent per call and only a human noticing a timeout keeps the work attached to its heading.

*Related but distinct:* [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]] is about =org_capture= and =org_set_property= hanging over MCP *while Emacs is healthy and other tools answer*. This one is about every tool failing because Emacs is not there at all. They were confirmed separate on 2026-08-31: after the restart, =org_capture= over MCP timed out again and persisted nothing — buffer unmodified, not read-only, no prompt open, =recursion-depth= 0 — while the same function through =emacsclient= completed in under a second.


* TODO Nothing watches a REVIEW heading, and it is the state most easily forgotten :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       43aa25b2-1e21-4117-bf15-b92f3081b60d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:35]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:35]
:END:

=REVIEW= means finished work handed back for human judgement. Nothing in this project surfaces one, and it is structurally the state most likely to be forgotten: nothing is owed from our side, no clock runs, and no next action is missing -- so every existing nag looks straight past it.

*Measured 2026-08-31, on the day the corpus got its first.* The three =SessionStart= reports cover stale open intervals, the daily ceremony, and containers whose live members include no =NEXT=. A top-level =REVIEW= heading trips none of them. Before today the corpus held *zero* live =REVIEW= headings, which is why the gap has never been felt -- the keyword has been in use since 2026-08-21 and every heading that wore it passed straight through.

*What made it visible.* [[id:29734f79-e698-43dd-abf8-14bc86b8b0ae][29734f79]] went to =REVIEW= because its code shipped but its trigger has never fired on its own, and the only thing that closes it is a human noticing whether it does. Asked what would watch it, the answer turned out to be *slice membership and nothing else* -- and that is incidental, since a heading only lands in a slice if someone puts it there. It was added to [[id:b36e6369-a210-4540-adc7-0c0c188e9791][b36e6369]] for exactly that reason.

*The obvious shape*, if it survives scrutiny: a fourth =SessionStart= line naming =REVIEW= headings and how long they have sat, on the same contract as the others -- state the fact, ask, propose nothing. Note the existing reports all answer "something is owed"; this one answers "something is finished and unjudged", which is a different question and may deserve different wording rather than a fourth bullet in the same breath.

Worth deciding at the same time: whether =REVIEW= aging past some span is itself worth saying, since the keyword is still marked experimental ([[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]) and this is the first evidence about how it behaves in practice.


* TODO Adding a just-captured heading to a slice cannot be made lint-clean :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2d2211d5-97c3-4f69-8175-40df90d1db8a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:END:

Adding a heading to a slice in the same session that captured it cannot be made lint-clean, and the workflow that would avoid it was never chosen.

*Both lint rules are right, which is why this is not a lint bug.* Naming a keyword-less heading in a slice's =:BLOCKER:= is an error, because =org-depend= acts only on an unfinished TODO keyword. Omitting an unfinished member from that =:BLOCKER:= is also an error. A heading captured this session is keyword-less on disk until a human applies the queue, so both fire at once and =.githooks/pre-commit= refuses the commit.

*This is the residue of a closed heading.* [[id:798bb7a1-d5a6-4c08-8cf0-cc91a82d08e6][798bb7a1]] closed 2026-08-27 as "refuse loudly on both surfaces" and listed candidate answers with *none chosen*, the first being: capture, apply, then add to the slice. It now sits in DONE.org, which readers are told to treat as history -- so its open question is on the path to becoming invisible, which is why this is a heading rather than a sentence.

Hit again 2026-08-31 while composing, costing a full revert and a wait on the human. Twice is enough to pick one.

The cheapest candidate is procedural and costs nothing to write: state in the slice convention (=.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=) that a member is applied before it is added. The alternative is mechanical -- let a slice carry a member it cannot yet block on, and have the lint say so as a warning rather than an error -- which is more work and weakens a rule that is otherwise exactly right.


* TODO An archive pass wrote one subtree twice, and the mechanism is still unknown :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       381ea07d-633f-4640-abbb-89472a07acba
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:END:

On 2026-08-31 a live archive pass reported 65 and wrote 66. One story and its six children landed twice in DONE.org, ids and all. It is guarded but not understood.

*The arithmetic says where it is not.* =n= increments once per =org-archive-subtree= call and the pass reported 65, so exactly 65 calls ran and one pasted its subtree twice -- inside the call, on org's own paste path. Double-consumption of a marker would have reported 66. The heading it duplicated was also the last in the file and therefore the *first* consumed under reverse-order iteration, so no earlier deletion existed to disturb its marker. The marker-collapse explanation is excluded.

*It did not reproduce.* Same file, same code, fresh buffer: 65 archived, no duplicates. So it is a property of the live buffer's state rather than of the data. That buffer had been reverted, had its read-only flag cleared, and had just been rewritten by drawer consolidation and separation normalisation in the same ceremony.

*What stands in for a fix.* =claude-code-ide-org-archive-finished= now checks the result rather than the method: after a sweep, no =:ID:= may appear twice in the archive target, or it signals -- which the ceremony turns into a held repeater and a reported failure. Every other signal looked right at the time, the count and the levels included; only counting ids found it.

Worth attempting first: re-run the sweep against a buffer put through the same sequence -- revert, consolidate, normalise, then archive -- to see whether the combination is what does it.


* TODO [0/2] Make an archived day hold that day's record :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f9fe9fac-a423-4ede-bc42-1d201c2c3d82
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:07]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:KIND:     slice
:COOKIE_DATA: checkbox recursive
:BLOCKER:  ids(d6fc47a5-b84d-4287-b973-aa5b37410270 591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b)
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:07]
:END:

Seeded 2026-08-31 from the proposal reviewed in conversation: DONE.org's datetree should gather what happened on a day, not only what was filed. Both members move *records* into it rather than changing what a task is.

*Its work is blocked from outside this slice.* [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] builds the datetree and belongs to [[id:b36e6369-a210-4540-adc7-0c0c188e9791][b36e6369]], so nothing here starts until that lands. That is precisely why these two were kept *out* of b36e6369 rather than added to it: including them would have left a 27-member slice unable to close until its own downstream consumers were done too.

*Use org's own datetree machinery, and note which entry point.* =org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= takes a comparison function per level and =org-datetree-comparefun-from-regex= builds one; negating its result gives the descending order [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] asks for. Measured in batch 2026-08-31 -- entries inserted 08-10, 08-31, 07-05, 08-20 landed in strict descending order at year, month and day. Only =org-datetree-find-date-create= hard-codes ascending, which is what made a hand-rolled builder look necessary at first.

*What a day node means, and does not.* It holds what org would allocate to that date. A task's *earlier* clock intervals stay where they were recorded, so a day node is not a substitute for a clocktable when the question is how much time went where.

- [ ] [[id:591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b][591e7af3]] TODO Bound Review attention's LOGBOOK by refiling old entries into dated clones


* TODO Bound Review attention's LOGBOOK by refiling old entries into dated clones :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 11:21]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 11:22]
:END:

Keep =* Review attention= ([[id:335dfa97-c1b0-4e98-804f-81495eb63f20][335dfa97]]) exactly as it is, and stop its =:LOGBOOK:= growing without bound by *refiling old entries into clones*. For each day at or before yesterday, clone the heading into that day's node in DONE.org's datetree and move that day's CLOCK lines into the clone. The clones are archival records, not tasks, and carry no =:ID:=.

*What this deliberately does not do, since an earlier reading of the proposal got it backwards.* It does not restructure the live heading into day nodes. So the clock target is unchanged: =claude-code-ide-org-review-attention-heading= still resolves to this heading by title, =--review-attention-start= still opens the clock on it, and neither needs touching. =--ceremony-reviewed-today-p= is likewise unaffected, because it looks for a CLOCK line dated *today* and only previous-day-or-older lines ever move.

*The cost, which is the trade and should be written down rather than discovered.* A clocktable scoped to this heading stops seeing the moved intervals -- they live in the clones from then on. Any report of review attention *over time* has to sum the clones too. That is the price of a bounded =:LOGBOOK:=, and it is worth paying, but a report built later against this heading alone will quietly under-count.

*Two things to check before writing it.* Clones with no =:ID:= and no =:CREATED:= are a deliberate exception to the every-heading rule, so confirm =bin/lint-org= does not demand either at that depth. And splitting a =:LOGBOOK:= by day is the highest-risk edit shape in this repo's history -- two body corruptions on record from hand-rolled region edits -- so it wants a tested function rather than a step inside a sweep.

Idempotence is the same requirement as the sibling heading: a re-run must merge into the day's existing clone, never append a second.


* TODO Distribute Review and planning's past day nodes into DONE.org's datetree :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d6fc47a5-b84d-4287-b973-aa5b37410270
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 11:21]
:CATEGORY: Meta

:END:
