- [2026-08-14 Fri 08:49]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:59] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 09:02]--[2026-08-14 Fri 09:04] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-14 Fri 09:02]--[2026-08-14 Fri 09:04] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:
:PLAN:

*Noticed 2026-08-13.* =WAIT= had quietly acquired a second, narrower
meaning here — the resting state at the end of a Claude work increment,
"the ball is in the human's court" — which is not what the =#+TODO:=
table says it means. The proposal was to read a =DOING -> WAIT=
transition as a *bracket*: a hard right edge after which guideposts
provably do not belong to that heading, killing the span guess's known
latching defect. It needed no new event kind — =todo= events already
carry state and timestamp whether or not anyone has applied them.

Two caveats came with it. The keyword is ambiguous: a =WAIT= meaning
"waiting on a vendor to email back" carries none of this, and the real
discriminator is probably the event's =source=/=session_id=. And the
bracket is only as reliable as its consistency — a session that ends
leaving a heading =DOING= emits nothing at all.

Composes with [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]]:
commits and =:CREATED:= stamps inside a window are the evidence,
brackets are the bounds on which windows are worth looking at. A
cheaper, mechanical partial answer to
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]], which proposes
asking Claude to narrate the same gaps; and it bounds the damage from
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]] and
[[id:09c134c4-31eb-4f6c-aa10-f836d1d28f16][09c134c4]], since a bracket
constrains what a stray guidepost can be attributed to even when the
guidepost itself is wrong.

*Provenance:* the pattern came from the user's own correction, not from
design — two instances, one person, one afternoon. An observed habit
worth testing, not a measured regularity.

:END:
*Twice overtaken by the code, and only one thing is left.*

*First, 2026-08-14.* The claim above that "nothing ever unsets it" was
wrong, and was already wrong when this heading was written.
=claude-code-ide-org--review-suggest-heading= already cleared the active
heading on every keyword except =DOING= and =PLANNING=, and had done
since =3c6b3f2= on 2026-08-13 — *hours before* this heading was
captured, with the latching-guess comment sitting directly above it.
Found by grep while inventorying keyword touch points; it was never
verified when the heading was written, which is the actual lesson.

*Second, and it closes one of the two survivors that correction left.*
The "denylist should be an allowlist" half shipped in =6a81822=
("Bracket the span guess on any keyword that is not work in progress").
The six named keywords are gone;
=claude-code-ide-org--work-in-progress-keywords= is =("DOING")= and
every other keyword brackets by construction — so adding =REVIEW=
needed no edit here, and neither did retiring =PLANNING=. Confirmed by
reading =config.el= on 2026-09-01.

*What is left is one thing, and it is data rather than logic.* The
bracket fires only when a =todo= event exists to fire it. A session that
ends leaving a heading =DOING= emits nothing, so the guess still latches
across exactly that case. Whether that is answered by a lint
([[id:3bd3402b-3ab6-4ace-b20b-3ff84caa3fb2][3bd3402b]]), a =Stop=-hook
transition, or a bracket inferred at review time is undecided.

The =WAIT= framing in the title is vestigial — the keyword was never the
load-bearing part — and the title should be rewritten if this is picked
up.


* TODO Re-measure the guidepost gap threshold against a month of queue data :code:research:
SCHEDULED: <2026-09-13 Sun +1m>
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 12:20]
:END:

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
on 2026-08-13.* It was filed there because the threshold it re-measures
belongs to that epic's queue — but it carries a =+1m= repeater and so
never reaches =DONE=, which meant the epic could not either, and
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] is blocked on that
epic. A recurring measurement has no terminal state and therefore no
completable parent.
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
  scheduled re-measure, one month out
:END:

Scheduled rather than left to memory: the finding below is real but
thin, and the failure mode is adopting a four-day number as permanent
because nobody re-ran it.

*The survey, 2026-08-13.* 422 queue events, 2026-08-10 to 08-13, across
six sessions. Measured the *queue* rather than =:LOGBOOK:= deliberately:
=:LOGBOOK:= intervals are reconstructions produced by the very threshold
under test, so measuring them would partly measure the parameter.

Decomposed each session's stream into the two gaps that mean different
things:

- =resume= -> =pause= — how long the agent worked. n=111, median *81s*,
  p90 *211s*, one outlier over an hour (the concurrent subagent run).
- =pause= -> =resume= — how long the human took to reply. n=150, median
  *181s*.

*The result, and it is sharper than expected: the second is bimodal with
an empty band between the modes.* Sorted gaps run =… 949, 971, 1061=
and then jump straight to =2070, 2103, 2135 …=. *No gap anywhere in the
corpus falls between 1061s and 2070s.* A threshold sweep sees the same
hole from the other side: span count falls steeply, then sits flat at 29
across 1200-1800s, then resumes falling. Every value inside the band
produces an identical reconstruction.

Acted on immediately: =claude-code-ide-org-guidepost-gap-threshold= 900
-> *1200s*, the round number inside the band, at the start of the
plateau with margin either side. 900 sat just below the band and split
five spans nothing justifies splitting.

*A stated assumption the data contradicted, which is the more useful
half.* The survey was proposed on the premise that silence from ~18:00
to ~08:00 is almost certainly AFK. True, but not where the long gaps
are: of 19 gaps over 2000s, *11 start inside working hours* — 07:00,
10:00, 12:00, 14:00, 15:00, 16:00, 17:00 — including the two longest
(151 and 158 minutes, starting 12:59 and 15:34). Daytime absence is
indistinguishable from nighttime absence. Gap *length* separates the
modes cleanly; clock hour does not. Recorded against
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]], whose
working-hours guess rests on exactly the premise this refutes.

*What to re-run on the scheduled date*, and the reason the number is not
yet settled: n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually
intense stretch of work on this very project. The band may be an artifact
of that. Re-derive the histogram and the sweep; if the band has moved or
closed, retune again and say so here rather than defending 1200.

*This is a moving target, not a one-off calibration* — the user's own
framing, 2026-08-13, and it changes what the re-measure is for.
Proficiency, setup and work objectives all evolve, and the habits
generating these gaps evolve with them. A faster operator, a different
mix of long-running subagent work, or a stretch of shallow triage rather
than deep implementation would each shift the modes and could close the
band entirely. So the finding above describes *August 2026*, not this
project. The scheduled task is not "confirm 1200 was right"; it is
"re-derive, and expect a different answer."

*Made a =+1m= repeater at the user's instruction, 2026-08-13*, which is
the honest encoding of "moving target": a one-shot date would have
implied the number gets settled once. It does not. Each occurrence
re-derives the histogram and the sweep against whatever the preceding
month's habits actually were, and either confirms the current value or
moves it.

Practical note for whoever handles an occurrence: marking it =DONE=
rolls the =SCHEDULED= stamp forward a month and returns the heading to
=TODO= — org's own repeater behavior — so the record of *that* month's
finding has to be written into this body before closing it, or it is
gone. Append; do not overwrite the previous month's numbers.

Charts from the original survey are an artifact, not committed here —
regenerate rather than trusting a stale rendering.

*Retarget before the September run — this is now pointed at the wrong
knob.* Since 2026-08-18 (=226ed53b= step 2(b)) the gap threshold governs
only *grouping and display*: it decides how many items a review buffer
shows and how wide each reads, and moves no recorded minute. Its
docstring says so. Re-measuring it on a month of data would answer a
question whose answer changes nothing.

What now decides recorded duration is the pair of floors:

- =claude-code-ide-org-span-idle-floor= (120s) — how much idle is
  absorbed into a run.
- =claude-code-ide-org-span-minimum-interval= — the floor on what gets
  written, named 2026-08-20 by
  [[id:e0c55d26-706c-42b6-9405-54e9d5ed4750][e0c55d26]]. It is 0 today,
  meaning the two rendering conditions alone; its docstring carries the
  sweep this review needs. *Do not cite a stored number.* 120s measured
  at 6.5% of recorded time on 2026-08-18 and at 3.03% two days later on
  a grown corpus — the estimate halved without the code changing.

Both rest on eight days of data. This heading's own body already names
that failure: /"adopting a four-day number as permanent because nobody
re-ran it."/ Same trap, one knob over.

Keep the =+1m= repeater and the parentless placement — the reasoning
recorded above still holds, and applies to the floors identically.

*Interim observation 2026-09-01, twelve days before the scheduled occurrence and deliberately not one.* Prompted by the user asking whether the 1200s figure had drifted. It has, and the headline is the one this body said to look for.

*The empty band has closed.* The August survey's central finding was that no gap anywhere in the corpus fell between *1061s and 2070s* — a hole that made every value inside it produce an identical reconstruction, which is what justified picking 1200. Against the corpus as it stands there are *43 gaps inside that band*.

*The corpus is now large enough that this is not sampling noise.*

| | Aug 2026 survey | 2026-09-01 |
|--------------------------+-----------------+------------|
| distinct days | 4 | *22* (2026-08-07 .. 09-01) |
| queue events | 422 | *2735* |
| =pause= -> =resume= gaps | 150 | *867* |
| =resume= -> =pause= runs | 111 | *886* |
| gap median | 181s | *232s* |

So the band was an artifact of four days, exactly as this body allowed for — /"n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually intense stretch of work on this very project. The band may be an artifact of that."/ It was. The prediction and the refutation are both recorded here, which is the value of having written the caveat down.

*What this does and does not change.* 1200s is no longer a round number inside a hole; it is an arbitrary point on a continuum. But since =226ed53b= the gap threshold governs only *grouping and display*, so an arbitrary value there costs legibility rather than accuracy — which is why the retarget note above still stands and the floors remain the live question.

*Explicitly not the scheduled run.* This is a histogram check on one statistic. It is not the sweep: no span-count-versus-threshold curve, no re-derivation for =span-idle-floor=, no percentage-of-recorded-time figure for the floors. The occurrence on =<2026-09-13 Sun>= should still do all of that, and should now expect to *move* 1200 rather than confirm it.

*A third question the September run should absorb, from the user's framing 2026-09-01.* The floors are tuned to keep the human's idle *out* of the agent's clock, which is right for per-heading attribution and backwards for the ratio of human time to agent time that the user is actually curious about ([[id:20cecd53-2338-4237-ab06-f8129e10a2c8][20cecd53]]). Measured on one session that day: of ~90 minutes of plausible at-keyboard gap time, 3 minutes were credited to the agent and 87 recorded nowhere. The same histogram answers both questions; only the threshold's *purpose* differs. Re-deriving without asking what the gaps say about the human would waste the pass.

*A second question for the September occurrence, added by the user 2026-09-04.* Alongside re-deriving
the threshold: **assess whether a clear class of spans emerges that should be marked _active_ —
angle-bracketed — at review time**, meaning the interval was the human's attention rather than the
agent's.

That is the automatic half of =01849bef=, which today gives the review buffer no key for the flip and
leaves it as four bracket characters edited by hand. The distinction is not cosmetic: =b8e6007a=
settled that the queue records *agent* activity and the agenda must not absorb it, so an active
timestamp is an assertion about *whose* time an interval was, and it is the only kind the agenda
should show.

**Do not expect the class to be found by a rule.** =409a69ff= now records the user's own account of
inconsistent =AFK= discipline — popping in mid-turn to queue a prompt, then out again without
re-declaring — which makes a marker land in the middle of an absence as often as at its edge. So the
question this occurrence answers is empirical and narrower than "what is the heuristic": *given a
month of spans, is there a subset whose evidence is unambiguous enough to mark without asking?* A
negative answer is a real result and should be recorded as one, since it retires the search rather
than leaving it open indefinitely.

The corpus for it is already larger than the threshold work needs: the queue, the transcripts, and
the =AFK= interjections that =e75d660f= says are ground truth and are currently discarded.

CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 17:57]--[2026-08-13 Thu 18:09] =>  0:12
- State "DOING"      from "WAIT"       [2026-08-13 Thu 17:57] \\
  go-ahead given, implementing the plan
- [2026-08-13 Thu 17:57]--[2026-08-13 Thu 18:09] implementing capture/amend queue extension
- State "WAIT"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-13 Thu 18:09] \\
  v1 built, awaiting your review
- State "TODO"       from "WAIT"       [2026-08-14 Fri 11:56]
:END:
:PLAN:

*The ask, in the user's words:* so many times they want to ask a question
or make a proposal while something else is in flight, which often leads
to something new to capture or amend. The literal proposal was a file or
Emacs buffer lock/semaphore on =TODO.org= and friends, causing
concurrent sessions to wait their turn while another holds it.

*What actually collides.* The queue already removed most of the
contention: =todo= and clock events are append-only, per-session,
written blind with =O_APPEND=, so two sessions cannot corrupt each other
there by construction. What remains is *direct writes to the file* —
=org_capture=, =org_refile=, =org_archive=, =org_move_sibling= and
=org_sort_children=, which all =save-buffer= immediately; the org
skill's prose edits via Claude Code's =Edit= tool, which bypass Emacs
entirely; the user's own hand-edits in the live buffer; and =.git/index=,
a separate incident owned by
[[id:2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c][2aeb65d6]]. Not hypothetical:
on 2026-08-13 two edits in one session hit "the file had been modified
on disk since you last read it". The ad-hoc mitigation in use — checking
=buffer-modified-p= first — is a hand-rolled version of what this
heading proposes.

*Why a blocking lock looks like the wrong primitive,* recorded as the
argument against because "why not just take a lock" is the obvious
question a future reader will ask.

1. *Waiting reintroduces the failure this architecture exists to avoid.*
   A session blocked on a lock burns wall-clock with no way to report
   why, and waits forever if the holder crashed — the same shape as the
   non-interactive =emacsclient= hang that pushed this project to
   append-only.
2. *The contention is asymmetric.* The human is the authoritative
   writer; Claude sessions should yield to them and never the reverse. A
   symmetric semaphore models the wrong relationship.
3. *It cannot be enforced where it matters most.* The =Edit= tool
   consults neither Emacs nor any lockfile, so any lock is advisory for
   exactly the writer most likely to be mid-flight. A mechanism that
   looks like mutual exclusion but is not is worse than an honest
   advisory one.
4. *Stale locks are stale clocks again* — an owner, a TTL and a recovery
   path, rebuilt for a new resource.

*The reframe this rests on, and the part most worth challenging.* The
ask may not be mutual exclusion at all. Note where the user's own
sentence ends: the interruption *leads to something new to capture or
amend*. That is a missing queue, not a missing lock — state changes
already work that way; capture and amend did not. If the reframe is
wrong — if the real need is genuinely "stop the other writer" rather
than "do not lose my thought" — then the recommendation below is wrong
too, and a fail-fast lock alone is the answer.

*Proposed, cheapest first.*

1. *Turn on =create-lockfiles=.* Measured 2026-08-13: it is =nil=,
   Doom's default, so Emacs's own mechanism is off entirely. One line.
   It will not stop the =Edit= tool, but it makes Emacs-mediated
   collisions visible rather than silent. Checked before proposing
   anything bespoke, per
   [[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]].

   *Why Doom disables it, checked 2026-08-13 at the user's request.*
   =lisp/doom-emacs.el:462= sets it under a =Backup & autosave files=
   heading, sharing one comment with =make-backup-files=: "Don't
   generate backups or lockfiles. … This is better suited to version
   control, and I don't want world-readable copies of potentially
   sensitive material floating around our filesystem."

   That rationale is about *backups* and does not describe a lock file.
   Per the Emacs Lisp manual a lock file is a symlink named =.#FILE=
   whose *target* is the string =user@host.pid:boot=; it holds no
   contents of the edited file at all. So the "copies of sensitive
   material" argument does not apply to the thing it justifies —
   lockfiles were bundled in with backups under one heading and
   inherited a comment written for backups. A residual privacy point
   survives (the target leaks username, host and PID), but it is a much
   smaller claim.

   The real-world objection in the wider community is *tooling*, not
   privacy: =.#FILE= is a dangling symlink and file watchers choke on it
   (e.g. =ember-cli= issue #6527). Low risk here — nothing watches this
   tree — but it is the reason to check before flipping it globally
   rather than per-directory.

   *And one point actively in favour, which is why this step is listed
   at all:* on detecting a lock, Emacs calls =ask-user-about-lock=,
   which offers grab / ignore / abort. It *asks*; it does not wait —
   precisely the fail-fast shape argued for in step 2, already
   implemented upstream.
2. *A fail-fast advisory lock in the MCP write tools* — a refusal, not a
   wait: "another session is writing TODO.org; queued instead." Fast
   failure is debuggable and cannot deadlock.
3. *Extend the queue to capture/amend proposals.* The interjection
   becomes an event, nothing waits, and the existing human review pass
   applies it — which also makes it reviewable rather than immediate,
   the property already chosen deliberately for state changes.

Steps 2 and 3 overlap: if 3 lands, 2 may be unnecessary, since there
would be nothing left that needs to write immediately.

:END:
*Provenance and standing, which are not design.*

*Promoted out of
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on 2026-08-13.* It
arose from that epic's work but is not required for it to complete: the
queue can be finished and correct without ever gaining capture/amend
events. It also has its own plan file, its own scope decisions and its
own deferred phases — *a child with a plan file of its own is usually an
epic wearing a child's clothes.* Left as a level-2 task rather than
declared an epic, since it has no children yet; it will become one
naturally if capture and amend split.

[[file:~/.claude/plans/capture-amend-queue.md][Plan]]

*The plan lives in its own file rather than this conversation's
=hazy-moseying-sky.md=:* that path is already the permanent link for
[[id:6b1e73c4-25da-4f0e-8a51-9c0d3f7ab214][6b1e73c4]] and
[[id:f290161c-f9e7-4bfb-9adf-4c5630d4c5ae][f290161c]], and Claude Code
reuses one plan file per conversation, so planning here overwrote it.
Restored from =plans/= and the new plan given its own name, so both
older links still resolve to the plan they were written for.

*Plan approved 2026-08-13, scoped by two decisions the user made during
planning:* v1 covers *capture and amend* only — the structural writers
stay immediate — and capture *writes through when the file is free*,
queueing only when it is busy, so today's immediacy survives and only
contended writes defer. That is the user's original lock idea made
non-blocking, and it supersedes the "always queue" reading of step 3.

*Read by the user 2026-08-13* — both the original draft and the
=create-lockfiles= research. *No verdict was recorded, and none should
be inferred.* They said only that they had reviewed it and that the
heading could be amended accordingly; they did not approve the
recommendation, reject it, or choose between the three steps. So the
three load-bearing claims are still open to challenge: that a *blocking*
lock is the wrong primitive, that the underlying need is a missing queue
rather than a missing lock, and that the ordering is the right one.

*What shipped, checked in the code 2026-09-01.*

- *Step 3 is built, for both capture and amend.* =config.el= defines the
  four reply constants =--reply-captured=, =--reply-queued-capture=,
  =--reply-amended= and =--reply-queued-amend=, and =bin/hooks/queue-append=
  gates on exactly two of them, appending only when the tool deferred.
  That is the write-through-when-free design above, in force.
- *Step 2 was therefore never needed,* as this heading predicted: with
  capture and amend deferring, nothing is left that must write
  immediately and also contends.
- *Step 1 never shipped.* =create-lockfiles= is set nowhere in
  =~/.config/doom/config.el=, so it is still Doom's =nil= — unchanged
  since the 2026-08-13 measurement, and the research above is still
  unspent. It is independent of the other two and remains the cheapest
  thing here.
- The =7771fc63= reference in the stale-lock argument said that heading
  was "currently deciding whether to retire"; it decided, and is DONE.

So the recommendation this heading drafted was carried out without a
verdict ever being recorded — which is worth noticing rather than
tidying away, since the paragraph above says explicitly that none should
be inferred. What is left of the heading is step 1 and the standing
invitation to challenge the reframe.


* TODO Concurrent sessions share one git index, and explicit staging protects nothing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 16:20]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 16:22] \\
  user is blocked on this: no second session until a mechanism exists
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-17 Mon 12:11] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:95087d8f-e16d-45d3-bdea-9b179dc672b4][Lint for headings that are valid org but structurally unintended]] becoming NEXT.
:END:
:PLAN:

*This one actually happened, deliberately, as a resilience test.* On
2026-08-12 two sessions ran against this same checkout. This session
wrote a heading to TODO.org, staged it with an explicit pathspec (=git
add -- TODO.org=), and went to commit. The commit failed with =nothing
to commit, working tree clean=: the other session had already committed,
and its pathspec-less =git commit= took the whole index, sweeping this
session's 69 staged lines into =a8dc693=, a commit whose message
describes unrelated work and names two unrelated heading =:ID:=s.
Content survived; attribution did not. =36410e2= is an empty commit
recording that, since =a8dc693= was already pushed.

*The mechanism is the index, not the files.* Every convention this
project had was about file contents — check for a modified buffer, stage
explicitly, never =git add -A=. None touch the actual shared mutable
state, which is =.git/index=: one per checkout, not one per session.
Worse, following the convention *increased* the damage — staging early
loaded this session's work into the index *before* the other session
committed. Had this session not staged at all, its work would have been
left alone.

*Worktrees do not fix this; they fix everything around it.*
[[id:2e09adb7-6e29-499c-a40e-98cd2b327cff][2e09adb7]] concludes that one
checkout and branch per session handles the *code* side, and that only
the org files need to stay a single canonical copy in the main checkout.
Read against this incident the two halves collide: the file swept up
*was* TODO.org, which by that heading's own =:ID:=-uniqueness and
clock-fragmentation argument is exactly the file that cannot be
partitioned into a worktree. So a worktree session still commits
TODO.org through the main checkout's index and hits this identically.
New information for 2e09adb7, which assumed the code side was the whole
problem.

*Related but distinct:*
[[id:3cb3f955-f10a-47cd-84ab-e629d73ea59d][3cb3f955]] asks whether =git
commit -- TODO.org= is safe, scoped to a *single* session not sweeping
up its own unrelated hunks in one file. This is the cross-session case:
another session's *staged* work, in any file. Same command, different
failure. Worth answering together.

*One mitigation is verified.* Tested 2026-08-12 in a scratch repo: with
=A.txt= staged, =git commit -- B.txt= commits B only, leaves A staged,
and does not commit A. So a pathspec-scoped commit is genuinely safe for
the *other* session's work. The catch is that it constrains the wrong
party — safety depends on the *committing* session complying, and the
session at risk cannot enforce it.

*Caveat found while defining the hook:* =git commit -- <paths>= *fails
on an untracked file* (=pathspec 'NEW.txt' did not match any file(s)
known to git=). A new file must be =git add=-ed first, so for brand-new
content the staging window this mitigation exists to avoid is
unavoidable. The mitigation is complete only for already-tracked files —
which is where this incident happened, TODO.org being tracked.

*Options, none chosen:*
- *Convention only:* ban =git add= in favour of =git commit -- <paths>=,
  in CLAUDE.md and the org skill. Cheapest, verified, and binds only
  sessions that follow it. Less hollow than it sounds when both sessions
  are Claude Code reading the same CLAUDE.md — but it is guidance, and
  the user asked for a mechanism.
- *A =pre-commit= hook*, defined below. The only option that is a
  mechanism rather than guidance, and cheaper than first assumed.
- *Route git writes through the event queue* of
  [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]], which already
  carries =session_id=/=agent_id= and exists to stop concurrent sessions
  mutating shared state directly. Heaviest, and most consistent with
  where the project is going.

*The =pre-commit= option, defined.* Measured 2026-08-12 in a scratch
repo with a hook that printed its own environment. =GIT_INDEX_FILE=
identifies which command form invoked the commit:

| Command                 | =GIT_INDEX_FILE=             | Sweeps a staged file? |
|-------------------------+------------------------------+-----------------------|
| =git commit=            | =.git/index=                 | yes                   |
| =git commit --amend=    | =.git/index=                 | yes                   |
| =git commit -a=         | =.git/index.lock=            | yes                   |
| =git commit -- <paths>= | =.git/next-index-<pid>.lock= | no                    |

The hook can read *both* views at once: what the commit will contain
(=git diff --cached=) and what the real index holds
(=GIT_INDEX_FILE=$(git rev-parse --git-dir)/index git diff --cached=).
In the pathspec case those differ exactly as they should.
=CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= also propagates into the hook, confirmed by
printing it.

*Proposed rule:* refuse when the form is a whole-index one (basename of
=GIT_INDEX_FILE= is =index= or =index.lock=) *and* the real index is
non-empty; fire only when =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= is set. That is the
sweep case and nothing else. =git commit -- <paths>= always passes; =git
commit -a= against an empty index passes; a human committing from a
plain terminal is never affected. The message should name the staged
paths and print the pathspec form to use instead.

*This retires the "needs ownership tracking" objection,* which the first
version of this heading asserted and which is wrong: the command *form*
is a sufficient discriminator and the safe form is self-identifying.
Per-session ownership of staged paths would only be needed for a more
permissive rule ("allow the sweep if everything staged is mine"). If it
ever is, the precedent for where such state lives is
=claude-code-ide-org-queue-directory=.

*Known limitations, accepted rather than solved:* =--amend= is
indistinguishable from a plain pathspec-less commit — both report
=.git/index= — so it gets refused too, absent an =ALLOW_SWEEP= escape
hatch. =--no-verify= bypasses the hook entirely, making the mistake loud
rather than impossible. Merge/rebase/cherry-pick paths are *untested*;
the scratch-repo run errored before reaching them. And it is opt-in per
clone via =core.hooksPath=, inheriting =pre-push='s setup caveat.

*Testing* would follow the existing =bin/*-test= pattern: a scratch
repo, each of the four command forms, asserting exit status and message.

:END:
*Where this stands, 2026-09-01.*

Nothing here has been built, and one premise has moved:
=.githooks/pre-commit= now *exists* — it lints staged org files — so the
second option is no longer "add a hook" but "extend one", which removes
the setup cost it was costed with and puts the sweep guard beside a
check that already reads the staged set. Checked the same day: neither
=CLAUDE.md=, the skills, nor =.claude/rules/= mentions the pathspec
convention, and =GIT_INDEX_FILE= appears nowhere in =.githooks/=. So the
convention option has not shipped either, in this repo, whatever any
session's standing instructions say.

*Blocked on this in practice:* the user has stated they will not run a
second concurrent session until a mechanism is in place, so this gates
concurrent work generally rather than being one more backlog item.


* MAYBE Decide what this module may change on a user's machine when installed :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 11:54]
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, from a narrow question — how to set
=org-log-into-drawer= permanently — that turned out to have a general
answer: *a distributable package configures its own behavior, and does
not silently reconfigure the user's.* Worth deciding once, because the
module already crosses that line in more than one place and each
crossing is cheap to fix now and expensive to fix after other people
depend on it.

*The specific inventory, as of 2026-08-12:*

1. *Four global hooks, installed unconditionally at load*
   (the =add-hook= forms in =config.el=): one =org-blocker-hook= and three
   =org-trigger-hook= functions. Anyone who enables =:tools
   claude-code-ide-org= gets auto-clock-in on every transition to
   =DOING=/=PLANNING=, =NEXT= demotion, and sole-=TODO= promotion
   applied to *every org file they open* — not only this project's.
   These are opinionated GTD policies, and they are the bigger problem
   here, not the smaller one.
2. *A hardcoded assumption about one user's config*
   (a comment in =config.el=): the comment reads "With =org-log-into-drawer=
   nil (this user's setting)". The =let=-binding it justifies is
   correct and should stay — binding locally rather than globally is
   exactly the right instinct, and it is already documented as
   deliberate — but the *default* wants to be a =defcustom= rather than
   an assumption about whoever wrote it.

*The precedent already set, same day.* The =org-log-into-drawer=
question was resolved by adding =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= to this repo's
own =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= headers rather than by =setq=-ing the
variable. Verified: =#+STARTUP:= sets it *buffer-locally*, leaving the
global untouched, so the scope claimed matches the scope owned. That is
the shape the rest of this should follow.

*The module is not starting from zero here.* Eight =defcustom=s already
exist (=-queue-directory=, =-capture-file=, =-working-hours=,
=-query-files=, =-session-recovery-enabled=, =-audit-log-file=,
=-clock-status-file=, =-guidepost-gap-threshold=). Configuring its own
behavior is established practice; the gap is the global hooks and the
one hardcoded assumption, not the general habit.

*Genuinely open, and the reason this is =MAYBE= rather than =TODO=:*
whether a *Doom module* earns more latitude than a *MELPA package*. A
Doom module is opted into explicitly, per user, by editing =init.el= —
which is arguably consent to exactly these policies, and the hooks are
much of why someone would enable it at all. A MELPA package is a
library, and the same behavior on load would be indefensible. The two
targets may want different answers, or one guarded entry point
(=claude-code-ide-org-setup=) that installs nothing until called. Decide
that before doing the work, since it determines whether the hooks move
behind a toggle or stay as they are.

*Related:* [[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] changes
one of these very hooks, so whichever lands second should not re-litigate
the other's decision.
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] owns the file-header
convention the =#+STARTUP:= line was added to.


* TODO Read power assertions as a review-time attribution signal :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 15:52]
:END:

CLAUDE.md records =pmset -g log= as "not yet attempted" and frames the
per-app power assertions as noise obscuring clean sleep/wake
transitions. For *this* question the framing is backwards: a laptop that
never sleeps emits zero transitions and a rich assertion record, and the
assertion holder is the answer.

Tried 2026-08-11 against a known-answer window. The log named the
competing activity exactly:

: 14:01:56  Created   NoDisplaySleepAssertion  (zoom.us, PID 58601)
: 14:42:14  Released  NoDisplaySleepAssertion  00:40:17

The user's own estimate was "roughly 14:00 to roughly 14:45" — the log
was right to the second, and the 40m17s sits entirely inside the 54m11s
permission-block of
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]].

*Not a validated method, on one observation.* Zoom happened to hold a
cleanly-named assertion; =coreaudiod= held 158 of the 361 entries in the
same window and is a far noisier proxy. An assertion also is not
inattention — people work through calls. The most it can honestly do is
prompt the human reviewer, never decide.

*Retention bounds it:* the log went back ~7 days (earliest 2026-08-04).
That puts a deadline on "the human applies at a moment of their own
choosing."

Revises the disposition recorded in
[[id:9d2fcdad-9bf7-47b6-8018-223b13ec4577][9d2fcdad]]'s neighbourhood,
which proposed closing the =pmset= lead as won't-do — correct for the
stale-interval guess heuristic, wrong for attribution.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on
2026-08-14,* as a capability rather than a requirement. Note it is
explicitly *not* the =pmset= lead that
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] closed as
won't-do: that one guessed when a stale clock stopped, this one assigns
an existing span to a heading at review time. Same data source,
different question, and only the first was refuted.

*A ground-truth absence window, reported by the user 2026-08-25 and worth keeping because this heading has none.* They started a =/next-session= run and left immediately, confirming at 17:59 that they had been away since roughly 16:56 and still were. So the interval =[2026-08-25 Tue 16:56]--[2026-08-25 Tue 17:59]+= is a *known* absence, stated rather than inferred -- which is exactly the kind of label any proposed signal has to be scored against, and exactly what the corpus lacks.

*Note what the session's own guideposts said about that hour, because it is the whole problem.* The agent ran continuously and productively throughout it -- two headings worked, a commit landed, four probe scripts ran -- so =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= bracketing produced one long unbroken span of genuine *agent* work. Nothing in the queue distinguishes that hour from an hour with a human watching every turn. Both look identical from the queue, and both are correctly credited as agent time; what is missing is orthogonal to the clock rather than an error in it.

*Which sharpens what a power-assertion signal would be for.* Not correcting a clock -- the clock was right. It would answer a different question the review pass asks constantly: whether a span had a human *available to be interrupted*, and therefore whether the work in it has had any human judgement applied to it at all. Everything this session queued is unreviewed in that stronger sense, and no existing field records the difference.

*Caveat on using this datum.* It is one window, reported to the minute by memory, with a soft start ("immediately after kicking off" -- the session's first event was 16:56) and no end yet. Good enough to sanity-check a signal's shape; not a benchmark.

*Tested 2026-08-25 against a known event, and the result corrects this heading's own proposal.* The user reported that the Mac slept mid-session because the clamshell was closed, and could not say for how long -- so there was ground truth for the *fact* and none for the *bounds*, which is exactly the case this heading is for.

*The signal is real, but it is not the one proposed here.* This heading asks for *power assertions*. Those are precisely the noise =7771fc63= dismissed: =pmset -g log= for the session window is dominated by =PID 5048(Claude) Summary NoIdleSleepAssertion "Electron"= every fifteen minutes, =AddressBookSourceSync=, =storagekitd=, =coreaudiod= -- hundreds of lines, no transition. What answered the question was the =Notification= domain in the same log, two lines:

: 2026-08-25 17:06:53  Notification  Display is turned off
: 2026-08-25 17:50:00  Notification  Display is turned on

*It agrees with the queue to the second.* =clock_in= on [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was queued at 17:06:54, one second after the display went off; the =resume= guidepost is at 17:50:14, fourteen seconds after it came on. Neither number was available to the other mechanism.

*Cross-validated against a third, independent source* rather than trusted on the coincidence: mtimes of the probe scripts this session wrote to its scratch directory show execution at 17:07:07, 17:07:35, 17:08:10, 17:08:55, 17:09:39, 17:09:45 -- and then nothing until 18:02. So work continued for about three minutes after the lid closed and stopped somewhere in 17:09:45--17:50:00.

*The crucial limitation, and it is what stops this being a drop-in fix.* =Display is turned off= means *the lid closed*, not *the machine stopped*. The same session has a counterexample two hours later: display off 18:05:47, on 18:10:34, with continuous work throughout ([[id:f421c5c3-f81f-4ebf-99c9-42c53cc00558][f421c5c3]]'s probes wrote at 18:06:08, 18:10:09 and 18:11:33). Something held an assertion that time and the machine kept running.

*So the two edges are not equally useful, which is the finding worth keeping:*

- *The wake edge is a reliable *resume* signal.* Display-on means someone opened the lid, and work can restart from there. Cheap and unambiguous.
- *The sleep edge is *not* a reliable stop signal.* It bounds when work *may* have stopped and must be combined with evidence of absence -- no queue events, no file writes -- to say that it did.

*What it would have bought today.* [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was credited 0:03 for roughly seven minutes of real work. The wake edge anchors the post-wake segment exactly; the pre-sleep segment is bounded below by its own =clock_in= and above by the last artefact, giving 17:06:54--17:09:45. Neither bound is a guess. The sleep *onset* stays unbounded within a forty-minute window, and should stay that way in any record.

*Note the shape of the correction.* This is the second time the useful signal turned out to be a different line in a log that had already been dismissed wholesale. Worth re-reading a rejected source for the *specific* record wanted, rather than re-litigating the rejection.


* TODO Make task lifecycle answerable from one timeline, starting at creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 10:32]
:END:

Prompted 2026-08-11 by a concrete reporting question — "how long did it
take from capture time to the PLANNING phase, for each of these tasks?"
— which is the first properly articulated demand on this project's
second stated goal, trustworthy tracking of where attention actually
went. It cannot be answered today, and the reason is not the one it
looks like.

*Measured 2026-08-11:*

: TODO.org  headings carrying :CREATED:   56
: TODO.org  "- State ..." lines            7
: DONE.org  "- State ..." lines            0

All seven come from the two hooks that write their note explicitly
(=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=, =--promote-planning-to-doing=). Not
one came from =org_set_todo=, which binds =org-inhibit-logging= to =t=
unconditionally for every state. So the transition history barely
exists, and in DONE.org — where post-hoc analysis would actually look —
it does not exist at all. That zero is *not* the archiver stripping
drawers: =org-archive-subtree= moves the whole subtree intact. Nothing
was ever written.

The example question is the worst case of this. =PLANNING= is transient
— auto-promoted to =DOING= the instant a plan is approved — and the
same census found *zero* headings sitting in it. Current state can
therefore never answer "when did this reach PLANNING"; only a
transition log can.

*So the blocker is the missing log, not =:CREATED:='s location,* and
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] already fixes it:
apply runs inside a genuinely interactive command, which is what lets
org's native logging fire instead of being suppressed. This heading is
the remaining half — making the timeline start at the right place — and
is close to worthless before the cutover, since there would be a
creation line and then silence.

*Proposal: duplicate =:CREATED:= into =:LOGBOOK:= as a creation entry.
Do not move it.* As a property it is cheaply queryable — org-ql filters
and sorts on properties directly — and drawer text is not. Moving it
trades an easy query for a parse. Duplicating buys one timeline and one
parser: analysis becomes "read the entries in order" rather than "read
a property, read a drawer, reconcile two timestamp conventions." It also
gives [[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]]'s single
chronological drawer somewhere to begin.

*org already has the shape, so nothing needs inventing.* With no
previous state org renders the field as an empty string — =org.el= line
11092, =(or org-log-note-previous-state "")= — producing a native
creation entry with no previous state.

*Corrected 2026-08-11, twice, both by direct test rather than reading.*

1. *The literal above was wrong.* The empty string is padded, not
   quoted, so the real rendering carries no ="= pair at all:

   : - State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-11 Tue 15:46]

   A parser keyed on =from ""= matches nothing. The claim that "existing
   parsers, and org-ql, handle it unmodified" was never verified and
   should be re-checked against that actual string before anything is
   built on it.

2. *=TODO= alone carried no =!= marker*, so the one transition this
   feature most needs — into =TODO=, the default keyword a capture
   receives — logged nothing, silently and without error. Interactivity
   is necessary for native logging but not sufficient; logging also has
   to be *requested*. Fixed by
   [[id:90a5a0cf-a81d-45dc-a453-0bfe61e3ef93][90a5a0cf]]. Had this
   shipped as written, it would have produced no creation entries for
   precisely the common case and looked like a bug in apply.

*The alternative, which may well be better and is not yet weighed:*
synthesise the creation entry *at read time*, in whatever reporting
function consumes this, from the =:CREATED:= property that already
exists. Nothing in any file changes, there is no duplication to diverge,
and no migration. The cost is that every consumer must know to do it,
where writing it once puts it in the record for anything that reads the
drawer — including a human. Decide this before writing anything.

*If the write-it-down option wins, two consequences:*
- *Backfill.* 56 headings have =:CREATED:= and no creation entry. A
  one-time pass can synthesise them from the property — mechanical and
  safe, with precedent in the retrospective consolidation pass recorded
  in DONE.org. Without it the analysis has a cutoff date.
- *It depends on [[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]].*
  =org_capture= does not write =:CREATED:= at all, so headings born from
  the tool have no creation time for a backfill to read. Fix that first
  or those headings have no origin in either place.

*Mechanism, if written at creation time:* it need not be special. Under
the queue model creation can simply *be* the =capture= event proposed in
[[id:7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f][7fa68d5c]], with apply
writing its =:LOGBOOK:= entry exactly as it writes a state transition.
The two headings are one feature approached from different sides —
capture durability there, timeline origin here.

*One semantic caveat for whoever writes the parser:* "created" and
"acquired its first TODO keyword" are not the same event in general.
They coincide throughout this project because every task heading is born
with a keyword, but the equivalence is a local accident, not a rule, and
a heading that gains a keyword later would make them diverge.


* TODO Fall back to the queue when org_capture cannot reach Emacs :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 07:31]
:END:

=org_capture= requires a live Emacs. When the server is unreachable the
call fails outright and the task exists only in the conversation, which
ends. Not hypothetical: on 2026-08-10 the Emacs server was down for the
first ten minutes or so of a session (=emacsclient= reporting "can't
find socket"), and any capture attempted then would simply have been
lost.

This is the one creation path that cannot use the property the event
queue exists for — durable, cheap, no Emacs required. Everything else a
session records survives an Emacs outage; the act of writing down a new
task does not.

*Proposal:* attempt the live capture; on failure, append a =capture=
event to the queue, and let apply create the heading.

*Three things this raises, none of them settled:*
- *It is the queue's first outbox use.* Every existing kind is a
  proposal about a heading that already exists; a =capture= event would
  bring one into being. That is a genuinely different contract, and the
  review buffer would need to render it as such.
- *The :ID: must be minted by the session, not at apply time.* An ID
  assigned on application is useless to the session that queued it — it
  could not link to the heading, block on it, or clock against it, which
  is exactly what =claude-code-ide-org-capture= returns its ID for
  today. Minting without Emacs is fine (=org-id-new= is a UUID), but it
  means an ID can be referenced before the heading it names exists.
- *Reconciliation.* If the live capture succeeded and only its reply was
  lost, applying the queued event would duplicate the heading. A
  session-minted ID gives apply a cheap idempotency key: if that :ID:
  already resolves, the event is already realised.

*The larger caveat, which bears on this and on
[[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]] both.* This tool creates a bare
=* TODO <title>= at top level in a single target file. It cannot place a
heading under a named parent, nor carry tags, a body, or any property
beyond the =:ID:= it generates. Consequently almost every heading in
this project is written by direct Elisp instead — four were created that
way on 2026-08-10 alone, none through the tool. So instrumenting or
repairing =org_capture= improves a path that is rarely taken, and the
prior question is whether the tool should become capable enough to be
worth using. Decide that first; it may reorder or absorb both headings.

*Considered and set aside:* queueing a =capture= event on *every*
capture, as an audit record carrying =session_id=/=agent_id= — which is
the only place session attribution exists, and would answer "which
session created this heading?" structurally rather than from prose in
the body. Rejected for now on the caveat above: it would record almost
nothing real. Revisit if the tool becomes the normal creation path.


*Also the timeline origin:* recording creation as a =:LOGBOOK:= entry
([[id:d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5][d9132c57]]) can ride this same event rather
than needing a mechanism of its own — apply would write the creation
entry exactly as it writes a state transition.
