** TODO CLAUDE.md carries dated history that costs every session's context :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9d009401-93dd-40e5-a243-7a30a8a5bd55
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 12:47]
:BLOCKER:  ids(9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806)
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 12:48] \\
  filed from audit, prune needs user in loop
:END:

*Filed 2026-08-21, not done.* CLAUDE.md is ~5,500 words, loaded into every session, and a large share is dated narrative -- retirements, incident stories, won't-dos, measured-on-date facts -- the same disease [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]] names in TODO.org bodies, in the one file no session can skip. The rules should stay; the archaeology should live in DONE.org bodies or the relevant heading, with a pointer where the rule needs its provenance. Judgment-heavy prune, so it needs the user in the loop, not a background pass.

*Deliberately not done 2026-09-02, and the reason is this heading's own
last sentence:* "Judgment-heavy prune, so it needs the user in the loop,
not a background pass." Nine sibling doc edits landed that day
unattended; this one stopped. Recording the stop so it does not read
later as an oversight.

*What did happen is that the file got bigger, which this heading has a
claim on.* CLAUDE.md went from 1022 lines / 58,723 characters at session
start to 1182 / 67,747 — *+160 lines, +15%* — in the same session, and
in the same slice, as a heading asking that it shrink. The additions are
rules rather than archaeology (the authority section, the three interval
numbers, the grouping clock exception, the guidepost rewrite), but the
tension is real and belongs on the record rather than in a commit
message.

*The premise is weaker at line level than the filing assumed, measured
today.* Of 1183 lines, *38* carry an explicit date and *40* carry a
historical marker (=retired=, =used to=, =until 20xx=, =no longer=,
=measured=, =was wrong=). That is ~3%, not "a large share". Paragraph
level would score higher — one date can carry a whole paragraph of
archaeology — so the honest reading is that the *count* is small and the
*mass* is unmeasured.

*Where the mass is, since that is what a prune needs:*

| section                  | lines | dated | historical |
|--------------------------+-------+-------+------------|
| State transition rules   |   315 |    14 |          9 |
| Engineering practices    |   278 |     9 |          6 |
| Session tracking         |   221 |     8 |         15 |
| MCP tools                |    81 |     2 |          4 |
| everything else combined |   288 |     5 |          6 |

Three sections hold *69%* of the file. Any prune worth doing is a prune
of those three, and a pass that trims evenly across the rest would move
almost nothing.

*A question for the decision, not settled here.* Several of the densest
passages are load-bearing *because* they are dated — "this said the
opposite until 2026-08-26 and was wrong the whole time" is what stops a
reader trusting a remembered version of the rule. The prune's real
question is not "how much history" but *which history is still doing
work*, and that is exactly the judgement the heading reserved for a
human.

*Related:* [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]] names the same disease in org
bodies and now records the retention policy that answered it there. Its
criterion — prose earns its place when it lets a future session pick the
work up without re-deriving context — transfers directly, and is
probably the sharpest test available for this file too.

*The decisions, made concrete 2026-09-02 so this is a choice rather than
a framework.* Four, and only the last is hard.

*1. The criterion.* [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]] settled this for org bodies
and it transfers: prose earns its place when it lets a future session
pick the work up without re-deriving the context, and fails when it
narrates how the last session got there. Adopting it costs nothing and
makes most of the pass mechanical. A size target is the alternative and
is the thing that heading argued against, because it punishes
measurements — the most reused part.

*2. Where removed text goes.* Into the DONE.org heading that owns the
story, into a =:PLAN:= drawer, or deleted outright. The last is
defensible *here* in a way it is not for org bodies: CLAUDE.md is
version-controlled with full history and no bounded archive, so nothing
is lost.

*3. Whether "won't do" is a category that lives here at all.* There is
an 11-line =Won't do= block (the =pmset= sleep/wake log). It is not
archaeology about a mechanism — it is a decision record stopping someone
re-proposing a dead idea. The question generalises beyond this block:
do won't-dos live in CLAUDE.md or in the heading that declined them?

*4. Which dated history is still doing work.* Ten retirement blocks,
*114 lines*, about 10% of the file:

| block                                        | lines |
|----------------------------------------------+-------|
| =PLANNING= retirement                        |    24 |
| =:SESSIONS:= retirement                      |    17 |
| datetree / "belongs to another system"       |    12 |
| won't-do: =pmset= log                        |    11 |
| =--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo= removal   |    11 |
| working-hours guess retired                  |     9 |
| epic vs =:EPIC:= decision                    |     8 |
| =active_only= caveat, since fixed            |     8 |
| what replaced the promotion                  |     8 |
| read-only clear-and-restore retired          |     6 |

*They are not the same kind of thing, which is why a sweep is wrong.*
Some describe a mechanism that no longer exists and nothing depends on —
the =:SESSIONS:= drawer is gone and nothing writes one. Others are
load-bearing *because* they are dated: the =active_only= caveat exists
because the old text was wrong in a way that produced misparented
headings, and a reader carrying the old version needs telling it
changed. Delete that and the file is smaller and someone reintroduces
the bug.

*The sharpest test case is the epic-versus-=:EPIC:= block* — pure
decision archaeology, zero operational content, and exactly what stops
the next person "cleaning up" =:CATEGORY:= into a purpose-built
property.

*So the honest split is probably:* a mechanism that no longer exists can
go; a rule that *changed* stays with its date.

*Recommended sequence, not taken:* adopt criterion 1, settle 2 as
"delete, git has it", then a first pass over only the blocks where the
mechanism is entirely gone — =:SESSIONS:=, working-hours,
=auto-promote-sole-todo=, the superseded =active_only= caveat. About 45
lines, all low-judgement, leaving every "this rule changed" note intact.
What remains afterwards is the genuinely contested ~70 lines, which is
the conversation worth having.

*Stated so it is not mistaken for progress:* 45 lines out against 160
lines in today. A first pass would not undo this session's growth and
should not be reported as fixing the problem.

*Re-aimed 2026-09-02, before any pruning was done.* This heading's premise is that CLAUDE.md's cost is *dated history*. Verified against the Claude Code plugin docs that day, a larger claim on the same file holds: **roughly 660 of its 1196 lines document machinery that would ship to a consuming project and currently cannot.**

=.claude/rules/= is not a plugin component and appears nowhere in the plugin documentation, and there is no documented mechanism for a plugin to contribute always-loaded instructions to a consuming project's CLAUDE.md. So the state-transition rules, the queue architecture and the tool surface are stranded in the one surface with no distribution path — and they are stranded *because* of the deliberate test that kept them here, that a rule which must hold with no =.org= file open cannot live in a path-scoped rule.

*Which means this heading and [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] are the same triage from opposite ends.* This one asks what earns its place in an always-loaded file; that one asks what must reach another project. A passage that must ship is load-bearing by definition; a passage that is testbed-only is a prune candidate for a better reason than being dated — **audience rather than age**, which cuts first and cuts cleaner than the criterion this heading was going to use.

*Consequence for sequencing, and it is the reason nothing was pruned:* [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] runs first, and its classification is most of the work this heading needs. Pruning before it risks deleting or burying exactly the rules a consuming project requires, after which they must be reconstructed from git.

*Four decisions were settled by the user 2026-09-02 and stand, scoped to the archaeology rather than the relocation:* the criterion is [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]]'s — prose earns its place when it lets a future session pick the work up without re-deriving context. Removed archaeology is deleted outright, since this file is version-controlled with no bounded archive. Won't-dos belong on the heading that declined them, not here. The fourth — what becomes of decision records guarding a *live* convention — was left open, and the relocation question above changes it: a guard for a convention that ships should travel with the convention rather than being compressed into a file that does not.


** DONE The file-header template omits #+STARTUP:, which both files carry :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-02 Wed 09:45]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       4072b6ec-2785-40ba-81e8-aa559508501c
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 16:10]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-02 Wed 09:45] \\
  template now matches both files byte for byte
:END:
:PLAN:

*Moved down from the parent 2026-09-01,* where it had sat since
2026-08-12 as part of a four-drift list whose other three have all
landed.

*The gap, measured 2026-09-01.* Both =TODO.org= and =DONE.org= begin
=#+STARTUP: logdrawer logdone content=. The documented file-header
template — now in =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=, lines 19–21 —
lists =#+TODO:=, =#+TAGS:= and =#+ARCHIVE:= and stops. =grep -c
logdrawer= returns 0 there and 0 in CLAUDE.md. So a header line both
files carry, and which changes how org logs, is written down nowhere.

Note what *has* been fixed, since this item was originally filed as one
of four: the documented =#+TODO:= line now matches TODO.org's line 1
byte for byte, and DONE.org now carries a =#+TODO:= line where it
previously had none. Only the =#+STARTUP:= omission survives.

*Why a file header and not a =setq=, which the rule should say rather
than leave to be rediscovered.* =org-log-into-drawer= defaults to nil,
so org's *native* state-change logging writes notes bare, just after the
property drawer, while this module's apply path lands them in
=:LOGBOOK:= — it =let=-binds the variable locally and deliberately, so a
user's own interactive =org-todo= keeps their configured behaviour. The
result was two paths disagreeing, visible on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]], whose =DOING=
note sits above the drawer while older entries sit inside it.

Verified 2026-08-12 that =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= sets the variable
*buffer-locally* — effective value ="LOGBOOK"= inside the file, global
still nil — which is why it is the right instrument: it claims exactly
the scope this project owns and changes nothing for a user's other org
files. The alternatives were weighed and rejected. A global =setq=
reconfigures org for everyone who installs the module (see
[[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]]), and a
=:LOG_INTO_DRAWER:= property — which does work, and does inherit — would
have had to sit on the top-level category headings, which the same
conventions forbade from carrying a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer at all. *That
last objection has since expired*: the top-level category tier was
retired, so the property route is no longer blocked by it. The
buffer-local argument still stands on its own.

*The verification precondition, which belongs beside the rule.*
=#+STARTUP:= is read when org initialises a buffer, so a file already
open in Emacs keeps the old value until it is reverted. Saving is not
enough, and neither is =global-auto-revert-mode= noticing the change if
the buffer is unmodified — the mode has to re-run.

*Done when* the file-header template in =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=
lists =#+STARTUP:= alongside the other three, with the buffer-local
reasoning and the revert precondition.

*Verify:* =grep -c logdrawer .claude/rules/org-conventions.md= is
non-zero, and the template block matches =head -4= of both org files.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-02.* =#+STARTUP: logdrawer logdone content= is now the
fourth line of the documented template in
=.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=, and the template matches =head -4= of
both TODO.org and DONE.org byte for byte.

*The three things worth having beside it all went in:* that
=org-log-into-drawer= defaults to nil, so without the header org's
native logging and this project's apply path disagree — with
=:ID:= b5f94b88 named as the visible instance, a =DOING= note sitting
above its drawer while older entries sit inside it; that a header line
is right rather than a =setq= because it binds *buffer-locally*,
claiming exactly the scope this project owns; and the verification
precondition, which is the one nobody would guess — =#+STARTUP:= is read
when org initialises a buffer, so a file already open keeps the old value
until reverted, and saving is not enough.

*One objection in the source material had expired and was dropped
rather than repeated.* The original reasoning rejected a
=:LOG_INTO_DRAWER:= property because it "would have to sit on the
top-level category headings, which this same CLAUDE.md section forbids
from carrying a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer at all." The top-level category
tier was retired, so that objection no longer holds. The buffer-local
argument stands on its own and is the one kept — carrying the dead
objection forward would have been the exact staleness this pass exists
to remove.

*Note what this closes that is larger than itself.* This was the
surviving quarter of the parent's "four concrete drifts" from
2026-08-11; the other three, including the =#+TODO:= line matching
neither file, had all been fixed without the list being updated. The
documented template and the real files now agree in full.


** DONE A :BLOCKER: on a MAYBE heading is dormant, and no convention says so :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-02 Wed 09:45]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       72e82469-3d4f-4ee1-b0c4-35b1c253901c
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 16:11]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-02 Wed 09:45] \\
  keep the property; reframe the warning
:END:
:PLAN:

*Moved down from the parent 2026-09-01,* where it was a one-line aside
under a 2026-08-13 scope addition whose main claim has since been fixed.

A =:BLOCKER:= on a =MAYBE= heading is *dormant*, because org evaluates
blocking against the blocked heading's own state — nothing blocks a
heading that is not trying to move. So the property looks like a live
dependency and is not one.

*It is caught but not documented,* which is the awkward half.
=bin/lint-org= warns on it, and did three times on 2026-09-01 —
=5798=, =5871=, =7192= in TODO.org. But =grep -ci dormant
.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= returns 0, and the =:BLOCKER:=
convention there says nothing about it. A reader who has not tripped the
lint has no way to know, and a reader who has tripped it gets a warning
with no rule behind it.

*Why it matters more than a lint warning suggests.* The whole point of
=:BLOCKER:= is that it is the machine-checkable form of a dependency
that would otherwise rot in prose. A dormant one is the worst case: it
reads as enforcement to a human and enforces nothing, so it is *less*
honest than the prose it replaced.

*Done when* the =:BLOCKER:= section of
=.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= states the dormancy and what to do
about it — whether that is "do not put one on a MAYBE", or "expect it to
wake when the heading leaves MAYBE", which is a small decision this
heading has to make rather than assume.

*Verify:* the rule file mentions the MAYBE case, and the wording agrees
with what =bin/lint-org= actually warns.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-02,* into the "Dependencies between tasks" section of
=.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=, directly after the paragraph that
says =:BLOCKER:= *is* enforced — which is where the qualification has to
sit to be read by anyone it applies to.

*The decision the heading said it had to make.* It asked whether the rule
should be "do not put one on a =MAYBE=" or "expect it to wake when the
heading leaves =MAYBE=". *Keep it* — the second. A =:BLOCKER:= on a
=MAYBE= records a real dependency that wakes by itself the moment the
heading takes a live keyword, and deleting it to silence the lint would
lose that record for nothing. So the rule reframes the warning rather
than the property: read the lint line as "this is filed, not enforced",
and if you are *relying* on the block to hold, the heading is not
=MAYBE=.

That last clause is the useful half. It turns a passive caveat into a
test a reader can apply to their own heading, which is what distinguishes
this from a footnote nobody acts on.

*The framing kept from the body:* this is the worst shape a dependency
can take, because the whole point of =:BLOCKER:= over a prose sentence is
that it is machine-checkable — so one that reads as enforcement and
enforces nothing is *less* honest than the sentence it replaced. Three
instances stand in TODO.org today, and =bin/lint-org= has been warning
on all three with no written rule behind the warning.

*Moved down from the parent 2026-09-01,* where it was scoped on
2026-08-12 and never written.

*The problem is specific to this repo's files.* They quote =CLOCK:= and
=- State "..."= lines constantly in *prose* — as worked examples, as
recorded experiment output, as evidence in an outcome summary. A pattern
match over the raw file therefore returns real-looking timestamps that
were never real, and the wrong answers are indistinguishable from the
right one by inspection.

*Cost, measured 2026-08-12* while estimating when work on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]'s children began.
Three attempts, two wrong:

1. =[2026-08-06 Thu 17:41]= — matched a =- State "DOING"= line inside
   [[id:3d576d29-747f-40b9-a5b3-ecc76679daad][3d576d29]]'s *body*, part
   of the write-up of the backdating experiment itself.
2. =[2026-08-06 Thu 22:35]=, verified by grepping the file for that
   =CLOCK:= line — which returned line 1538, *also* example prose, and in
   a different heading outside the subtree entirely. The extraction was
   right by then; the *verification* repeated the original mistake.
3. Correct only when both scoped to the subtree *and* restricted to
   lines between =:LOGBOOK:= and =:END:=.

Attempt 2 is the part worth keeping: a check that re-uses the failing
method confirms the failure rather than catching it.

*The rule to write:* timestamps and clock data come from inside a
=:LOGBOOK:= drawer, or from =org_clock_report= / =org_outline= / =org_body=,
never from a bare pattern match. It generalises the anchored-drawer
caution — =grep -c ':SESSIONS:'= counting prose mentions — from "does
this drawer exist" to "what does it contain."

*Placement: the org skill.* It is a way of reading any org file whose
prose discusses org, not a convention specific to this repo. Verified
2026-08-12 that neither the skill nor CLAUDE.md said anything about
pattern-matching org files; re-verified 2026-09-01, =grep -ci 'bare
pattern match' .claude/skills/org/SKILL.md= still returns 0. A session
handoff once asserted the skill already covered it, which was itself an
unchecked claim of exactly this kind.

*Note the sharper modern form.* =org-map-entries= bounds entries by
construction and is the parser of record — the org-dev skill's §0b now
says so for *reading*, not just building. This heading is the
timestamp-specific case of that rule and should cross-reference it
rather than restate it.

*Done when* the org skill states it, with the three-attempt cost as the
evidence.

*Verify:* =grep -ci 'LOGBOOK' .claude/skills/org/SKILL.md= finds the
rule, and it names =org_body=/=org_clock_report= as the sanctioned
readers.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-02* into the org skill's "Reading & parsing" section, where a reader arrives before they have extracted anything.

*The three-attempt cost went in as the evidence,* and attempt 2 is the part that earns its place: it was "verified" by grepping the file for the very =CLOCK:= line just extracted, which returned a different heading's example prose. A check that re-uses the failing method confirms the failure rather than catching it — which is the same shape as [[id:542924c1-9152-4414-8da8-d530d3423611][542924c1]]'s class, one level in.

*Named the sanctioned readers rather than only the prohibition,* since "never pattern-match" without an alternative is an instruction people route around: =org_body=, =org_clock_report=, =org_outline=, or =org-map-entries= from a buffer.

*Verified:* =grep -c ➺re pattern match'= on the skill returns 1, where it returned 0 this morning and on 2026-08-12 when the item was first scoped.

*Correction to the line above, 2026-09-02:* it should read =grep -c
'bare pattern match'=. The mangled character came from writing this
debrief through =emacsclient= with =\x27= for an apostrophe — the hex
escape greedily consumed the following characters, which happened to be
hex digits. Recorded rather than silently fixed because it is a small
instance of this project's own theme: the write succeeded, reported
success, and produced something wrong that only reading the result
caught.


** DONE The sibling-survey idiom is undocumented, and org_outline cannot navigate up :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-02 Wed 09:47]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6e59c0f9-8af4-43d5-99c1-637ea360c3d0
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 16:11]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-02 Wed 09:47] \\
  idiom, limitation and buffer-side half all documented
:END:
:PLAN:

*Moved down from the parent 2026-09-01,* where it was scoped on
2026-08-13. Re-verified the same day: =grep -i sibling
.claude/skills/org/SKILL.md= returns one hit and it is about refile
levels, not this; =org-map-entries= appears once, inside an unrelated
code example. Still unwritten.

*Pairs with the nomination rule.* CLAUDE.md warns that setting =NEXT=
may demote a sibling via =--trigger-demote-conflicting-next=, and never
says how to find out *which* sibling before committing to it.

*The idiom: to survey a heading's siblings, call =org_outline= scoped to
the _parent's_ =:ID:=*, with =active_only= and a =max_depth=. One call,
one screen:

: org_outline scope=<parent id> active_only=true max_depth=3

*Why it needs writing down rather than being obvious.* Twice on
2026-08-13 this check was done with a hand-rolled Python scan of
=TODO.org= instead, by someone who had used =org_outline= correctly an
hour earlier. The tool's =scope= parameter already documented "an =:ID:=
to index only that subtree" — the capability was never missing, only the
reflex. That is the pattern
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]] exists to catch,
and it recurred *inside* a session that had already invoked the tool.

*The limitation to document alongside it, so the idiom is not mistaken
for a full answer:* =org_outline= navigates *down* only. Scoped to a leaf
it returns that leaf and its descendants, never its ancestors — so the
caller must already know the parent's =:ID:=. A =relation: siblings=
parameter was considered on 2026-08-13 and *rejected in favour of this
documentation*: it would only shorten an idiom that already works, on a
tool whose value is being cheap to call.

*A second reason the survey matters, beyond demotion.* =grep= reads what
is *on disk*, which post-cutover is not what has been *decided* — queued
transitions have not landed. =org_outline= reports applied state, so the
difference between "I set this to NEXT" and "this is NEXT" stays
visible. The Python scan on 2026-08-13 silently reported =TODO= for a
heading whose promotion was sitting in the queue; the answer was right,
but only by luck of what was being asked.

*The buffer-side half, which resolves the upward-navigation limitation
rather than leaving it open.* =org_outline= is a thin wrapper —
=org-map-entries=, =org-id-find= for the scope, about ten lines of
formatting — so everything it does is available directly from a buffer,
and more is. Two things =org-map-entries= offers that the tool does not
expose:

1. *Positional scopes* — =tree=, =region=, =file=, =agenda=, or an
   explicit file list. So the sibling survey has a buffer-side form
   needing no parent =:ID:= at all:

   : (save-excursion (org-up-heading-safe)
   :   (org-map-entries #\x27org-get-heading "/NEXT" \x27tree))

   That is exactly the navigate-up case the tool cannot do, free from
   point. It is why =relation: siblings= stays rejected: the capability
   is not missing, only ID-addressed access to it.
2. *org's full MATCH syntax* — the =/NEXT= above, and tag/property
   queries generally. =org_outline= has no MATCH argument and filters
   after the fact with =active_only=, so anything more selective than
   "drop DONE and CANCELLED" is easier from a buffer.

*Two caveats to write down with it, both quiet failures.*
=org-map-entries= respects narrowing, so a narrowed buffer silently maps
only the visible portion and reports a confidently short answer. And
with a file-list scope it visits those files; =org_outline='s docstring
records that this was verified not to move point or mark buffers
modified, which is what makes it safe against files the user has open —
worth preserving rather than rediscovering.

*Done when* the org skill carries the idiom, the downward-only
limitation, and both caveats.

*Verify:* =grep -ci sibling .claude/skills/org/SKILL.md= finds the
survey, not only the refile-level sentence at line 140.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-02* as its own "Surveying a heading's siblings"
subsection under "Working with .org Files", beside the timestamp rule —
both are about *reading* org, and a reader who needs one usually needs
the other.

*Both halves went in, tool and buffer.* The =org_outline scope=<parent
id>= idiom with =active_only= and =max_depth=; the downward-only
limitation stated plainly, so the idiom is not mistaken for a full
answer; and the =org-map-entries … 'tree= form that resolves it from
point. Keeping the second is what makes the rejection of a
=relation: siblings= parameter legible rather than arbitrary — *the
capability is not missing, only ID-addressed access to it.*

*The framing that earns the section its place* is that the reflex was
missing rather than the capability: twice in one day the check was
hand-rolled by someone who had used =org_outline= correctly an hour
earlier, and the =scope= parameter had always documented what it does.
A skill cannot install a capability that already exists; it can only
make it the first thing that comes to mind.

*Two reasons kept, not one.* Demotion is the obvious one — a =NEXT=
nomination may silently demote a sibling and nothing else says which.
The second is sharper and post-cutover: =grep= reads what is *on disk*,
which is not what has been *decided*, since queued transitions have not
landed. =org_outline= reports applied state, so "I set this to NEXT"
stays distinguishable from "this is NEXT". That distinction did not
exist before the queue and is the reason the idiom is worth more now
than when it was first noticed.

*Both quiet failures recorded:* narrowing silently truncates the answer,
and a file-list scope visits files — verified not to move point or mark
buffers modified, which is what makes it safe against files the user has
open.

*Verified:* =grep -ci sibling= on the skill returns 5, where the single
prior hit was about refile levels and had nothing to do with this.


** DONE load-file does not pick up a changed defcustom default :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-02 Wed 09:48]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e5fef4c4-5c42-48c2-a5d7-577dc199c22a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 16:11]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-02 Wed 09:48] \\
  demonstrated in batch, filed inside the reload section
:END:
:PLAN:

*Moved down from the parent 2026-09-01,* where it was scoped on
2026-08-13 for the org-dev skill specifically. Re-verified the same day:
=grep -c defcustom .claude/skills/org-dev/SKILL.md= returns 1, and that
hit is about =:CATEGORY:='s character limit. The trap is unwritten.

*Measured 2026-08-13.* The guidepost threshold was committed as 1200,
=load-file= returned =t=, and the live value stayed 900 — because
=defcustom= only sets a default when the symbol is *unbound*. Reloading
a file whose =defcustom= default changed leaves the old value in place.
It needed an explicit =setq=.

*Why it is worth a rule rather than a note.* Every signal says success:
the commit is right, =load-file= returns =t=, no error is raised. Anyone
who then "verifies" the change against the running Emacs measures the
old value and reads a passing check. That is precisely the failure the
user's standing reload-precondition rule exists to prevent — state what
must be reloaded *before* running the command whose purpose is to see
whether the change took effect — and this is the case where naming the
precondition is not enough, because the obvious precondition
(=load-file=) is the one that does not work.

*Placement: the org-dev skill,* which owns reload-and-verify for this
project's code. It already carries §2's =org-clock-persist-load= trap
and §7's config guidance; this is the same shape of "the reload you
would reach for is insufficient".

*Note the narrower sibling already documented elsewhere:* =#+STARTUP:=
is read at buffer initialisation, so a file open in Emacs keeps the old
value until reverted — recorded on
[[id:4072b6ec-2785-40ba-81e8-aa559508501c][4072b6ec]]. Same class,
different mechanism, and the skill should name the class: *a reload that
succeeds is not evidence the new value is live.*

*Done when* the org-dev skill states the =defcustom= case and the
=setq= remedy, and =bin/check-org-dev-skill= asserts whatever part of it
is mechanically checkable.

*Verify:* =grep -ci 'defcustom' .claude/skills/org-dev/SKILL.md= finds
the reload trap, and the claim about unbound symbols is demonstrated
rather than asserted.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-02* as a subsection of the org-dev skill's §1, "Live
reload procedure" — inside the section describing the reload it
undermines, rather than filed near it.

*Demonstrated rather than asserted, which the Done-when asked for.* Two
one-line files declaring the same symbol with defaults 900 and 1200, run
under =emacs -Q --batch=:

: after first load: 900
: after loading the changed default: 900
: after explicit setq: 1200

So the transcript is in the skill, not a claim about elisp semantics the
reader has to take on trust. It also cost nothing to produce, which is
the argument for doing it: the fact was already believed, and the
demonstration is what makes it checkable by the next person.

*The remedy is stated as a precondition, not as a fix,* because that is
how the standing rule in §0 is phrased and this is its hardest case:
*"this needs =load-file= **and** a =setq=, because =defcustom= will not
overwrite a bound symbol."* Naming the obvious precondition is not
enough here — the obvious precondition is the one that does not work,
and a reader who says "this needs a reload" and reloads has satisfied
their own stated check while measuring the old value.

*Cross-linked to its narrower sibling,*
[[id:4072b6ec-2785-40ba-81e8-aa559508501c][4072b6ec]]'s =#+STARTUP:= case — same class, different
mechanism, and the pair is what makes the class visible: *a reload that
succeeds is not evidence the new value is live.*

*Verified:* =grep -c defcustom= on the org-dev skill returns 5, where it
returned 1 this morning and that hit was about =:CATEGORY:='s character
limit. =bin/check-org-dev-skill= still passes, so the new section did
not break any claim the skill already made about itself.


** DONE Say in CLAUDE.md that it is a starting point and the artifact is the authority :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-02 Wed 09:43]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5f89f872-6c0d-4805-88da-a182971c7ad9
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 16:11]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-02 Wed 09:43] \\
  stated near the top, with state-vs-rationale resolved
:END:
:PLAN:

*Moved down from the parent 2026-09-01,* where it accumulated across
2026-08-11 and -08-12 as two related cautions. Re-verified the same day:
=grep -ci 'starting point' CLAUDE.md= returns 0.

*The mechanism, worth naming rather than leaving as four instances.*
CLAUDE.md is loaded into every session by default while the artifacts it
describes are not, so a stale claim in it *outranks the truth* until
someone deliberately checks. Sections describing mechanisms that have
been cut over are the highest-risk kind, because the prose survives the
code that justified it.

*And the counter-intuitive half, which is the reason this is a rule and
not just an accuracy push.* An accurate CLAUDE.md makes answering *from*
it more often correct, which makes the habit of answering from it
instead of from the artifact *harder to notice* — the same behaviour
with better odds. Accuracy therefore does not retire the risk; it
disguises it.

*The evidence is that most wrong claims did not come from this file at
all.* On 2026-08-11 only four of roughly a dozen wrong claims traced to
CLAUDE.md. The rest came from unchecked inference, from a session's own
earlier summaries, and three times from reading a silently-failing
command's empty output as a result. A rewrite that only fixes the file
addresses a third of the problem and quietly encourages the other
two-thirds.

*What to write:* that the file is a starting point and the artifact is
the authority — plainly, in its own voice, rather than implied. It
should say which artifact wins for which question: the code and tests
for what the system does, the org files for what is planned, the live
Doom config for how Emacs is set up. CLAUDE.md already does this
locally, twice ("Read the live =~/.config/doom/config.el= rather than
any summary of it"; "the current state of the implementation is in the
code, the tests and the config"), so the work is generalising a habit
the file already has rather than inventing one.

*Note the tension to resolve rather than paper over.* The file also
argues, correctly, that the load-bearing *reasons* live only in a body
and are worth quoting inline. So "the artifact is the authority" is
about *state*, not about rationale — a distinction the wording has to
carry, or it will read as an instruction to distrust the file's own
explanations.

*Related but distinct:*
[[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]] makes the checkable claims checkable. This
one is about the claims that are not — and about the reading habit,
which no check can enforce.

*Done when* CLAUDE.md says it, near the top where it is read before the
claims it qualifies.

*Verify:* =grep -ci 'authority\|starting point' CLAUDE.md= finds it
outside the tracker-reading section.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-02* as a section immediately after the opening goals
and before "Architecture: the event queue" — near the top, where the
Done-when asked, so it is read before the claims it qualifies.

*It says the mechanism, not just the instruction.* This file is loaded
into every session by default while the artifacts it describes are not,
so a stale claim outranks the truth until someone deliberately checks,
and sections describing cut-over mechanisms are the highest-risk kind.
Stating the *reason* matters more than stating the rule: "check the
artifact" reads as generic caution, while "the prose outlives the code
that justified it" says when to bother.

*A table settles which artifact wins for which question* — code and
tests for what the system does, the live Doom config for how Emacs is
configured, TODO.org for what is planned, a =defcustom='s own docstring
for what a number means. That last row is new today and pairs with
[[id:594adec6-4855-470d-8ceb-5fe29bf0c935][594adec6]], which put three
such numbers into this file: the summary here is now the thing most
likely to go stale, and the docstring is named as the authority over it.

*The tension the body flagged is resolved explicitly rather than papered
over.* This file also argues that load-bearing *reasons* live only in a
body and are worth quoting inline — so the section says the rule is
about *state*, not *rationale*: distrust the file's account of what is,
do not distrust its account of why. Without that sentence the section
would read as an instruction to distrust the file's own explanations,
which would undercut half of what it exists to carry.

*And the counter-intuitive half is kept,* since it is the reason this is
a rule rather than an accuracy push: an accurate CLAUDE.md makes
answering from it more often correct, which makes the habit of answering
from it *harder to notice*. The 2026-08-11 evidence went in with it —
only four of roughly a dozen wrong claims traced to this file, the rest
to unchecked inference, a session's own summaries, and three times to
reading a silently-failing command's empty output as a result. A rewrite
that only fixes the file addresses a third of the problem.

*Closes with the boundary of the mechanical check,*
=bin/check-conventions=, which verifies cited =:ID:=s resolve and the
keyword set agrees, and cannot check a claim that is merely out of date
— which is most of them. Naming what the check *cannot* do is what keeps
a green check from being read as a green file.

* TODO Direct file writes to a live org buffer can desync org-clock-marker :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-04 Tue 18:27]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-14 Fri 13:51] \\
  confirmed surviving; three dependents
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/clock-marker-desync-guard.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-04, hit live during this session's TODO.org commit-
splitting work: writing TODO.org's content directly via =git
hash-object=/=update-index= (bypassing Emacs) and relying on
=global-auto-revert-mode= to pick up the change desynced Emacs's
*in-memory* clock state from the file text. =org-clock-marker= kept
pointing at its old raw character offset, which after the rewrite
landed on an unrelated =:SESSIONS:= line instead of the =CLOCK:= line
— =org_clock_out= then failed outright with "Clock start time is
gone", and the statusline (which reads live =org-clocking-p=/
=org-clock-marker= state, not the file text — see
=claude-code-ide-org--statusline-task-string=) kept showing "clocked
in" even after a text-level recovery via
=claude-code-ide-org-close-open-interval= had already closed the
=CLOCK:= line on disk.

Worked around manually this time: reset
=org-clock-marker=/=org-clock-hd-marker=/=org-clock-start-time=
directly via =emacsclient=, then called
=claude-code-ide-org--clock-status-hook-out= by hand since normal
=org-clock-out= never got far enough to fire it. The existing "Stale
interval recovery" design (CLAUDE.md) only repairs the *text* side of
a stale interval (crash/shutdown case, where Emacs's own in-memory
state is simply gone after a restart) — it has no equivalent for *this*
case, where Emacs is still running but its live clock state has gone
stale relative to a file it didn't itself edit. Whether the real fix
is "never write live org files outside Emacs, full stop" (a
process/discipline fix, not code) or something more automatic (e.g.
detecting the marker/text mismatch and self-healing) is a question for
planning.

*Second, distinct incident, 2026-08-05*: on
[[id:4cda6bf7-441b-47b2-b3d5-b8c7e7a207a5][Show the TODO keyword, not just clocked in/out, in the statusline]],
after a cross-session clock resume (see the =d150c02e= sibling below)
followed by a proper =org_clock_out=, the =:LOGBOOK:= briefly
contained a genuinely malformed line — bare =CLOCK:= with *no*
timestamp at all — sitting between two valid entries, before
=claude-code-ide-org-consolidate-history= rebuilt the drawer and it
disappeared (the arithmetic of what remained checks out, so nothing
was silently lost, but *why* a timestamp-less =CLOCK:= line got
written in the first place is unconfirmed — a race between two
near-simultaneous writers to the same drawer is the working
hypothesis, not proven). A second, structurally different way the
same "shared mutable file" pressure can corrupt =:LOGBOOK:=, worth
folding into whatever planning addresses this cluster.

Also worth noting for whoever plans =4cda6bf7='s own fix: swapping
=status-label= for =org-get-todo-state= *at the clock marker* is
still marker-dependent — it inherits this exact desync mode (a stale
TODO keyword shown instead of a stale in/out label), it doesn't
route around it.

*Kept and narrowed 2026-08-13, by the user's decision.* Reviewed against
its two partial owners rather than as keep-or-cancel, because that is
what it actually is: a gap between them.

*What is retired.* The original =org_clock_out= failure is unreachable —
that function opens and closes nothing since
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]]'s cutover. So is
the concurrent-writer =CLOCK:= corruption:
=claude-code-ide-org-consolidate-history= has no production caller left,
only tests.

*What survives, and is the whole of this heading now.* Two surfaces,
each only partly owned elsewhere:
1. =--trigger-auto-clock-in= still
   opens a real clock on *any* transition to =DOING=/=PLANNING=,
   including a human's own =C-c C-t=. Apply suppresses it via the
   re-entrancy guard; a hand-edit does not.
   [[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] covers
   *container* headings only, so leaf hand-edits remain.
2. =--statusline-task-string= still reads
   =org-clocking-p=/=org-clock-marker=/=org-clock-history= directly.
   [[id:290b6fc5-3ebb-4827-9a97-5a3542e354b6][290b6fc5]] is scoped to
   the statusline's *data source*, not to marker desync generally.

Cancel this and the uncovered middle belongs to nobody. The deciding
fact against cancelling is that the desync was observed *live on
2026-08-12*, after the cutover, and recorded on =ab75d6d2= — not a
theoretical residue.

*Newly established 2026-08-13, and it raises this heading's stakes.*
Reading =org-clock.el= for
[[id:803314aa-eda9-4d48-9e43-b3eb4802a4e8][803314aa]] found that
=org-clock-out= signals =(error "Clock start time is gone")= exactly when =org-clock-marker='s line is no longer
a =CLOCK:= line — i.e. precisely this heading's desync. That is the same
error incident 1 above recorded. So this desync is not merely a display
problem: it is the reachable trigger for apply's fabricated-interval
bug. The two headings are one causal chain, and this one is upstream.

*Confirmed surviving, and promoted out of =MAYBE=, 2026-08-14 by
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]]* — the heading
whose job was to decide whether this one is superseded. It is not, and
three separate things now depend on it, which is more than "someday"
can carry:

1. Observed *live on 2026-08-12*, after the cutover, so it is residue of
   nothing.
2. It is the upstream half of the causal chain to =803314aa=, per the
   paragraph directly above.
3. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s decision to
   *keep* =claude-code-ide-org-close-open-interval= rests explicitly on
   this heading — a shipped decision now cites it as a reason.


* MAYBE Org skill: commit a newly-captured TODO immediately, when safe :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Skill
:ID:       3cb3f955-f10a-47cd-84ab-e629d73ea59d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-04 Tue 17:54]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-06 Thu 12:07] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/capture-commit-safety.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-04 from conversation: right now, capturing a new org
heading (per CLAUDE.md's "any time a new task is described in
conversation, create an org heading" rule) only edits the working tree
— nothing commits it, so it sits as an uncommitted change alongside
whatever else is in progress until a human notices and commits by
hand. Tell the org skill to commit the capture itself, immediately,
*given that it's safe to do so*.

"Safe" needs pinning down during planning, but at minimum: only the
=.org= file(s) touched by the capture are staged (never a broad
=git add -A=/=git add .=, per this project's own general git-safety
practice), the repo isn't mid-rebase/mid-merge/otherwise in a weird
state, and — the tricky part — the capture commit doesn't get tangled
up with *other* unrelated uncommitted changes already sitting in the
same file from earlier in the session (a commit should be scoped to
the capture, not sweep up unrelated in-progress edits just because
they're in the same file). Whether that means a targeted
=git commit -- TODO.org= (commits the whole file's current diff,
including any unrelated hunks) is actually "safe" in the sense meant
here, or whether something narrower is needed, is exactly the kind of
question actual planning should resolve rather than deciding here.

Should this be prose guidance in the skill (the model decides "is this
one safe" case by case, same register as the existing read-only-buffer
guidance) or something more mechanical? Lean prose, given the "safe"
judgment call looks inherently contextual rather than a fixed
checklist — same reasoning CLAUDE.md already uses elsewhere for calls
that "only the model can make."

*Update 2026-08-05:* substantial overlap with
[[id:782cda6c-111c-454e-93eb-b2b27012078b][Audit for clock/session bookkeeping noise before committing during a clocking-suppressed session]]
— that heading is a concrete, evidence-backed version of the exact
open question above ("doesn't get tangled up with other unrelated
uncommitted changes"). Not merging them here; worth deciding during
planning whether they should be one task, sequenced, or just
cross-referenced as they are now.

[[file:~/.claude/plans/blocker-hook-enforcement.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-04, surfaced while adding a =:BLOCKER:= property to
the sibling heading above: this project's own
=claude-code-ide-org--blocker-clock-running-p= (the one function
currently on =org-blocker-hook=) only denies a =DONE= transition while
a clock is running on that same heading — it never reads =:BLOCKER:=
at all, and org-depend itself isn't loaded anywhere in this project
(=packages.el= notes no additional packages). So every existing
=:BLOCKER:= property in =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= (e.g. ID
799b14be-6849-4e22-8a76-4e214d8a25fe's) is purely advisory today —
=org_set_todo= will happily transition a blocked heading to =DONE=
with nothing stopping it, contrary to what the property visually
implies.

*Premise falsified 2026-08-13: org-depend IS loaded and IS on the hook.*
Measured in the live Emacs, =org-blocker-hook= holds
=(org-block-todo-from-children-or-siblings-or-parent
org-depend-block-todo claude-code-ide-org--blocker-clock-running-p)=,
and =org-enforce-todo-dependencies= is =t=. The inference above went
from "=packages.el= adds no packages" to "org-depend isn't loaded",
which is wrong: Doom loads it as part of org, so nothing had to be
added.

Corroborating measurement rather than inference:
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]], =TODO= with three
unfinished blockers, reports =(org-entry-blocked-p)= = *YES*. Something
is evaluating =:BLOCKER:=, and this project's own function does not.

*What is NOT established, deliberately left open rather than assumed.*
A scratch reproduction — two headings in a temp file, one blocking the
other — did *not* reproduce it: neither reported blocked and both
accepted =DONE=. That is far more likely a fault in the test's id
resolution than a contradiction, but it means the end-to-end claim
"=org-todo= to =DONE= is actually denied" is *unproven*. So this heading
is not cancelled: its premise is dead, its goal may or may not already
be met, and settling that needs a reproduction that works.

*A second finding, which the heading would want either way:* blocking is
evaluated against the *blocked heading's own state*.
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] is =MAYBE= with an
unfinished blocker and reports blocked=no, while =02aaae22= at =TODO=
reports YES. So a =:BLOCKER:= on a =MAYBE= heading is dormant — it looks
enforcing and is not, until the heading leaves =MAYBE=.

Extend =claude-code-ide-org--blocker-clock-running-p= (or add a second
=org-blocker-hook= function alongside it) to also deny a transition to
=DONE= while any ID listed in the heading's own =:BLOCKER:= property
resolves to a heading that isn't itself in a done state (=DONE= or
=CANCELLED=). Should reuse =org-id-find= the same way the existing
clock-marker check and =org_set_todo='s post-transition heading
lookup already do. Whether to also honor the inverse =:TRIGGER:=
property (documented in the org skill alongside =:BLOCKER:=) is an
open question for planning — =org-depend= itself implements both, but
this project may only need one direction enforced.

Blocked on the sibling heading above (=Add a PLANNING TODO state,
driven by the ExitPlanMode matcher=, ID
b95b9fba-f78e-48fe-8546-988709cce309), recorded via the project's
=:BLOCKER:= convention (native org-depend syntax; see the org skill

running clock, not on =:BLOCKER:= (see the sibling heading below for
closing that gap). *Update 2026-08-05: the blocker is now satisfied*
— =b95b9fba= is =DONE=, =PLANNING= is fully implemented, so this

Property left in place regardless (a satisfied =:BLOCKER:= is

Captured 2026-08-04 from conversation, deliberately at "capture the
idea" depth, not fully designed yet:

Whenever the user says to modify some feature (in the org skill's
scope), first check whether the request matches any existing TODO
heading, including =DONE= ones:
- If it matches an existing heading, ask the user for clarification
  (e.g. "this looks like it overlaps with <heading> — same task, or
  something new?") rather than silently creating a duplicate or
  silently assuming it's the same thing.
- If it doesn't match anything, create a new heading, capture it (via
  =org_capture=), set it to =PLANNING=, ask the user any necessary
  clarifying questions, and then offer to plan it properly (i.e. enter
  Plan Mode). If the user declines planning, revert the heading back
  to =TODO= for later planning rather than leaving it stuck in
  =PLANNING=.

Separately, also check whether the proposed change interacts with any
*open* (non-DONE, non-CANCELLED) task via a depends-on relationship in
either direction — i.e. either the new change depends on unfinished
work, or some existing open task depends on the thing being changed —
and surface that to the user.

Open questions deferred to actual planning, not resolved here: how
"matches an existing TODO" is actually determined (semantic search via
=org_query=? something else?), whether discovering an existing
=:BLOCKER:= depends-on link (this heading's own, above) is sufficient
or whether the check also needs to search prose for informal
cross-references that predate this convention, and exactly when in the
org skill's flow this check should fire.

*Considered for promotion 2026-09-03 while composing =ff7ccb2d=, and held at =MAYBE= for a reason
the satisfied blocker hides.* The prerequisite landing is not the constraint any more; the *design
is stale*. The behaviour specified above sets a new heading to =PLANNING= and reverts it to =TODO= if
the user declines to plan — and =PLANNING= was **retired 2026-08-28** (=c954f650=), along with the
=ExitPlanMode= promotion hook, after measuring 7 transitions in 432. Nothing replaced it, deliberately.

So this heading cannot be implemented as written, and the part that survives — check a modification
request against existing headings before creating a duplicate, and surface depends-on relationships
in either direction — needs restating without the keyword it was built around. That is a rewrite, not
a start, and it should happen before the heading is picked up rather than by whoever picks it up.


* MAYBE Worktree-based session partitioning, with a canonical-file caveat :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       2e09adb7-6e29-499c-a40e-98cd2b327cff
:CREATED:  [2026-08-04 Tue 13:29]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-04 Tue 13:30] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/worktree-session-partitioning.md][Plan]]

Raised in conversation 2026-08-04: can separate Claude Code sessions
work on distinct TODOs simultaneously by each operating in its own
=git worktree= of a project repo? For the *code* side, yes — that's
exactly what worktrees are for, one checkout + branch per session,
no shared-file collisions. Emacs' own uniquify already disambiguates
same-basename buffers at different paths, so that part isn't even in
question.

Where it doesn't extend: =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= themselves must stay a
single canonical copy in the main checkout, not one copy per
worktree. Two failure modes rule out a per-worktree copy: (1) =:ID:=
lookups go through =org-id-locations=, a single global hash table
keyed by ID — a heading =:ID:= duplicated across two on-disk copies
(which a worktree checkout produces automatically) means whichever
copy was scanned last silently wins, with every =org_*= tool
targeting an unpredictable copy; (2) CLOCK/=:SESSIONS:= data written
into a worktree-local copy is invisible to =org_clock_report=/
=org_query=/the agenda until that branch merges, and is lost outright
if the worktree is torn down first — fragmenting exactly the
attention-tracking data the clock exists to keep whole. The single
clock living in one Emacs process reading one canonical file is the
mechanism that enforces "one attention at a time," not a limitation
to route around.

Related: [[id:337f7fb2-b9e9-4c02-82dd-d88e60df364b][Concurrent Claude Code sessions share one global org-clock with no session identity]]
already solved the narrower "which session owns the running clock"
problem this would otherwise collide with. This entry is scoped to
whether/how to formalize worktrees-for-code as a documented pattern
(e.g. in CLAUDE.md's Emacs integration section) alongside that
existing single-canonical-file constraint — not yet decided as worth
building anything for; may just be a documentation note rather than
code.

*Update 2026-08-05:* the core premise here — "the single clock is the
mechanism that enforces one attention at a time, not a limitation to
route around" — is exactly what's being stress-tested by real
concurrent-session activity found this same day:
[[id:582cc7f4-41a2-4666-ad3f-1b76b459147e][session-resume blindly inherits any session's last-clocked heading]],
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][Direct file writes to a live org buffer can desync org-clock-marker]],
and
[[id:d150c02e-06fe-4514-bf69-e2cc5d8df4c7][org_clock_in doesn't record clock ownership, unlike session-resume]].
Not reopening the conclusion here — just flagging that if/when this
gets planned, it should be read against that fresh evidence, not just
the original 2026-07-30 reasoning.

*The practice arrived without the decision, 2026-09-03 — noted, but not grounds for promotion.* This
body says worktrees-for-code is "not yet decided as worth building anything for; may just be a
documentation note". Since then a worktree has become a normal way to review a PR here: one was used
this session to establish that =bin/pre-commit-test='s failures reproduce at =8c52cd6=, and
=f8c86914= exists precisely because reviewing PR 8 from =.claude/worktrees/pr8-review= made a
convention check report the wrong cause.

Held at =MAYBE= because =f8c86914= already carries the concrete defect and is =TODO=. Promoting this
too would put the same work in the slice twice under two framings — the duplication this project
rejects elsewhere. What is left here is the *documentation* half, and it is worth writing only once
someone decides the pattern is sanctioned rather than merely happening.


* TODO Evaluate choice of shell and standardize all project scripts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       84b7d8b3-5161-4146-9167-0e7e2100e365
:CREATED:  [2026-07-31 Fri 15:23]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-05 Wed 14:06] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
- State "TODO"       from "MAYBE"      [2026-09-03 Thu 16:07] \\
  fish moved from a leaf script into the commit gate
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/script-shell-standardization.md][Plan]]

Surfaced 2026-07-31 while relocating the statusline script
(=bin/statusline.fish=) into the repo: every other script in
=bin/=/=bin/hooks/=/=.claude/hooks/= is bash (mostly no extension,
one =.sh=), but the statusline script is fish — the only fish
dependency anywhere in this project, and one =future users= (per the
README/=CLAUDE.md= discussion) can't assume is installed the way bash
can be. Named =.fish= explicitly specifically so this deviation is
visible from the file listing rather than buried in the shebang, but
that's a stopgap, not a resolution.

*Update 2026-07-31:* option 1 (pure Emacs) below now has a real case
study, not just a hypothetical — see the sibling heading above ("Case
study: apply \"maximum Emacs\" to the statusline script", ID
e202e93d-cfa7-4d0a-8721-8f06428bc1e4). The statusline's =jq=-based
model-name extraction moved into Elisp
(=claude-code-ide-org--statusline-model-name=), and the shell wrapper
became =bin/statusline.sh= — plain POSIX =sh=, no =jq=, no fish — since
once the logic moves out, there's nothing left in the wrapper that
needs a fancier shell. The fish-vs-bash framing above is now
historical for this one script specifically (it's =sh= now, not fish),
but the broader project-wide decision (which of the five options to
standardize on) is still open.

Needs an actual decision, not just noticing the inconsistency:
1. Pure Emacs, given Emacs is already a hard dependency for every
   script in this project (they all end in an =emacsclient -e= call
   regardless of which shell wraps it). Rather than the shell side
   parsing stdin JSON (via =jq= or Python) and interpolating specific
   fields into an elisp expression string, write stdin to a temp file
   and pass *that path* to =emacsclient -e=, letting Elisp read and
   parse the raw JSON itself (native =json-parse-string=, no external
   dependency) and do all the field-extraction, decision logic, *and*
   output formatting — mirroring the temp-file convention this project
   already uses in the opposite direction for output
   (=claude-code-ide-org-write-*-report=). The shell wrapper would
   shrink to an near-unconditional stub: write stdin to a temp file,
   call emacsclient with its path, cat/format whatever comes back.
   Beyond just cutting the jq/Python dependency, this would eliminate
   an entire class of bug this project has already had to work around
   once (see =bin/hooks/session-pause=/=session-resume='s =jq -R -s .=
   JSON-string-encoding, specifically to avoid unsafe shell-to-elisp
   string interpolation) — if the shell layer never interpolates a
   parsed field into an elisp expression at all, that whole hazard
   disappears by construction rather than being carefully avoided each
   time.
2. Python instead of shell for some or all of these. Every hook
   script that parses the Claude Code JSON payload currently shells
   out to =jq=; Python's stdlib =json= module does that natively, no
   external dependency, arguably clearer than =jq='s own query
   language for anyone unfamiliar with it. Tradeoff: gives up the
   one-liner brevity these scripts have today for more verbose (if
   more structured) code, and Python, while extremely common on
   macOS/Linux dev machines, is still one more runtime assumption on
   top of whatever shell gets picked for the scripts that stay
   shell — this wouldn't replace the bash/fish/sh decision below so
   much as sit alongside it (e.g. "simple wrapper: shell; anything
   parsing/branching on JSON: Python").
3. Broader: is bash itself the right universal baseline for "future
   users," or should everything target POSIX =sh= for maximum
   portability instead?
4. Decide fish is fine for this one script specifically (e.g. if it's
   meaningfully clearer/shorter in fish) and document *why* it's the
   one exception, so a future contributor doesn't "fix" it back to
   bash without understanding the tradeoff, or
5. Standardize on bash (matching every other script) and rewrite
   =bin/statusline.fish= to drop the fish dependency entirely.

Whatever's decided should also cover: should this be written down as
a project convention (e.g. in CLAUDE.md's "Repository layout" or a new
"Scripting conventions" note) so it doesn't have to be
re-litigated per script.

*Promoted =MAYBE= → =TODO= 2026-09-03, because the fact this heading rests on has inverted.* The
body above says fish is "the only fish dependency anywhere in this project", carried by one
peripheral statusline script — and the 2026-07-31 update records even that one moving to POSIX =sh=.
Both were true. Neither is now.

=bin/check-conventions= and =bin/check-conventions-test= are fish, and =.githooks/pre-commit= invokes
=check-conventions= on every commit that touches a documentation file. So fish is no longer a
deviation in a leaf script that a future user could ignore — **it is a prerequisite for committing to
this repo at all**, and it arrived without the decision this heading exists to make ever being taken.

That is what changes the merit rather than merely adding to it. A stylistic inconsistency in one
peripheral script is a reasonable =MAYBE=; an undeclared interpreter dependency in the commit gate of
a project whose stated goal is to travel to other machines is not.

Found while fixing =0fa403f4=, whose own first hypothesis was that this dependency explained a red
=bin/pre-commit-test=. It did not — the cause there was stale fixtures — but reproducing that
hypothesis is what surfaced how far the dependency had spread. The five options below are unchanged
and still need the decision they always needed.

No README.md exists in this repo today — CLAUDE.md is currently the
*only* documentation file, doing double duty as both agent
instructions and de facto human-facing intro (confirmed: this repo has
no other .md file at all). Decided against Claude Code's =@README.md=
import syntax inside CLAUDE.md (which would auto-load the full file
into every session's context, same as CLAUDE.md itself) in favor of a
small, deliberate amount of redundancy: a human-facing README.md
(intro/tutorial framing, quickstart, maybe a trimmed install summary)
kept separate from CLAUDE.md, accepting that the small overlap zone
(mainly the intro/description, per CLAUDE.md's own opening lines and
maybe an abbreviated version of its "Emacs integration" section) needs
manual syncing rather than paying the token cost of importing the
whole file every session.

Two parts:
1. Write README.md itself.
2. Add a =PostToolUse= hook (matching =Edit|Write=, filtered to fire
   only when =tool_input.file_path= is =CLAUDE.md= or =README.md=,
   read via =jq= from the hook's stdin JSON — same idiom as
   =bin/hooks/pretooluse-transition-guard=) that injects an
   =additionalContext= reminder to check the sibling file for anything
   that also needs updating. Mirrors the existing =SessionStart=
   stale-interval-recovery hook's pattern (inject context, let Claude
   relay/act on it) rather than hard-blocking the edit, which would be
   heavier-handed than this warrants.

Revisit 2026-07-31.

*Note added 2026-08-05:* consider a "Known limitations" section in the
README, surfacing accepted-but-easy-to-miss gaps that today live only
as prose buried in CLAUDE.md — cited here by content, not line number,
since line numbers drift (confirmed: this note's original citations
were already off by ~10 lines within the same day). Two examples, both
deliberately left as low-cost/self-correcting rather than fixed, from
CLAUDE.md's "State transition rules" section:
- *"Known gap, accepted"*: the =ExitPlanMode= hook fires whether a plan
  was approved or rejected, with no reliable signal to tell the two
  apart, so a rejected plan can still trigger a stray =PLANNING= →
  =DOING= promotion.
- *"Cross-session guard"*: the same promotion only fires for the
  session that set =PLANNING= in the first place, tracked via
  =claude-code-ide-org--planning-owner-session-id=.

A human skimming the repo has no reason to find either today unless
they read that CLAUDE.md section end to end.

*Part 2 is contingent as of 2026-08-14, and part 1 is not.* The
=PostToolUse= consistency hook above justifies itself by mirroring the
=SessionStart= hooks' inject-and-let-Claude-relay pattern. That
precedent's reliability has never been checked — see
[[id:c5b02503-1700-47fd-bf89-bd3296b24af2][c5b02503]], raised the same
day — so building part 2 as specified would copy an unexamined
assumption into a third place. The argument recorded above is sound
about *hard-blocking being too heavy*; it says nothing about whether the
lighter mechanism works.

Writing =README.md= itself is untouched by any of that.

** TODO MCP HTTP server responds 200 instead of 202 Accepted to notifications :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80
:CREATED:  [2026-07-30 Thu 12:21]
:END:
=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--send-empty-response= (the
id-less/notification response path, e.g. for
=notifications/initialized=) replies with HTTP =200= +
=Content-Type: text/plain=, but the MCP Streamable HTTP spec expects
=202 Accepted= for that case. Confirmed live, found while bridging
Warp to this server: Warp's own MCP client fails with "Transport
channel closed" sending =notifications/initialized= against the
unpatched server; the Python =mcp= SDK's client (used by
=mcp-proxy=) instead rejects the same response with "Unexpected
content type: text/plain". Verified fix: changing just the status
code to =202= (confirmed transiently via =emacsclient= before being
persisted as a Doom config override) resolves both — a full
=tools/list= round-trip succeeded afterward. Worked around locally in
this project's Doom config pending an upstream report/PR.


** TODO json-rpc-error is signalled but never define-error'd, so a bad request hangs the connection :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ce012ad4-87f7-435c-a2ee-25210c071865
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:10]
:END:

*Found 2026-09-02 while diagnosing [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]].* This is the upstream half: our three tool declarations were the trigger, but the reason a bad request *hangs* instead of erroring is in =claude-code-ide= itself, and it will bite any tool anyone registers.

=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server.el= signals =json-rpc-error= in three places — =--dispatch= for an unknown method (=-32601=), =--handle-tools-call= for an unknown tool (=-32602=), and =--validate-args= for a missing required argument (=-32602=) — and *never passes the symbol to =define-error=*.

An error symbol with no =error-conditions= property matches no handler. Measured directly:

: (condition-case e (signal 'json-rpc-error (list -32602 "x")) (error "caught"))
: => *ERROR*: peculiar error: -32602, "x"

So the =(error …)= handler in =--handle-post= does not catch it, and neither does the inner =condition-case= in =--handle-tools-call= — which the =--validate-args= call sits outside of anyway, being evaluated in the enclosing =let*=. The signal escapes the whole request handler, =--send-json-response= is never reached, its =(throw 'close-connection nil)= never runs, and the HTTP connection is neither answered nor closed. The client waits until it times out. Emacs stays fully responsive throughout, which is what makes the symptom so hard to place.

*The fix upstream is one form:* =(define-error 'json-rpc-error "JSON-RPC error")=, plus a handler in =--handle-post= that renders it through =--send-json-error= with its own code and message. Every one of the three signal sites already carries the right JSON-RPC code, so they need no change — today those codes are simply never delivered to anyone.

*Worth reporting because the failure is maximally unhelpful.* The three conditions it covers are ordinary client mistakes — unknown method, unknown tool, missing argument — and each is the kind of thing a caller could correct instantly if told. Instead all three present identically as a hang with no diagnostic anywhere.

Sibling of [[id:8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80][8f986c6f]] under this heading: both are the HTTP server answering wrongly rather than the tools misbehaving.


* MAYBE Consider archives in org_clock_report's scope (agenda-with-archives) :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       0465c1d5-a064-4c8f-8082-9b853377ddf6
:CREATED:  [2026-07-29 Wed 12:25]
:END:
Deferred out of the tracked-files fix above: once =DONE.org= (or any
archive file) is ever reachable from
=claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files=, retrospective =org_clock_report=
calls without an =:ID:= should account for archived work's clocked
time too — completed tasks' =:LOGBOOK:= history moves into =DONE.org=
on archive, so excluding it undercounts. Not urgent now: =DONE.org=
isn't symlinked into =~/org= today, so it isn't in scope either way.

Org's clocktable machinery already has a built-in mechanism for
exactly this — the =:scope= parameter accepts ='agenda-with-archives=
(or ='file-with-archives=), which follows each agenda file's
=#+ARCHIVE:=/=org-archive-location= automatically. Prefer that over
symlinking =DONE.org= alongside =TODO.org= in =~/org= if/when this
becomes worth building — it keeps the interactive prospective agenda
and the retrospective clock-report scope independently correct
instead of coupled to the same file list.


* MAYBE Coarsen :SESSIONS: to session-level, not turn-level :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       c6b598ed-9e92-4a3a-99fb-9c5194d290e4
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 11:06]
:END:
:SESSIONS: currently inherits the same Stop/UserPromptSubmit hooks as
:LOGBOOK:, so every turn boundary writes a Paused/Resumed pair — fine
for CLOCK, whose "many short intervals instead of one long one"
granularity is intentional (see the dual-timekeeping design note), but
not what :SESSIONS: was meant to show: the *session-level* bracketing
arc, not per-prompt noise. Wanted instead: a genuine close only at
DOING → DONE with the work tested and committed, at the end of a
working day, or on an explicit signal — sketched as a command like
=/dfd <task>= ("done for the day").

*Open question:* does "end of a working day" need its own detection
(e.g. against =claude-code-ide-org-working-hours=), or is =/dfd=
always a manual call — auto-detecting day boundaries crosses into
guessing user intent, exactly the kind of call CLAUDE.md's existing
rules say only the model/user should make, not a hook.


* MAYBE Idle-based auto clock-out :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       b2ac0353-1e9a-47f7-8add-e0b37994a7af
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
Via =org-clock-idle-time=, already built into org — just needs
enabling. Guards against a clock left running because a session
died mid-DOING without ever reaching org_clock_out.

No config.el/config-test.el changes — there's no wall-clock idle to
simulate in ERT's scratch-buffer fixtures.


* MAYBE Remote MCP access :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       5adeb6c2-afc0-4472-9529-fff48f8ba825
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
Off-machine access to the tools server and IDE-companion. This
session already got a =claude= CLI running directly in a Warp pane
on the same machine to reach Emacs (HTTP tools server +
IDE-companion lockfile, no vterm required). The obvious next step
is the same thing from *off-machine* — phone, another computer, a
CI job — but that means opening those servers beyond localhost,
which is real attack surface: arbitrary elisp execution against
your Emacs, today gated only by "it's on localhost." Don't build
this without designing auth first — bearer token at minimum, ideally
scoped per tool.

*Risk:* every registered tool call is unsandboxed elisp execution in
your live Emacs — a bearer token alone adds no TLS, per-tool
scoping, or rate limiting. Don't just flip the host to 0.0.0.0 and
call it done.


* MAYBE Package the Warp wiring :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       e396f94a-59db-4c6b-80b4-dc144146877b
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
As a reusable snippet, not a private hack. Per the earlier scope
conversation: the port-pinning + session-registration +
IDE-companion-autostart block currently lives in personal
config.el, hand-rolled against claude-code-ide's internals rather
than a public API. Short of a real upstream PR, even just
generalizing it (hardcoded project path → variable) and writing it
up would make it something worth handing to someone else.

*Open question:* the wrapped functions (=register-session=,
=mcp-start=) aren't really designed as public standalone-mode API
today, so an upstream PR is a bigger lift than it looks. Start with
the snippet, treat upstreaming as a separate future MAYBE.


* Review and planning
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Meta
:DATE_TREE: t
:END:
Meta-work: the review pass, planning, and the time that goes into deciding what to do rather than doing it. Time is clocked against the *day node*, which is why the day node carries an =:ID:= and the year and month nodes above it do not -- they are org-datetree's own scaffolding, and =bin/lint-org= knows the difference ([[id:e30d52d7-960b-44f3-ab92-93846c81a1bd][e30d52d7]]).
